A note on vertex algebras and Costello-Gwilliam factorization algebras Thanks: This work is supported by JSPS Research Fellowship for Young Scientists (No. 23KJ1120).
Yusuke Nishinaka

Abstract
. We show that the construction of vertex algebras from Costello-Gwilliam factorization algebras on ℂ \mathbb{C} can be achieved without the discreteness condition on the weight spaces. Furthermore, we construct locally constant factorization algebras from commutative vertex algebras, and discuss the relationship between this construction and the jet algebras of commutative algebras.



*[clist]label=(), nosep

0.
Introduction

The notion of factorization algebras, introduced by Costello and Gwilliam, is a mathematical formulation of the structure of observables in a field theory, whether in classical or quantum field theory. In
[
CG17
,
CG21
]
, they developed the theory of factorization algebras and the Batalin-Vilkovisky (BV) formalism for perturbative quantum field theory. One of the main results of
[
CG17
,
CG21
]
is the deformation quantization theorem, which states that the factorization algebra associated with a classical field theory admits a quantization when certain obstruction groups vanish. This quantization theorem is proved using the techniques of perturbative renormalization developed mathematically by Costello
[
C11
]
.

On the other hand, it is known that the notion of vertex algebras, introduced by Borcherds
[
Bo86
]
, is an algebraic framework of the chiral part of two-dimensional conformal field theory. Thus, it is naturally expected that factorization algebras on the complex plane
\mathbb{C}
with suitable conditions recover vertex algebras. In fact, Costello and Gwilliam showed in
[
CG17
, §5.3]
how to construct vertex algebras from prefactorization algebras called
S^{1}
-equivariant holomorphically translation invariant prefactorization algebras. Conversely, it is also expected that the construction of factorization algebras from vertex algebras is possible. Costello and Gwilliam constructed using factorization envelopes, the Kac-Moody factorization algebra and the quantum observables of the
\beta\gamma
system in
[
CG17
, §5.4, §5.5]
, which correspond respectively to the affine vertex algebra and the
\beta\gamma
vertex algebra. Using the same method, Williams
[
W17
]
constructed Virasoro factorization algebra, which corresponds to the Virasoro vertex algebra. For general vertex algebras, Bruegmann
[
B20a
]
constructed factorization algebras from vertex algebras. However, the connection between the factorization algebras constructed by him and those constructed as factorization envelops in
[
CG17
, §5.4, §5.5]
and
[
W17
]
is not established (see the example in the introduction of
[
B20a
]
).

We now remark that there is another notion of factorization algebras introduced by Beilinson and Drinfeld
[
BD04
]
. Beilinson-Drinfeld factorization algebras are essentially the same as chiral algebras, which provide an algebro-geometric formulation of two-dimensional conformal field theory. Furthermore, it is known that the category of translation-equivariant chiral algebras on the affine line
\mathbb{A}^{1}
is equivalent to that of vertex algebras
[
BD04
, §0.15]
(see also
[
BDHK19
, Corollary A.2]
).
In
[
CG17
, §1.4.1]
, Costello and Gwilliam heuristically explain the relationship between the two notions of factorization algebras, but establishing this connection as a rigorous mathematical theorem remains an open problem.

This note began with the motivation to identify the class of factorization algebras that gives a categorical equivalence to vertex algebras. Unfortunately, this goal has not yet been achieved and appears to be very challenging. However, we have uncovered several new aspects of the relationship between vertex algebras and factorization algebras, which are outlined below.

The first result is about the construction of vertex algebras from prefactorization algebras with values in the category
\mathsf{LCS}_{\mathbb{C}}
of locally convex spaces:

Theorem 1

(
Theorem

3.2.11
,
Proposition

3.3.2
)
.

If
S^{1}\ltimes\mathbb{C}
-equivariant prefactorization algebra
\mathcal{F}
on the complex number plane
\mathbb{C}
with values in
\mathsf{LCS}_{\mathbb{C}}
is holomorphic (
Definition

3.2.7
), then the linear space

\mathbf{V}(\mathcal{F})\coloneqq\bigoplus_{\Delta\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathcal{F}^{0}_{\Delta}

has the structure of a
\mathbb{Z}
-graded vertex algebra induced by the prefactorization algebra structure and the
S^{1}\ltimes\mathbb{C}
-equivariant structure of
\mathcal{F}
. Here
\mathcal{F}^{0}
denotes the costalk at
0\in\mathbb{C}
, and
\mathcal{F}^{0}_{\Delta}\subset\mathcal{F}^{0}
denotes the weight space with respect to the rotation action of
S^{1}
. Additionally, if
\mathcal{F}
is locally constant (
Definition

1.5.1
), then the vertex algebra
\mathbf{V}(\mathcal{F})
is commutative.

Our construction is similar to the one proposed by Costello and Gwilliam in
[
CG17
, §5.3]
or the one by Bruegmann in
[
B20a
, §1.3]
, but it offers some advantages:

•

As we mentioned above, Costello and Gwilliam constructed vertex algebras from prefactorization algebras called
S^{1}
-equivariant holomorphically translation invariant prefactorization algebras. These prefactorization algebras take values in the category of cochain complexes of differentiable vector spaces (see
[
CG17
, §3.5.1]
for the definition of differentiable vector spaces). A notable feature of the category of differentiable vector spaces is that it is an abelian category containing many classes of topological vector spaces as subcategories. This makes it much easier to perform homological algebra since, as is well-known, the category of topological vector spaces is not abelian. However, as shown in
[
CG17
, Theorem 5.3.3]
, the vertex algebra structure emerges at the level of cohomology, so it is not essential for prefactorization algebras to take values in cochain complex in order to produce vertex algebras. Since the notion of differentiable vector spaces is relatively unfamiliar, especially to algebraists, it seems better to adopt
\mathsf{LCS}_{\mathbb{C}}
as the target category of prefactorization algebras.

•

Bruegmann also constructed vertex algebras from prefactorization algebras in
[
B20a
, §1.3]
. He adopted the category of bornological vector spaces as the target for prefactorization algebras. However, what he actually constructed from prefactorization algebras were geometric vertex algebras, and he also proved in
[
B20b
]
the categorical equivalence between geometric vertex algebras and vertex algebras, which is a simplified version of Huang’s result in
[
H97
]
. Consequently, his construction is divided into two parts, making the direct connection between vertex algebras and prefactorization algebras unclear. In contrast, we construct vertex algebras directly.

•

In both the constructions by Costello and Gwilliam, and by Bruegmann, a discreteness condition is imposed on prefactorization algebras (see condition (iii) in
[
CG17
, Theorem 5.3.3]
and the first paragraph of
[
B20a
, §1.3]
). We show that this discreteness condition is unnecessary, as expected by Bruegmann, using the complex analysis of functions with values in a locally convex space.

The second result concerns the inverse construction. We construct locally constant factorization algebras from commutative vertex algebras:

Theorem 2

(
Proposition

3.4.6
,
Proposition

3.4.9
)
.

For a commutative
\mathbb{Z}
-graded vertex algebra

V=\bigoplus_{\Delta\in\mathbb{Z}}V_{\Delta}

such that
V_{\Delta}=0
(
\Delta\ll 0
) and
\dim_{\mathbb{C}}V_{\Delta}<\infty
(
\Delta\in\mathbb{Z}
), one can construct a locally constant holomorphic factorization algebra
\mathbf{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{\overline{V}}
, which satisfies

\mathbf{V}(\mathbf{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{\overline{V}})\cong V

as
\mathbb{Z}
-graded vertex algebras.

The construction of
\mathbf{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{\overline{V}}
is briefly as follows: In
§​

2.2
, we construct a locally constant factorization algebra
\mathbf{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{A}
on
\mathbb{C}
with values in the category
\mathsf{Mod}\,\mathbb{C}
of linear spaces from a commutative algebra
A
. The condition
V_{\Delta}=0
for
\Delta\ll 0
guarantees that
\overline{V}\coloneqq\prod_{\Delta\in\mathbb{Z}}V_{\Delta}
has the canonical structure of a commutative algebra induced from that of
V
. Thus, we obtain a locally constant factorization algebra
\mathbf{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{\overline{V}}
with values in
\mathsf{Mod}\,\mathbb{C}
. Additionally, by using the conformal weight and the translation operator of
V
, we can define an
S^{1}\ltimes\mathbb{C}
-equivariant structure on
\mathbf{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{\overline{V}}
. Imposing the condition
\dim_{\mathbb{C}}V_{\Delta}<\infty
for
\Delta\in\mathbb{Z}
, the canonical topology of
V_{\Delta}
ensures that
\mathbf{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{\overline{V}}
becomes a factorization algebra with values in
\mathsf{LCS}_{\mathbb{C}}
. Finally, it can be verified that
\mathbf{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{\overline{V}}
is holomorphic.

As mentioned above, Bruegmann constructed factorization algebras from vertex algebras in
[
B20a
, §1.4]
. The relationship between our construction and his has not been established yet. Furthermore, unfortunately, our construction cannot yet be extended to general vertex algebras. However, our approach leads to the following result about jet construction of differential algebras:

Theorem 3

(
Proposition

3.4.11
)
.

Let
A
be a finitely generated commutative algebra, and denote by
\mathcal{J}\!A
the (infinite) jet algebra of
A
. For a commutative
\mathbb{Z}
-graded vertex algebras
V=\bigoplus_{\Delta\in\mathbb{Z}}V_{\Delta}
satisfying the conditions in
Theorem

2
and a morphism
\varphi\colon\mathbf{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{A}\to\mathbf{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{V}
of factorization algebras, there exists a unique morphism
\widetilde{\varphi}\colon\mathbf{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{\overline{\mathcal{J}\!A}}\to\mathbf{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{\overline{V}}
of
S^{1}\ltimes\mathbb{C}
-equivariant factorization algebras which commutes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{\overline{\mathcal{J}\!A}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{V}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{\overline{V}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\varphi}}

Here horizontal arrows denote the morphisms of factorization algebras induced from the canonical injections
A\hookrightarrow\mathcal{J}\!A\hookrightarrow\overline{\mathcal{J}\!A}
and
V\hookrightarrow\overline{V}
.

The jet construction plays an important role in the geometric study of vertex algebras: It is known that every vertex algebra is canonically filtered
[
L05
]
, and the spectrum of the associated graded algebra is called the singular support. The singular support is a vertex Poisson scheme, which can be viewed as a chiral analogue of Poisson schemes. Since vertex algebras are quite complicated, it is often hoped that the problems can be reduced to the study of geometric objects associated with a vertex algebra, such as the singular support. Research from this perspective has been growing; we mention here only
[
A15
,
AM21
,
A18
]
. Jet schemes of affine Poisson schemes are a typical example of vertex Poisson schemes
[
A12
]
. Therefore, it might be expected that
Theorem

3
offers new insights into such geometric studies of vertex algebras.

Organization
.

§​

1
is an exposition of the notion of factorization algebras. Sections
§​

1.1
to
§​

1.3
cover preliminaries on precosheaves, factorization algebras, and equivariant factorization algebras, respectively. In
§​

1.4
, we construct prefactorization algebras from those defined on a given open basis. This extension construction from an open basis generalizes the idea presented in the example of
[
CG17
, §3.1.1]
, where the construction of factorization algebras on
\mathbb{R}
from associative algebras over
\mathbb{R}
is discussed. We note that our setting of construction differs slightly from that in
[
CG17
, §7.2]
(see
Remark

1.4.2
). In
§​

1.5
, we recall the definition of locally constant factorization algebras on
\mathbb{C}
, and discuss the basic properties of this class of factorization algebras. Although these properties may be well-known to experts, we could not find them in the literature, including
[
CG17
,
CG21
]
.

§​

2
provides the categorical equivalence between locally constant factorization algebras on
\mathbb{C}
with values in
\mathsf{Mod}\,\mathbb{C}
and commutative algebras over
\mathbb{C}
. Although this is a special case of the equivalence of
(\infty,1)
-categories between locally constant factorization algebras on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
E_{n}
-algebras stated in
[
CG17
, Theorem 6.4.2]
, we give an explicit proof since a comprehensive proof for the general case is not available. In
§​

2.1
, we construct commutative algebras from locally constant prefactorization algebras. Conversely, in
§​

2.2
, we construct locally constant factorization algebras from commutative algebras using the extension from an open basis discussed in
§​

1.4
. Finally,
§​

2.3
gives a proof of the categorical equivalence.

§​

3
is the main part of this note.
§​

3.1
provides a preliminary on vertex algebras, including the jet construction of differential algebras. In
§​

3.2
, we construct vertex algebras from prefactorization algebras on
\mathbb{C}
with values in
\mathsf{LCS}_{\mathbb{C}}
, called holomorphic. As mentioned above, our construction does not require prefactorization algebras to be discrete and is not divided into two parts via geometric vertex algebras. In
§​

3.3
, we show that if holomorphic prefactorization algebras are assumed to be locally constant, then the resulting vertex algebras are commutative.
§​

3.4
gives a partial inverse construction, namely, the construction of locally constant holomorphic factorization algebras from commutative vertex algebras. As explained briefly above, this construction is achieved by modifying the method in
§​

2.2
. Furthermore, in this
§​

3.4
, we discuss the relationship between our construction and jet algebras of commutative algebras.

Appendix

A
develops the calculus of functions with values in a locally convex space and is structured to prove the Laurent series expansion (
Theorem

A.5.4
). This appendix is used to remove the discreteness condition imposed on prefactorization algebras by Costello-Gwilliam and Bruegmann. While it is well-known that complex analysis holds for functions with values in a Banach space, we could not find literature that extends this to the setting of locally convex spaces. Although this generalization is a straightforward exercise, we give detailed proofs to ensure the completeness of this note and to make the content more accessible to algebraists familiar with vertex algebras.

Notations
.

Here we list the notations used throughout in this note. Some of them are also introduced in each section.

•

The symbol
\varnothing
denotes the empty set.

•

The symbol
\mathbb{N}
denotes the set
\{0,1,2,\ldots\}
of non-negative integers.

•

The symbol
\delta_{m,n}
denotes the Kronecker delta.

•

For a positive integer
m\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
, we denote
[m]\coloneqq\{1,\ldots,m\}
.

•

For sets
E_{1},\ldots,E_{n}
, we denote
E_{1}\sqcup\cdots\sqcup E_{n}\coloneqq E_{1}\cup\cdots\cup E_{n}
if
E_{i}\cap E_{j}=\varnothing
for all
i\neq j
.

•

For a topological space
X
, we denote by
\mathfrak{U}_{X}
the set of all open subsets of
X
, and by
\mathfrak{U}_{X}(x)
the set of all open neighborhood of
x\in X
.

•

For a topological space
X
, an an open basis
\mathfrak{B}
of
X
and an open subset
U\subset X
, we denote

\mathfrak{B}(U)\coloneqq\{L\in\mathfrak{B}\mid L\subset U\}.

•

For
R\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
and
z\in\mathbb{C}
, we denote by
D_{R}(z)\coloneqq\{\zeta\in\mathbb{C}\mid|\zeta-z|<R\}
the open disk, and by
D_{R}^{\times}(z)\coloneqq D_{R}(z)\mathbin{\setminus}\{z\}
the punctured disk. Also, by convention we denote
D_{\infty}(z)\coloneqq\mathbb{C}
for all
z\in\mathbb{C}
.

•

For
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
and a subset
E\subset\mathbb{C}
, we denote

\Conf_{n}(E)\coloneqq\{(z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})\in E^{n}\mid z_{i}\neq z_{j}\ (i,j\in[n],i\neq j)\}.

•

We denote by
\mathsf{Mod}\,\mathbb{C}
the category of linear spaces over
\mathbb{C}
.

•

We denote by
\mathsf{LCS}_{\mathbb{C}}
the category of locally convex spaces over
\mathbb{C}
, whose morphisms are continuous linear maps.

•

A commutative
\mathbb{C}
-algebra always means a unital and associative one, and a morphism of commutative
\mathbb{C}
-algebras always means a
\mathbb{C}
-linear map which preserves the multiplications and the units. We denote by
\mathsf{CAlg}_{\mathbb{C}}
the category of commutative
\mathbb{C}
-algebras.

1.
Factorization algebras

This section is an exposition of the notions of precosheaves, prefactorization algebras, and equivariant prefactorization algebras, which is minimum for our usage in this note.

1.1.
Precosheaves

In this subsection we recall the notion of precosheaves and costalks.
For a topological space
X
, we denote by
\mathfrak{U}_{X}
the set of all open subsets of
X
.

Definition 1.1.1
.

Let
X
be a topological space, and
\mathsf{A}
a category. A map
\mathcal{F}\colon\mathfrak{U}_{X}\to\Ob\mathsf{A}
together with a family of morphisms

\mathcal{F}^{U}_{V}\colon\mathcal{F}(U)\to\mathcal{F}(V)\quad(U,V\in\mathfrak{U}_{X},U\subset V)

of
\mathsf{A}
is called a
precosheaf
on
X
with valued in
\mathsf{A}
if the following conditions hold:

1

For open subsets
U,V,W\subset X
such that
U\subset V\subset W
, we have
\mathcal{F}^{V}_{W}\circ\mathcal{F}^{U}_{V}=\mathcal{F}^{U}_{W}
.

2

For an open subset
U\subset X
, we have
\mathcal{F}^{U}_{U}=\id_{\mathcal{F}(U)}
.

Let
X
be a topological space and
\mathsf{A}
a category. The set
\mathfrak{U}_{X}
is an ordered set with respect to inclusions, so it can be viewed as a category. A precosheaf
\mathcal{F}
is nothing but a functor
\mathcal{F}\colon\mathfrak{U}_{X}\to\mathsf{A}
.

Definition 1.1.2
.

Let
\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}\colon\mathfrak{U}_{X}\to\mathsf{A}
be precosheaves and

\varphi_{U}\colon\mathcal{F}(U)\to\mathcal{G}(U)

be a morphism of
\mathsf{A}
for each open subset
U\subset X
. A family
\varphi=\{\varphi_{U}\}_{U\in\mathfrak{U}_{X}}
is called a
morphism of precosheaves
if

\mathcal{G}^{U}_{V}\circ\varphi_{U}=\varphi_{V}\circ\mathcal{F}^{U}_{V}

for all open subsets
U\subset V\subset X
.
In other words, a morphism
\varphi\colon\mathcal{F}\to\mathcal{G}
of precosheves is a natural transformation from the functor
\mathcal{F}
to
\mathcal{G}
.

We denote by
\mathsf{PCSh}(X,\mathsf{A})
the category of precosheaves
\mathcal{F}\colon\mathfrak{U}_{X}\to\mathsf{A}
.

Now, fix a topological space
X
and a category
\mathsf{A}
which admits projective limits.
For
x\in X
and a precosheaf
\mathcal{F}\colon\mathfrak{U}_{X}\to\mathsf{A}
, notice that

(\{\mathcal{F}(U)\}_{U\in\mathfrak{U}_{X}(x)},\{\mathcal{F}^{U}_{V}\}_{V\supset U})

is a projective system in
\mathsf{A}
. Here
\mathfrak{U}_{X}(x)
denotes the set of all open neighborhood of
x
, and is ordered by reverse inclusion.

Definition 1.1.3
.

For
x\in X
and a precosheaf
\mathcal{F}\colon\mathfrak{U}_{X}\to\mathsf{A}
, let

\mathcal{F}^{x}\coloneqq\varprojlim_{U\in\mathfrak{U}_{X}(x)}\mathcal{F}(U),

and denote by

\mathcal{F}^{x}_{U}\colon\mathcal{F}^{x}\to\mathcal{F}(U)

the canonical projection for each
U\in\mathfrak{U}_{X}(x)
. The object
\mathcal{F}^{x}
is called the
costalk
of
\mathcal{F}
at
x\in X
.

Let
x\in X
, and
\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}\colon\mathfrak{U}_{X}\to\mathsf{A}
be precosheaves. For a morphism
\varphi\colon\mathcal{F}\to\mathcal{G}
of precosheaves, there exists a unique morphism
\varphi^{x}\colon\mathcal{F}^{x}\to\mathcal{G}^{x}
such that

\mathcal{G}^{x}_{U}\circ\varphi^{x}=\varphi_{U}\circ\mathcal{F}^{x}_{U}

for all
U\in\mathfrak{U}_{X}(x)
.
Thus, the map
\mathcal{F}\mapsto\mathcal{F}^{x}
gives a functor

(-)^{x}\colon\mathsf{PCSh}(X,\mathsf{A})\to\mathsf{A}.

1.2.
Prefactorization algebras

In this subsection we cite from
[
CG17
]
the notion of prefactorization algebras.
Let
X
be a topological space, and
\mathsf{M}
a symmetric monoidal category. We use the following notations:

•

For subsets
U_{1},\ldots,U_{n}\subset X
, we denote
U_{1}\sqcup\cdots\sqcup U_{n}\coloneqq U_{1}\cup\cdots\cup U_{n}
if
U_{i}\cap U_{j}=\varnothing
for all
i\neq j
.

•

We denote by
\otimes
the tensor product of
\mathsf{M}
.

•

We denote by
1_{\mathsf{M}}
the unit object of
\mathsf{M}
.

The following definition of prefactorization algebras is the same as
[
B20a
, Definition 1.2.1]
, and it appears different from that in
[
CG17
]
. However, they are essentially the same. See
Remark

1.2.2
after the definition.

Definition 1.2.1

(
[
CG17
, §3.1.1, §3.1.2]
)
.

Consider:

•

a precosheaf
\mathcal{F}\colon\mathfrak{U}_{X}\to\mathsf{M}
,

•

a family of morphisms in
\mathsf{M}

\mathcal{F}^{U,V}_{W}\colon\mathcal{F}(U)\otimes\mathcal{F}(V)\to\mathcal{F}(W)\quad(U,V,W\in\mathfrak{U}_{X},U\sqcup V\subset W),

called the multiplication,

•

a morphism
\eta\colon 1_{\mathsf{M}}\to\mathcal{F}(\varnothing)
, called the unit.

A tuple
(\mathcal{F},\{\mathcal{F}^{U,V}_{W}\},\eta)
is called a
prefactorization algebra
on
X
with valued in
\mathsf{M}
if it satisfies the following conditions:

1

For open subsets
U_{1},U_{2},V,W\subset X
such that
U_{1}\sqcup U_{2}\subset V\subset W
, we have

\mathcal{F}^{V}_{W}\circ\mathcal{F}^{U_{1},U_{2}}_{V}=\mathcal{F}^{U_{1},U_{2}}_{W}.

Also, for open subsets
U_{1},U_{2},V_{1},V_{2},W\subset X
such that

U_{1}\subset V_{1},\quad U_{2}\subset V_{2},\quad V_{1}\sqcup V_{2}\subset W,

we have

\mathcal{F}^{V_{1},V_{2}}_{W}\circ(\mathcal{F}^{U_{1}}_{V_{1}}\otimes\mathcal{F}^{U_{2}}_{V_{2}})=\mathcal{F}^{U_{1},U_{2}}_{W}.

2

For open subsets
U_{1},U_{2},V\subset X
such that
U_{1}\sqcup U_{2}\subset V
, the following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}(U_{1})\otimes\mathcal{F}(U_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}(U_{2})\otimes\mathcal{F}(U_{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}(V)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}^{U_{1},U_{2}}_{V}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}^{U_{2},U_{1}}_{V}}

Here
\mathcal{F}(U_{1})\otimes\mathcal{F}(U_{2})\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathcal{F}(U_{2})\otimes\mathcal{F}(U_{1})
denotes the brading of
\mathsf{M}
.

3

(Associativity)
For open subsets
U_{1},U_{2},U_{3},V_{1},V_{2},W\subset X
such that

U_{1}\sqcup U_{2}\subset V_{1},\quad U_{2}\sqcup U_{3}\subset V_{2},\quad U_{1}\sqcup V_{2}\subset W,\quad U_{3}\sqcup V_{1}\subset W,

the following diagram commutes:
(\mathcal{F}(U_{1})\otimes\mathcal{F}(U_{2}))\otimes\mathcal{F}(U_{3})
\mathcal{F}(U_{1})\otimes(\mathcal{F}(U_{2})\otimes\mathcal{F}(U_{3}))
\mathcal{F}(V_{1})\otimes\mathcal{F}(U_{3})
\mathcal{F}(U_{1})\otimes\mathcal{F}(V_{2})
\mathcal{F}(W)
\sim
\scriptstyle\mathcal{F}^{U_{1},U_{2}}_{V_{1}}\otimes\id_{\mathcal{F}(U_{3})}
\scriptstyle\id_{\mathcal{F}(U_{1})}\otimes\mathcal{F}^{U_{2},U_{3}}_{V_{2}}
\scriptstyle\mathcal{F}^{V_{1},U_{3}}_{W}
\scriptstyle\mathcal{F}^{U_{1},V_{2}}_{W}

Here
(\mathcal{F}(U_{1})\otimes\mathcal{F}(U_{2}))\otimes\mathcal{F}(U_{3})\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathcal{F}(U_{1})\otimes(\mathcal{F}(U_{2})\otimes\mathcal{F}(U_{3}))
denotes the associator of
\mathsf{M}
.

4

(Unit)
For an open subset
U\subset X
, the following diagrams commute:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1_{\mathsf{A}}\otimes\mathcal{F}(U)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}(\varnothing)\otimes\mathcal{F}(U)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}(U)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta\otimes\id_{\mathcal{F}(U)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}^{\varnothing,U}_{U}}

Here
1_{\mathsf{M}}\otimes\mathcal{F}(U)\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathcal{F}(U)
denotes the left unitor of
\mathsf{M}
.

Remark 1.2.2
.

Let
(\mathcal{F},\{m^{U_{1},\ldots,U_{n}}_{V}\},\eta)
be an unital prefactorization algebra in the sense of
[
CG17
, §3.1.1, §3.1.2]
, i.e.,
(\mathcal{F},\{m^{U_{1},\ldots,U_{n}}_{V}\},\eta)
is a data consisting of

•

a map
\mathcal{F}\colon\mathfrak{U}_{X}\to\Ob\mathsf{M}
,

•

a morphism

m^{U_{1},\ldots,U_{n}}_{V}\colon\bigotimes_{i=1}^{n}\mathcal{F}(U_{i})\to\mathcal{F}(V)

for open subsets
U_{1},\ldots,U_{n},V\subset X
such that
U_{1}\sqcup\cdots\sqcup U_{n}\subset V
,

•

a morphism
\eta\colon 1_{\mathsf{M}}\to\mathcal{F}(\varnothing)
,

which satisfies the certain compatibility. Then
\mathcal{F}
is a precosheaf by
\mathcal{F}^{U}_{V}\coloneqq m^{U}_{V}
, and the precosheaf
\mathcal{F}\colon\mathfrak{U}_{X}\to\mathsf{M}
is a prefactorization algebra in the sense of
Definition

1.2.1
by the multiplication
\mathcal{F}^{U,V}_{W}\coloneqq m^{U,V}_{W}
and the unit
\eta
.
Conversely, let
(\mathcal{F},\{\mathcal{F}^{U,V}_{W}\},\eta)
be a prefactorization algebra in the sense of
Definition

1.2.1
. For
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
n\geq 2
and open subsets
U_{1},\ldots,U_{n},V\subset X
such that
U_{1}\sqcup\cdots\sqcup U_{n}\subset V
, define a morphism

\mathcal{F}^{U_{1},\ldots,U_{n}}_{V}\colon\bigotimes_{i=1}^{n}\mathcal{F}(U_{i})\to\mathcal{F}(V)

by inductively

\mathcal{F}^{U_{1},\ldots,U_{n}}_{V}\coloneqq\mathcal{F}^{U_{1}\sqcup\cdots\sqcup U_{n-1},U_{n}}_{V}\circ\bigl(\mathcal{F}^{U_{1},\ldots,U_{n-1}}_{U_{1}\sqcup\cdots\sqcup U_{n-1}}\otimes\id_{\mathcal{F}(U_{n})}\bigr).

Then
(\mathcal{F},\{\mathcal{F}^{U_{1},\ldots,U_{n}}_{V}\},\eta)
is an unital prefactorization algebra in the sense of
[
CG17
, §3.1.1, §3.1.2]
.

Definition 1.2.3

(
[
CG17
, §3.1.4]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}\colon\mathfrak{U}_{X}\to\mathsf{M}
be prefactorization algebras. A morphism
\varphi\colon\mathcal{F}\to\mathcal{G}
of precosheaves is called a
morphism of prefactorization algebras
if the following conditions hold:

1

For open subsets
U,V,W\subset X
such that
U\sqcup V\subset W
, we have

\varphi_{W}\circ\mathcal{F}^{U,V}_{W}=\mathcal{G}^{U,V}_{W}\circ(\varphi_{U}\otimes\varphi_{V}).

2

We have
\varphi_{\varnothing}\circ\eta_{\mathcal{F}}=\eta_{\mathcal{G}}
, where
\eta_{\mathcal{F}}
and
\eta_{\mathcal{G}}
denotes the units.

For a topological space
X
, and a symmetric monoidal category
\mathsf{M}
, we denote by
\mathsf{PFA}(X,\mathsf{M})
the category of prefactorization algebras
\mathcal{F}\colon\mathfrak{U}_{X}\to\mathsf{M}
.

Next, we turn to define the notion of factorization algebras, following
[
CG17
, §6.1]
.

Let
X
be a topological space,
\mathsf{A}
a category which admits coproducts, and
\mathcal{F}\colon\mathfrak{U}_{X}\to\mathsf{A}
a precosheaf.
For an open subset
U\subset X
and an open cover
\{U_{i}\}_{i\in I}
of
U
, consider the morphisms

\displaystyle\iota_{i}\circ\mathcal{F}^{U_{i}\cap U_{j}}_{U_{i}}\colon\mathcal{F}(U_{i}\cap U_{j})\to\bigoplus_{i\in I}\mathcal{F}(U_{i})\quad(i,j\in I),

\displaystyle\iota_{j}\circ\mathcal{F}^{U_{i}\cap U_{j}}_{U_{j}}\colon\mathcal{F}(U_{i}\cap U_{j})\to\bigoplus_{i\in I}\mathcal{F}(U_{i})\quad(i,j\in I),

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{U_{i}}_{U}\colon\mathcal{F}(U_{i})\to\mathcal{F}(U)\quad(i\in I).

Here
\iota_{i}\colon\mathcal{F}(U_{i})\to\bigoplus_{i\in I}\mathcal{F}(U_{i})
denotes the canonical morphism of the coproduct. These morphisms induce the following morphisms in
\mathsf{A}
respectively:

(1.2.1)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&p\colon\bigoplus_{i,j\in I}\mathcal{F}(U_{i}\cap U_{j})\to\bigoplus_{i\in I}\mathcal{F}(U_{i}),\\
&q\colon\bigoplus_{i,j\in I}\mathcal{F}(U_{i}\cap U_{j})\to\bigoplus_{i\in I}\mathcal{F}(U_{i}),\\
&\pi\colon\bigoplus_{i\in I}\mathcal{F}(U_{i})\to\mathcal{F}(U).\end{split}

Remark 1.2.4
.

Suppose that
\mathsf{A}
admits any colimits. A precosheaf
\mathcal{F}
is called a cosheaf if for an open subset
U\subset X
and an open cover
\{U_{i}\}_{i\in I}
of
U
, the diagram (
1.2.2
) below gives a coequalizer. A factorization algebra is a kind of cosheaf but we adopt a different Grothendieck topology.

Definition 1.2.5

(
[
CG17
, Definition 6.1.1]
)
.

Let
X
be a topological space and
U\subset X
an open subset. An open cover
\{U_{i}\}_{i\in I}
of
U
is called a
Weiss cover
if for finitely many points
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\in U
, there exists
i\in I
such that
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\in U_{i}
.

Now, fix a topological space
X
and a symmetric monoidal category
\mathsf{M}
which admits colimits.

Definition 1.2.6

(
[
CG17
, Definition 6.1.3]
)
.

A prefactorization algebra
\mathcal{F}\colon\mathfrak{U}_{X}\to\mathsf{M}
is called a
factorization algebra
if it satisfies the following conditions:

1

For an open subset
U\subset X
and Weiss cover
\{U_{i}\}_{i\in I}
,

(1.2.2)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle\bigoplus_{i,j\in I}\mathcal{F}(U_{i}\cap U_{j})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle\bigoplus_{i\in I}\mathcal{F}(U_{i})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}(U)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}

is a diagram of coequalizer. Here we use the notations defined in (
1.2.1
).

2

For open subsets
U,V\subset X
such that
U\cap V=\varnothing
, the multiplication

\mathcal{F}^{U,V}_{U\sqcup V}\colon\mathcal{F}(U)\otimes\mathcal{F}(V)\to\mathcal{F}(U\sqcup V)

is an isomorphism.

Let
\mathcal{F}\colon\mathfrak{U}_{X}\to\mathsf{M}
be a factorization algebra and
\mathfrak{B}
an open basis of
X
such that for each open subset
U\subset X
, the family

\mathfrak{B}(U)\coloneqq\{L\in\mathfrak{B}\mid L\subset U\}

is a Weiss cover of
U
. For an open subset
U
, notice that

\bigl(\{\mathcal{F}(L)\}_{L\in\mathfrak{B}(U)},\{\mathcal{F}^{L}_{M}\}_{L\subset M}\bigr)

is an inductive system in
\mathsf{M}
, where
\mathfrak{B}(U)
is ordered by inclusion.

Proposition 1.2.7
.

Let
\mathcal{F}\colon\mathfrak{U}_{X}\to\mathsf{M}
be a factorization algebra and
\mathfrak{B}
an open basis of
X
such that for each open subset
U\subset X
, the family
\mathfrak{B}(U)
is a Weiss cover of
U
. For an open subset
U\subset X
, we have

\mathcal{F}(U)=\varinjlim_{L\in\mathfrak{B}(U)}\mathcal{F}(L)

with the canonical morphism
\mathcal{F}^{L}_{U}\colon\mathcal{F}(L)\to\mathcal{F}(U)
.

Proof.

It is clear that
\mathcal{F}^{M}_{U}\circ\mathcal{F}^{L}_{M}=\mathcal{F}^{L}_{U}
for
L,M\in\mathfrak{B}(U)
,
L\subset M
. Let
A\in\Ob\mathsf{M}
, and
\{f_{L}\colon\mathcal{F}(L)\to A\}_{L\in\mathfrak{B}(U)}
be a family of morphisms such that

f_{M}\circ\mathcal{F}^{L}_{M}=f_{M}
for
L,M\in\mathfrak{B}(U)
,
L\subset M
. To construct a morphism
\mathcal{F}(U)\to A
, we use the coequalizer diagram (
1.2.2
). By the universality of the coproduct
\bigoplus_{L\in\mathfrak{B}(U)}\mathcal{F}(L)
, there exists a unique morphism
f\colon\bigoplus_{L\in\mathfrak{B}(U)}\mathcal{F}(L)\to A
which commutes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}(L)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle\bigoplus_{L\in\mathfrak{B}(U)}\mathcal{F}(L)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{L}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{L}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

We claim that
f\circ p=f\circ q
. To prove this, it is enough to show that
f\circ p\circ\iota_{L,M}=f\circ q\circ\iota_{L,M}
for each
L,M\in\mathfrak{B}(U)
, where
\iota_{L,M}\colon\mathcal{F}(L\cap M)\to\bigoplus_{L,M\in\mathfrak{B}(U)}\mathcal{F}(L\cap M)
denotes the canonical inclusion, or equivalently
f_{L}\circ\mathcal{F}^{L\cap M}_{L}=f_{M}\circ\mathcal{F}^{L\cap M}_{M}
. Since we have a coequalizer diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle\bigoplus_{L^{\prime},M^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{B}(L\cap M)}\mathcal{F}(L^{\prime}\cap M^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle\bigoplus_{L^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{B}(L\cap M)}\mathcal{F}(L^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}(L\cap M),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{\prime}}

the equation
f_{L}\circ\mathcal{F}^{L\cap M}_{L}=f_{M}\circ\mathcal{F}^{L\cap M}_{M}
follows from
f_{L}\circ\mathcal{F}^{L\cap M}_{L}\circ\pi^{\prime}=f_{M}\circ\mathcal{F}^{L\cap M}_{M}\circ\pi^{\prime}
, but this is clear since

f_{L}\circ\mathcal{F}^{L\cap M}_{L}\circ\pi^{\prime}\circ\iota_{L^{\prime}}=f_{L^{\prime}}=f_{M}\circ\mathcal{F}^{L\cap M}_{M}\circ\pi^{\prime}\circ\iota_{L^{\prime}}\quad(L^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{B}(L\cap M)).

Thus, there is a unique morphism
\widetilde{f}\colon\mathcal{F}(U)\to A
such that
\widetilde{f}\circ\pi=f
. We find that
\widetilde{f}
is a unique morphism satisfying
f\circ\mathcal{F}^{L}_{U}=f_{L}
for each
L\in\mathfrak{B}(U)
.
∎

1.3.
Equivariant prefactorization algebras

In this subsection we cite from
[
CG17
]
the notion of equivariant prefactorization algebras.
Let
X
be a topological space,
\mathsf{M}
a symmetric monoidal category, and
G
a group acting on
X
. For a precosheaf
\mathcal{F}\colon\mathfrak{U}_{X}\to\mathsf{M}
and
a\in G
, a map

a\mathcal{F}\colon\mathfrak{U}_{X}\to\Ob\mathsf{M},\quad U\mapsto\mathcal{F}(aU)

is a precosheaf by letting

(a\mathcal{F})^{U}_{V}\coloneqq\mathcal{F}^{aU}_{aV}\colon(a\mathcal{F})(U)\to(a\mathcal{F})(V)

for each open subsets
U\subset V\subset X
.

Definition 1.3.1

(
[
CG17
, Definition 3.7.1]
)
.

A prefactorization algebra
\mathcal{F}\colon\mathfrak{U}_{X}\to\mathsf{M}
equipped with a morphism

\sigma_{a}=\{\sigma_{a,U}\}_{U\in\mathfrak{U}_{X}}\colon\mathcal{F}\to a\mathcal{F}

of precosheaves for each
a\in G
is called a
G
-equivariant prefactorization algebra
if the following conditions hold:

1

For
a,b\in G
and an open subset
U\subset X
, we have
\sigma_{ab,U}=\sigma_{a,bU}\circ\sigma_{b,U}
.

2

For the unit
1_{G}\in G
and an open subset
U\subset X
, we have
\sigma_{1_{G},U}=\id_{\mathcal{F}(U)}
.

3

For
a\in G
and open subsets
U,V,W\subset X
such that
U\sqcup V\subset W
, we have

\sigma_{a,W}\circ\mathcal{F}^{U,V}_{W}=\mathcal{F}^{aU,aV}_{aW}\circ(\sigma_{a,U}\otimes\sigma_{a,V}).

4

For
a\in G
, we have
\sigma_{a,\varnothing}\circ\eta=\eta
, where
\eta\colon 1_{\mathsf{M}}\to\mathcal{F}(\varnothing)
denotes the unit of
\mathcal{F}
.

Definition 1.3.2
.

Let
\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}\colon\mathfrak{U}_{X}\to\mathsf{M}
be
G
-equivariant prefactorization algebras. A morphism
\varphi\colon\mathcal{F}\to\mathcal{G}
of prefactorization algebras is called a
morphism of
G
-equivariant prefactorization algebras
if

\tau_{a,U}\circ\varphi_{U}=\varphi_{aU}\circ\sigma_{a,U}

for each
a\in G
and an open
U\subset X
. Here
\sigma_{a}\colon\mathcal{F}\to a\mathcal{F}
and
\tau_{a}\colon\mathcal{G}\to a\mathcal{G}
denote the
G
-equivariant structure on
\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{G}
respectively.

For a topological space
X
, a symmetric monoidal category
\mathsf{M}
, and a group
G
acting on
X
, we denote by
\mathsf{PFA}_{G}(X,\mathsf{M})
the category of
G
-equivariant prefactorization algebras
\mathcal{F}\colon\mathfrak{U}_{X}\to\mathsf{M}
.

1.4.
Extension from an open basis

In this subsection we discuss the extension construction of prefactorization algebras from those defined on a give open basis. This construction is slightly different from that in
[
CG17
, §7.2]
to use later in
§​

2.2
(see
Remark

1.4.2
below).
Let
X
be a topological space, and
\mathsf{A}
a category which admits inductive limits.
For an open basis
\mathfrak{B}
of
X
and an open subset
U\subset X
, we denote

\mathfrak{B}(U)\coloneqq\{L\in\mathfrak{B}\mid L\subset U\}.

Let
\mathfrak{B}
be an open basis, and
F\colon\mathfrak{B}\to\mathsf{A}
a precosheaf, i.e., a functor from
\mathfrak{B}
to
\mathsf{A}
. For an open subset
U\subset X
,

(\{F(L)\}_{L\in\mathfrak{B}(U)},\{F^{L}_{M}\}_{L\subset M})

is an inductive system in
\mathsf{A}
, where
\mathfrak{B}(U)
is ordered by inclusion. We define

\mathcal{E}_{F}(U)\coloneqq\varinjlim_{L\in\mathfrak{B}(U)}F(L)

and denote by

\widetilde{F}^{L}_{U}\colon F(L)\to\mathcal{E}_{F}(U)

the canonical morphism for each
L\in\mathfrak{B}(U)
. For open subsets
U\subset V\subset X
, the universality of
\mathcal{E}_{F}(U)=\varinjlim_{L\in\mathfrak{B}(U)}F(L)
yields a unique morphism

(\mathcal{E}_{F})^{U}_{V}\colon\mathcal{E}_{F}(U)\to\mathcal{E}_{F}(V)

which satisfies

(\mathcal{E}_{F})^{U}_{V}\circ\widetilde{F}^{L}_{U}=\widetilde{F}^{L}_{V}

for each
L\in\mathfrak{B}(U)
. Hence the map

\mathcal{E}_{F}\colon\mathfrak{U}_{X}\to\Ob\mathsf{A},\quad U\mapsto\mathcal{E}_{F}(U)

is a precosheaf by
(\mathcal{E}_{F})^{U}_{V}
. Notice that
\widetilde{F}^{L}_{L}\colon F(L)\to\mathcal{E}_{F}(L)
is an isomorphism for
L\in\mathfrak{B}
.

Let
F,G\colon\mathfrak{B}\to\mathsf{A}
be precosheaves, and
\varphi\colon F\to G
be a morphism of precosheaves, i.e., a natural transformation from
F
to
G
. For an open subset
U\subset X
, the universality of
\mathcal{E}_{F}(U)
induces a unique morphism

\mathcal{E}_{\varphi,U}\colon\mathcal{E}_{F}(U)\to\mathcal{E}_{G}(U),

which satisfies

\mathcal{E}_{\varphi,U}\circ\widetilde{F}^{L}_{U}=\widetilde{G}^{L}_{U}\circ\varphi_{L}

for all
L\in\mathfrak{B}(U)
. We find that
\mathcal{E}_{\varphi}\colon\mathcal{E}_{F}\to\mathcal{E}_{G}
is a morphism of precosheaves. Thus, the map
F\mapsto\mathcal{E}_{F}
gives a functor

\mathcal{E}\colon\mathsf{PCSh}(\mathfrak{B},\mathsf{A})\to\mathsf{PCSh}(X,\mathsf{A}),

where
\mathsf{PCSh}(\mathfrak{B},\mathsf{A})
denotes the category of precosheaves on
\mathfrak{B}
.

Now, fix

•

a symmetric monoidal category
\mathsf{M}
such that inductive limits exists, and any inductive limits commute with the tensor product,

•

an open basis
\mathfrak{B}
satisfying:

1

\varnothing\in\mathfrak{B}
.

2

For
L,M\in\mathfrak{B}
such that
L\cap M=\varnothing
, we have
L\sqcup M\in\mathfrak{B}
.

Let
F\colon\mathfrak{B}\to\mathsf{M}
be a prefactorization algebra.
For open subsets
U,V,W\subset X
such that
U\sqcup V\subset W
, the universality of

\mathcal{E}_{F}(U)\otimes\mathcal{E}_{F}(V)=\varinjlim_{\begin{subarray}{c}L\in\mathfrak{B}(U)\\
M\in\mathfrak{B}(V)\end{subarray}}F(L)\otimes F(M)

yields an unique morphism

(\mathcal{E}_{F})^{U,V}_{W}\colon\mathcal{E}_{F}(U)\otimes\mathcal{E}_{F}(V)\to\mathcal{E}_{F}(W)

which satisfies

(\mathcal{E}_{F})^{U,V}_{W}\circ(\widetilde{F}^{L}_{U}\otimes\widetilde{F}^{M}_{V})=\widetilde{F}^{L\sqcup M}_{W}\circ F^{L,M}_{L\sqcup M}

for all
L\in\mathfrak{B}(U)
and
M\in\mathfrak{B}(V)
. The precosheaf
\mathcal{E}_{F}\colon\mathfrak{U}_{X}\to\mathsf{M}
is a prefactorization algebra by the multiplication
(\mathcal{E}_{F})^{U,V}_{W}
and the unit

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1_{\mathsf{A}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(\varnothing)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{E}_{F}(\varnothing),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{F}^{\varnothing}_{\varnothing}}

where
1_{\mathsf{A}}\to F(\varnothing)
indicates the unit of
F
.

Proposition 1.4.1
.

Let
F\colon\mathfrak{B}\to\mathsf{M}
be a prefactorization algebra such that

F^{L,M}_{L\sqcup M}\colon F(L)\otimes F(M)\to F(L\sqcup M)

is an isomorphism for each
L,M\in\mathfrak{B}
,
L\cap M=\varnothing
. For open subsets
U,V\subset X
such that
U\cap V=\varnothing
, if

\mathfrak{B}(U\sqcup V)=\{L\sqcup M\mid L\in\mathfrak{B}(U),M\in\mathfrak{B}(V)\},

then

(\mathcal{E}_{F})^{U,V}_{U\sqcup V}\colon\mathcal{E}_{F}(U)\otimes\mathcal{E}_{F}(V)\to\mathcal{E}_{F}(U\sqcup V)

is an isomorphism.

Proof.

For
L\in\mathfrak{B}(U)
and
M\in\mathfrak{B}(V)
, denote

\varphi_{L,M}\coloneqq\widetilde{F}^{L\sqcup M}_{U\sqcup V}\circ F^{L,M}_{L\sqcup M}\colon F(L)\otimes F(M)\to\mathcal{E}_{F}(U\sqcup V).

It is enough to prove that
(\mathcal{E}_{F}(U\sqcup V),\{\varphi_{L,M}\}_{L\in\mathfrak{B}(U),M\in\mathfrak{B}(V)})
is the inductive limit of

\bigl(\{F(L)\otimes F(M)\}_{L\in\mathfrak{B}(U),M\in\mathfrak{B}(V)},\{F^{L}_{L^{\prime}}\otimes F^{M}_{M^{\prime}}\}_{L\subset L^{\prime},M\subset M^{\prime}}\bigr).

First, a direct calculation shows that

\varphi_{L^{\prime},M^{\prime}}\circ(F^{L}_{L^{\prime}}\otimes F^{M}_{M^{\prime}})=\varphi_{L,M}\quad(L,L^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{B}(U),M,M^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{B}(V),L\subset L^{\prime},M\subset M^{\prime}).

Let
A\in\Ob\mathsf{M}
, and
\{\alpha_{L,M}\colon F(L)\otimes F(M)\to A\}_{L\in\mathfrak{B}(U),M\in\mathfrak{B}(V)}
be a family of morphisms satisfying

\alpha_{L^{\prime},M^{\prime}}\circ(F^{L}_{L^{\prime}}\otimes F^{M}_{M^{\prime}})=\alpha_{L,M}\quad(L,L^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{B}(U),M,M^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{B}(V),L\subset L^{\prime},M\subset M^{\prime}).

Denote for
L\in\mathfrak{B}(U)
and
M\in\mathfrak{B}(V)
,

\widetilde{\alpha}_{L,M}\coloneqq\alpha_{L,M}\circ(F^{L,M}_{L\sqcup M})^{-1}\colon F(L\sqcup M)\to A,

then we have

\widetilde{\alpha}_{L^{\prime},M^{\prime}}\circ F^{L\sqcup M}_{L^{\prime}\sqcup M^{\prime}}=\widetilde{\alpha}_{L,M}\quad(L,L^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{B}(U),M,M^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{B}(V),L\subset L^{\prime},M\subset M^{\prime}).

Thus, by the assumption for
\mathfrak{B}(U\sqcup V)
, the universality of
\mathcal{E}_{F}(U\sqcup V)
yields an unique morphism

\alpha\colon\mathcal{E}_{F}(U\sqcup V)\to A

such that

\alpha\circ\widetilde{F}^{L\sqcup M}_{U\sqcup V}=\widetilde{\alpha}_{L,M}\quad(L\in\mathfrak{B}(U),M\in\mathfrak{B}(V)).

We find that
\alpha
is an unique morphism satisfying

\alpha\circ\varphi_{L,M}=\alpha_{L,M}\quad(L\in\mathfrak{B}(U),M\in\mathfrak{B}(V)),

and hence the claim follows.
∎

Let
F,G\colon\mathfrak{B}\to\mathsf{M}
be prefactorization algebras, and
\varphi\colon F\to G
be a morphism of prefactorization algebras. Then, we find that
\mathcal{E}_{\varphi}\colon\mathcal{E}_{F}\to\mathcal{E}_{G}
is a morphism of prefactorization algebras. Thus, the functor
\mathcal{E}\colon\mathsf{PCSh}(\mathfrak{B},\mathsf{A})\to\mathsf{PCSh}(X,\mathsf{A})
gives rise to the functor

\mathcal{E}\colon\mathsf{PFA}(\mathfrak{B},\mathsf{M})\to\mathsf{PFA}(X,\mathsf{M}),

where
\mathsf{PFA}(\mathfrak{B},\mathsf{M})
denotes the category of prefactorization algebras on
\mathfrak{B}
.

Remark 1.4.2
.

In
[
CG17
, §7.2]
, Costello and Gwilliam show that the categorical equivalence between factorization algebras on
X
and factorization algebras on a given factorization basis of
X
(
[
CG17
, Proposition 7.2.3]
, see also
[
CG17
, Definition 7.2.1]
). Our construction
\mathcal{E}_{F}
from an open basis
\mathfrak{B}
does not require
\mathfrak{B}
to be a factorization basis since we will construct a locally constant factorization algebra in
§​

2.2
from an open basis which is not closed under finite intersections.

Next, fix a group
G
acting on
X
, and suppose that
aL\in\mathfrak{B}
for all
a\in G
and
L\in\mathfrak{B}
.

Let
F\colon\mathfrak{B}\to\mathsf{M}
be a
G
-equivariant prefactorization algebra. We denote its structure morphism by

\sigma_{a,L}\colon F(L)\to F(aL)\quad(a\in G,L\in\mathfrak{B}).

For
a\in G
and an open subset
U\subset X
, the universality of
\mathcal{E}_{F}(U)=\varinjlim_{L\in\mathfrak{B}(U)}F(L)
yields an unique morphism

\widetilde{\sigma}_{a,U}\colon\mathcal{E}_{F}(U)\to\mathcal{E}_{F}(aU)

which satisfies

\widetilde{\sigma}_{a,U}\circ\widetilde{F}^{L}_{U}=\widetilde{F}^{aL}_{aU}\circ\sigma_{a,L}

for each
L\in\mathfrak{B}(U)
. The prefactorization algebra
\mathcal{E}_{F}\colon\mathfrak{U}_{X}\to\mathsf{M}
is a
G
-equivariant prefactorization algebra by the structure morphism
\widetilde{\sigma}_{a,U}
.

Let
F,F^{\prime}\colon\mathfrak{B}\to\mathsf{M}
be
G
-equivariant prefactorization algebras, and
\varphi\colon F\to F^{\prime}
be a morphism of
G
-equivariant prefactorization algebras.
Then, we find that
\mathcal{E}_{\varphi}\colon\mathcal{E}_{F}\to\mathcal{E}_{F^{\prime}}
is a morphism of
G
-equivariant prefactorization algebras. Thus, the functor
\mathcal{E}\colon\mathsf{PFA}(\mathfrak{B},\mathsf{A})\to\mathsf{PFA}(X,\mathsf{A})
gives rise to the functor

\mathcal{E}\colon\mathsf{PFA}_{G}(\mathfrak{B},\mathsf{M})\to\mathsf{PFA}_{G}(X,\mathsf{M}),

where
\mathsf{PFA}_{G}(\mathfrak{B},\mathsf{M})
denotes the category of
G
-equivariant prefactorization algebras on
\mathfrak{B}
.

1.5.
Locally constant factorization algebras on
\mathbb{C}

In this subsection we recall the notion of locally constant factorization algebras on
\mathbb{C}
, and discuss the basic properties of this class of factorization algebras.
Fix a symmetric monoidal category
\mathsf{M}
which admits any colimits.

Definition 1.5.1

(
[
CG17
, Definition 6.4.1]
)
.

A prefactorization algebra
\mathcal{F}\colon\mathfrak{U}_{\mathbb{C}}\to\mathsf{M}
is called locally constant if for
0<r<R\leq\infty
and
z,w\in\mathbb{C}
such that
D_{r}(z)\subset D_{R}(w)
, the morphism
\mathcal{F}^{D_{r}(z)}_{D_{R}(w)}\colon\mathcal{F}(D_{r}(z))\to\mathcal{F}(D_{R}(w))
is an isomorphism in
\mathsf{M}
.

We denote by
\mathsf{PFA}^{\mathsf{loc}}(\mathbb{C},\mathsf{M})
the full subcategory of
\mathsf{PFA}(\mathbb{C},\mathsf{M})
whose objects are locally constant prefactorization algebras. Also, denote by
\mathsf{FA}^{\mathsf{loc}}(\mathbb{C},\mathsf{M})
the full subcategory of
\mathsf{PFA}^{\mathsf{loc}}(\mathbb{C},\mathsf{M})
consisting of locally constant factorization algebras.

Lemma 1.5.2
.

A factorization algebra
\mathcal{F}\colon\mathfrak{U}_{\mathbb{C}}\to\mathsf{M}
is locally constant if and only if the following condition holds: For
0<r<R<\infty
and
z,w\in\mathbb{C}
such that
D_{r}(z)\subset D_{R}(w)
, the morphism
\mathcal{F}^{D_{r}(z)}_{D_{R}(w)}\colon\mathcal{F}(D_{r}(z))\to\mathcal{F}(D_{R}(w))
is an isomorphism.

Proof.

It is trivial that the condition is necessary. To prove that the condition is sufficient, it is enough to show that
\mathcal{F}^{D_{R}(z)}_{\mathbb{C}}
is an isomorphism for
R\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
and
z\in\mathbb{C}
. Since
\{D_{r}(z)\}_{r\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}}
is a Weiss cover of
\mathbb{C}
, we have a coequalizer diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle\bigoplus_{r,s\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}}\mathcal{F}(D_{r}(z)\cap D_{s}(z))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle\bigoplus_{r\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}}\mathcal{F}(D_{r}(z))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}(\mathbb{C}),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}

where the morphism
p,q,\pi
are defined as in (
1.2.1
). For
r\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
, define a morphism
f_{r}\colon\mathcal{F}(D_{r}(z))\to\mathcal{F}(D_{R}(z))
by

f_{r}\coloneqq\begin{cases}\mathcal{F}^{D_{r}(z)}_{D_{R}(z)}&(r\leq R)\\
(\mathcal{F}^{D_{R}(z)}_{D_{r}(z)})^{-1}&(r>R),\end{cases}

then, the family
\{f_{r}\}_{r\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}}
induces the morphism
f\colon\bigoplus_{r\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}}\mathcal{F}(D_{r}(z))\to\mathcal{F}(D_{R}(z))
. Since one can check
f\circ p=f\circ q
, the universality of the coequalizer
\mathcal{F}(\mathbb{C})
yields a unique morphism
\widetilde{f}\colon\mathcal{F}(\mathbb{C})\to\mathcal{F}(D_{R}(z))
such that
\widetilde{f}\circ\pi=f
. We find that
\widetilde{f}
is the inverse of
\mathcal{F}^{D_{R}(z)}_{\mathbb{C}}
.
∎

We denote

(1.5.1)

\mathfrak{B}\coloneqq\{D_{r_{1}}(z_{1})\sqcup\cdots\sqcup D_{r_{l}}(z_{l})\mid l\in\mathbb{N},r_{i}\in\mathbb{R}_{>0},z_{i}\in\mathbb{C},\,D_{r_{i}}(z_{i})\cap D_{r_{j}}(z_{j})=\varnothing\ (i\neq j)\},

then
\mathfrak{B}
is an open basis of
\mathbb{C}
such that for each open subset
U\subset\mathbb{C}
, the family
\mathfrak{B}(U)
is a Weiss cover of
U
.

Lemma 1.5.3
.

Let
\mathcal{F}\colon\mathfrak{U}_{\mathbb{C}}\to\mathsf{M}
be a locally constant prefactorization algebra, and fix
l\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
. For a domain
U\subset\mathbb{C}
and

L=D_{r_{1}}(z_{1})\sqcup\cdots\sqcup D_{r_{l}}(z_{l}),\quad M=D_{s_{1}}(w_{1})\sqcup\cdots\sqcup D_{s_{l}}(w_{l})\ \in\mathfrak{B}(U),

we have

\mathcal{F}^{\{D_{r_{i}}(z_{i})\}_{i=1}^{l}}_{U}\circ(\otimes_{i=1}^{l}(\mathcal{F}^{D_{r_{i}}(z_{i})}_{\mathbb{C}})^{-1})=\mathcal{F}^{\{D_{s_{i}}(w_{i})\}_{i=1}^{l}}_{U}\circ(\otimes_{i=1}^{l}(\mathcal{F}^{D_{s_{i}}(w_{i})}_{\mathbb{C}})^{-1}).

Proof.

For
L=D_{r_{1}}(z_{1})\sqcup\cdots\sqcup D_{r_{l}}(z_{l})\in\mathfrak{B}(U)
, denote by
f_{L}
the composition of morphisms

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}(\mathbb{C})^{\otimes l}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigotimes_{i=1}^{l}\mathcal{F}(D_{r_{i}}(z_{i}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}(U).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\otimes_{i=1}^{l}(\mathcal{F}^{D_{r_{i}}(z_{i})}_{\mathbb{C}})^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}^{\{D_{r_{i}}(z_{i})\}_{i=1}^{l}}_{U}}

Fix
L=D_{r_{1}}(z_{1})\sqcup\cdots\sqcup D_{r_{l}}(z_{l})\in\mathfrak{B}(U)
, and set

E\coloneqq\{(w_{1},\ldots,w_{l})\in\Conf_{l}(U)\mid\forall s_{i}\in\mathbb{R}_{>0},\,M=D_{s_{1}}(w_{1})\sqcup\cdots\sqcup D_{s_{l}}(w_{l})\in\mathfrak{B}(U)\Longrightarrow f_{L}=f_{M}\}.

Then, for
(w_{1},\ldots,w_{l})\in\Conf_{l}(U)
, we have

(w_{1},\ldots,w_{l})\in E\Longleftrightarrow\exists s_{i}\in\mathbb{R}_{>0},\,M=D_{s_{1}}(w_{1})\sqcup\cdots\sqcup D_{s_{l}}(w_{l})\in\mathfrak{B}(U)\land f_{L}=f_{M}.

Hence we find that
(z_{1},\ldots,z_{l})\in E
, and that both
E
and
\Conf_{l}(U)\mathbin{\setminus}E
are open subsets of
\mathbb{C}^{l}
. Thus
\Conf_{l}(U)=E
since
\Conf_{l}(U)\subset\mathbb{C}^{l}
is a connected open subset. Now the lemma is clear.
∎

Lemma 1.5.4
.

Let
\mathcal{F}\colon\mathfrak{U}_{\mathbb{C}}\to\mathsf{M}
be a locally constant prefactorization algebra. For
R\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
,
z\in\mathbb{C}
and a domain
U\subset\mathbb{C}
such that
D_{R}(z)\subset U
:

(1)

The morphism
\mathcal{F}^{D_{R}(z)}_{U}\colon\mathcal{F}(D_{R}(z))\to\mathcal{F}(U)
is a split monomorphism.

(2)

If
\mathcal{F}
is a factorization algebra, then
\mathcal{F}^{D_{R}(z)}_{U}\colon\mathcal{F}(D_{R}(z))\to\mathcal{F}(U)
is an isomorphism.

Proof.

(1) This is clear since
\mathcal{F}^{U}_{\mathbb{C}}\circ\mathcal{F}^{D_{R}(z)}_{U}=\mathcal{F}^{D_{R}(z)}_{\mathbb{C}}
is an isomorphism.

(2) It is enough to show that
\mathcal{F}^{D_{R}(z)}_{U}
is a epimorphism. Take
A\in\Ob\mathsf{M}
and morphisms
f,g\colon\mathcal{F}(U)\to A
such that
f\circ\mathcal{F}^{D_{R}(z)}_{U}=g\circ\mathcal{F}^{D_{R}(z)}_{U}
. To prove
f=g
, by
Proposition

1.2.7
, it suffices to show that
f\circ\mathcal{F}^{L}_{U}=g\circ\mathcal{F}^{L}_{U}
for all
L=D_{r_{1}}(z_{1})\sqcup\cdots\sqcup D_{r_{l}}(z_{l})\in\mathfrak{B}(U)
. Take
r^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
and
z_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,z_{l}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{C}
such that

L^{\prime}\coloneqq D_{r^{\prime}}(z_{1}^{\prime})\sqcup\cdots\sqcup D_{r^{\prime}}(z_{l}^{\prime})\subset D_{R}(z),

then we get

f\circ\mathcal{F}^{L^{\prime}}_{U}=f\circ\mathcal{F}^{D_{R}(z)}_{U}\circ\mathcal{F}^{L^{\prime}}_{D_{R}(z)}=g\circ\mathcal{F}^{D_{R}(z)}_{U}\circ\mathcal{F}^{L^{\prime}}_{D_{R}(z)}=g\circ\mathcal{F}^{L^{\prime}}_{U}.

Also, by
Lemma

1.5.3
, there exists an isomorphism
\mathcal{F}(L)\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathcal{F}(L^{\prime})
which commutes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}(L)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}(U)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}(L^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}^{L}_{U}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}^{L^{\prime}}_{U}}

Thus, we have
f\circ\mathcal{F}^{L}_{U}=g\circ\mathcal{F}^{L}_{U}
.
∎

2.
Commutative algebras and locally constant factorization algebras

In the remaining of this note we will work over the field
\mathbb{C}
of complex numbers, so that all linear spaces and linear maps are defined over
\mathbb{C}
, unless otherwise stated.

Although it is known that the equivalence of
(\infty,1)
-categories between locally constant factorization algebras on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
E_{n}
-algebras, we explicitly prove this equivalence for the case
n=2
with the target category
\mathsf{Mod}\,\mathbb{C}
. We use the following notations:

•

We denote by
\mathsf{PFA}^{\mathsf{loc}}(\mathbb{C})\coloneqq\mathsf{PFA}^{\mathsf{loc}}(\mathbb{C},\mathsf{Mod}\,\mathbb{C})
the category of locally constant prefactorization algebras with values in
\mathsf{Mod}\,\mathbb{C}
.

•

We denote by
\mathsf{FA}^{\mathsf{loc}}(\mathbb{C})\coloneqq\mathsf{FA}^{\mathsf{loc}}(\mathbb{C},\mathsf{Mod}\,\mathbb{C})
the full subcategory of
\mathsf{PFA}^{\mathsf{loc}}(\mathbb{C})
whose objects are factorization algebras.

2.1.
Commutative algebras from locally constant prefactorization algebras

We construct a commutative algebra from a locally constant prefactorization algebra.
Let
\mathcal{F}\colon\mathfrak{U}_{\mathbb{C}}\to\mathsf{Mod}\,\mathbb{C}
be a locally constant prefactorization algebra.

Definition 2.1.1
.

For
l\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
, define a linear map
\mu_{l}
as the composition

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}(\mathbb{C})^{\otimes l}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigotimes_{i=1}^{l}\mathcal{F}(D_{R}(z_{i}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}(\mathbb{C})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\otimes_{i=1}^{n}(\mathcal{F}^{D_{R}(z_{i})}_{\mathbb{C}})^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}^{D_{R}(z_{1}),\ldots,D_{R}(z_{l})}_{\mathbb{C}}}

taking
R\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
and
z_{1},\ldots,z_{l}\in\mathbb{C}
such that
D_{R}(z_{i})\cap D_{R}(z_{j})=\varnothing
(
i,j\in[l]
,
i\neq j
).
Note that by
Lemma

1.5.3
, the definition of
\mu_{l}
is independent of the choice of
R\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
and
z_{1},\ldots,z_{l}\in\mathbb{C}
.

Lemma 2.1.2
.

(1)

For
a,b,c\in\mathcal{F}(\mathbb{C})
, we have
\mu_{2}(\mu_{2}(a\otimes b)\otimes c)=\mu_{3}(a\otimes b\otimes c)=\mu_{2}(a\otimes\mu_{2}(b\otimes c))
.

(2)

For
a,b\in\mathcal{F}(\mathbb{C})
, we have
\mu_{2}(a\otimes b)=\mu_{2}(b\otimes a)
.

(3)

For
a\in\mathcal{F}(\mathbb{C})
, we have
\mu_{2}(a\otimes\mathcal{F}^{\varnothing}_{\mathbb{C}}(1_{\mathcal{F}}))=a
, where
\mathbb{C}\to\mathcal{F}(\varnothing)
,
1\mapsto 1_{\mathcal{F}}
denotes the unit.

Proof.

(1) We prove
\mu_{2}(\mu_{2}(a\otimes b)\otimes c)=\mu_{3}(a\otimes b\otimes c)
. Take
0<r<R
and
z_{1},z_{2},z,w\in\mathbb{C}
such that

D_{r}(z_{1})\sqcup D_{r}(z_{2})\subset D_{R}(z),\quad D_{R}(z)\cap D_{R}(w)=\varnothing,

then

\displaystyle\mu_{2}(\mu_{2}(a\otimes b)\otimes c)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}^{D_{R}(z),D_{R}(w)}_{\mathbb{C}}\Bigl((\mathcal{F}^{D_{R}(z)}_{\mathbb{C}})^{-1}\mathcal{F}^{D_{r}(z_{1}),D_{r}(z_{2})}_{\mathbb{C}}\bigl((\mathcal{F}^{D_{r}(z_{1})}_{\mathbb{C}})^{-1}(a)\otimes(\mathcal{F}^{D_{r}(z_{2})}_{\mathbb{C}})^{-1}(b)\bigr)\otimes(\mathcal{F}^{D_{R}(w)}_{\mathbb{C}})^{-1}(c)\Bigr)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}^{D_{R}(z),D_{R}(w)}_{\mathbb{C}}\Bigl(\mathcal{F}^{D_{r}(z_{1}),D_{r}(z_{2})}_{D_{R}(z)}\bigl((\mathcal{F}^{D_{r}(z_{1})}_{\mathbb{C}})^{-1}(a)\otimes(\mathcal{F}^{D_{r}(z_{2})}_{\mathbb{C}})^{-1}(b)\bigr)\otimes(\mathcal{F}^{D_{R}(w)}_{\mathbb{C}})^{-1}(c)\Bigr)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}^{D_{r}(z_{1}),D_{r}(z_{2}),D_{R}(w)}_{\mathbb{C}}\bigl((\mathcal{F}^{D_{r}(z_{1})}_{\mathbb{C}})^{-1}(a)\otimes(\mathcal{F}^{D_{r}(z_{2})}_{\mathbb{C}})^{-1}(b)\otimes(\mathcal{F}^{D_{R}(w)}_{\mathbb{C}})^{-1}(c)\bigr)

\displaystyle=\mu_{3}(a\otimes b\otimes c),

where we used the first part of
Definition

1.2.1
(i) in the second equality.
Similarly, one can prove
\mu_{2}(a\otimes\mu_{2}(b\otimes c))=\mu_{3}(a\otimes b\otimes c)
.

(2) This follows from
Definition

1.2.1
(ii).

(3) Take
R\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
and
z_{1},z_{2}\in\mathbb{C}
such that
D_{R}(z_{1})\cap D_{R}(z_{2})=\varnothing
, then

\displaystyle\mu_{2}(a\otimes\mathcal{F}^{\varnothing}_{\mathbb{C}}(1_{\mathcal{F}}))

\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}^{D_{R}(z_{1}),D_{R}(z_{2})}_{\mathbb{C}}\bigl((\mathcal{F}^{D_{R}(z_{1})}_{\mathbb{C}})^{-1}(a)\otimes(\mathcal{F}^{D_{R}(z_{2})}_{\mathbb{C}})^{-1}\mathcal{F}^{\varnothing}_{\mathbb{C}}(1_{\mathcal{F}})\bigr)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}^{D_{R}(z_{1}),D_{R}(z_{2})}_{\mathbb{C}}\bigl((\mathcal{F}^{D_{R}(z_{1})}_{\mathbb{C}})^{-1}(a)\otimes\mathcal{F}^{\varnothing}_{D_{R}(z_{2})}(1_{\mathcal{F}})\bigr)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}^{D_{R}(z_{1}),\varnothing}_{\mathbb{C}}\bigl((\mathcal{F}^{D_{R}(z_{1})}_{\mathbb{C}})^{-1}(a)\otimes 1_{\mathcal{F}}\bigr)

\displaystyle=a,

where we used the second part of
Definition

1.2.1
(i) in the third equality and
Definition

1.2.1
(iv) in the forth equality.
∎

Remark 2.1.3
.

Similar to the proof of
Lemma

2.1.2
(1), one can prove

\mu_{2}(\mu_{n}(a_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{n})\otimes a_{n+1})=\mu_{n+1}(a_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{n+1})

for
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
and
a_{1},\ldots,a_{n+1}\in\mathcal{F}(\mathbb{C})
. As a result, we have

a_{1}\cdots a_{n}=\mu_{n}(a_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{n}),

where we denote

a_{1}\cdots a_{n}\coloneqq(a_{1}\cdots a_{n-1})a_{n},\quad a_{1}a_{2}\coloneqq\mu_{2}(a_{1}\otimes a_{2}).

Proposition 2.1.4
.

For a locally constant prefactorization algebra
\mathcal{F}\colon\mathfrak{U}_{\mathbb{C}}\to\mathsf{Mod}\,\mathbb{C}
, the linear space
\mathcal{F}(\mathbb{C})
has the structure of commutative algebra as follows:

•

The multiplication is
\mu_{2}\colon\mathcal{F}(\mathbb{C})\otimes\mathcal{F}(\mathbb{C})\to\mathcal{F}(\mathbb{C})
defined in
Definition

2.1.1
.

•

The unit is
\mathcal{F}^{\varnothing}_{\mathbb{C}}(1_{\mathcal{F}})
, where
\mathbb{C}\to\mathcal{F}(\varnothing)
,
1\mapsto 1_{\mathcal{F}}
denotes the unit.

Proof.

This follows from
Lemma

2.1.2
.
∎

Remark 2.1.5
.

Let
\mathcal{F}\colon\mathfrak{U}_{\mathbb{C}}\to\mathsf{Mod}\,\mathbb{C}
be a locally constant prefactorization algebra.
For
R\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
and
z\in\mathbb{C}
, since
\mathcal{F}^{D_{R}(z)}_{\mathbb{C}}\colon\mathcal{F}(D_{R}(z))\to\mathcal{F}(\mathbb{C})
is a linear isomorphism, the linear space
\mathcal{F}(D_{R}(z))
inherits the structure of a commutative algebra from
\mathcal{F}(\mathbb{C})
. The multiplication on
\mathcal{F}(D_{R}(z))
is equal to

a\cdot b=\mathcal{F}^{D_{r}(z_{1}),D_{r}(z_{2})}_{D_{R}(z)}\bigl((\mathcal{F}^{D_{r}(z_{1})}_{D_{R}(z)})^{-1}(a)\otimes(\mathcal{F}^{D_{r}(z_{2})}_{D_{R}(z)})^{-1}(b)\bigr)

for any
r\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
and
z_{1},z_{2}\in\mathbb{C}
such that
D_{r}(z_{1})\sqcup D_{r}(z_{2})\subset D_{R}(z)
. Also, the unit of
\mathcal{F}(D_{R}(z))
is
\mathcal{F}^{\varnothing}_{D_{R}(z)}(1_{\mathcal{F}})
.

Proposition 2.1.6
.

For locally constant prefactorization algebras
\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}\colon\mathfrak{U}_{\mathbb{C}}\to\mathsf{Mod}\,\mathbb{C}
and a morphism
\varphi\colon\mathcal{F}\to\mathcal{G}
of prefactorization algebras, the linear map
\varphi_{\mathbb{C}}\colon\mathcal{F}(\mathbb{C})\to\mathcal{G}(\mathbb{C})
is a morphism of commutative algebras. Thus, the map
\mathcal{F}\mapsto\mathcal{F}(\mathbb{C})
gives rise to the functor

(-)(\mathbb{C})\colon\mathsf{PFA}^{\mathsf{loc}}(\mathbb{C})\to\mathsf{CAlg}_{\mathbb{C}}

Proof.

We proceed the following steps:

•

The linear map
\varphi_{\mathbb{C}}
preserves the multiplication: For
a,b\in\mathcal{F}(\mathbb{C})
, take
R\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
and
z_{1},z_{2}\in\mathbb{C}
such that
D_{R}(z_{1})\cap D_{R}(z_{2})=\varnothing
, then

\displaystyle\varphi_{\mathbb{C}}(ab)

\displaystyle=\varphi_{\mathbb{C}}\mathcal{F}^{D_{R}(z_{1}),D_{R}(z_{2})}_{\mathbb{C}}\bigl((\mathcal{F}^{D_{R}(z_{1})}_{\mathbb{C}})^{-1}(a)\otimes(\mathcal{F}^{D_{R}(z_{2})}_{\mathbb{C}})^{-1}(b)\bigr)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{G}^{D_{R}(z_{1}),D_{R}(z_{2})}_{\mathbb{C}}\bigl(\varphi_{D_{R}(z_{1})}(\mathcal{F}^{D_{R}(z_{1})}_{\mathbb{C}})^{-1}(a)\otimes\varphi_{D_{R}(z_{2})}(\mathcal{F}^{D_{R}(z_{2})}_{\mathbb{C}})^{-1}(b)\bigr)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{G}^{D_{R}(z_{1}),D_{R}(z_{2})}_{\mathbb{C}}\bigl((\mathcal{G}^{D_{R}(z_{1})}_{\mathbb{C}})^{-1}\varphi_{\mathbb{C}}(a)\otimes(\mathcal{G}^{D_{R}(z_{2})}_{\mathbb{C}})^{-1}\varphi_{\mathbb{C}}(b)\bigr)

\displaystyle=\varphi_{\mathbb{C}}(a)\varphi_{\mathbb{C}}(b).

•

The linear map
\varphi_{\mathbb{C}}
preserves the unit: We have

\varphi_{\mathbb{C}}\mathcal{F}^{\varnothing}_{\mathbb{C}}(1_{\mathcal{F}})=\mathcal{G}^{\varnothing}_{\mathbb{C}}\varphi_{\varnothing}(1_{\mathcal{F}})=\mathcal{G}^{\varnothing}_{\mathbb{C}}(1_{\mathcal{G}}).\qed

2.2.
Locally constant factorization algebras from commutative algebras

Next, we construct a locally constant factorization algebra from a commutative algebra. Let
A
be a commutative algebra. We use the notation
\mathfrak{B}
defined in (
1.5.1
).
Note that
\mathfrak{B}
is an open basis of
\mathbb{C}
satisfying:

1

\varnothing\in\mathfrak{B}
.

2

For
L,M\in\mathfrak{B}
such that
L\cap M=\varnothing
, we have
L\sqcup M\in\mathfrak{B}
.

Definition 2.2.1
.

Define a map
F^{\mathrm{loc}}_{A}\colon\mathfrak{B}\to\Ob(\mathsf{Mod}\,\mathbb{C})
by

F^{\mathrm{loc}}_{A}(D_{r_{1}}(z_{1})\sqcup\cdots\sqcup D_{r_{l}}(z_{l}))\coloneqq\bigotimes_{i=1}^{l}F^{\mathrm{loc}}_{A}(D_{r_{i}}(z_{i})),\quad F^{\mathrm{loc}}_{A}(D_{r_{i}}(z_{i}))\coloneqq A

with the convention
F^{\mathrm{loc}}_{A}(\varnothing)\coloneqq\mathbb{C}
for
l=0
. Also, for

L=D_{r_{1}}(z_{1})\sqcup\cdots\sqcup D_{r_{l}}(z_{l}),\ M=D_{R_{1}}(w_{1})\sqcup\cdots\sqcup D_{R_{m}}(w_{m})\in\mathfrak{B}

such that
L\subset M
, define a linear map
(F^{\mathrm{loc}}_{A})^{L}_{M}\colon F^{\mathrm{loc}}_{A}(L)\to F^{\mathrm{loc}}_{A}(M)
as follows:

(1)

If
l=m=0
, then define
(F^{\mathrm{loc}}_{A})^{L}_{M}(1_{\mathbb{C}})\coloneqq 1_{\mathbb{C}}
.

(2)

If
l=0
,
m>0
, then define
(F^{\mathrm{loc}}_{A})^{L}_{M}(1_{\mathbb{C}})\coloneqq\otimes_{j=1}^{m}1_{A}
.

(3)

If
l,m>0
, then define

(F^{\mathrm{loc}}_{A})^{L}_{M}(\otimes_{i=1}^{l}a_{i})\coloneqq\otimes_{j=1}^{m}\prod_{i\in I_{j}}a_{i},

where
I_{1}\sqcup\cdots\sqcup I_{m}=[l]
is a unique decomposition such that
\bigsqcup_{i\in I_{j}}D_{r_{i}}(z_{i})\subset D_{R_{j}}(w_{j})
.

Lemma 2.2.2
.

The map
F^{\mathrm{loc}}_{A}\colon\mathfrak{B}\to\Ob(\mathsf{Mod}\,\mathbb{C})
is a precosheaf by
(F^{\mathrm{loc}}_{A})^{L}_{M}
.

Proof.

It is clear that
(F^{\mathrm{loc}}_{A})^{L}_{L}=\id_{F^{\mathrm{loc}}_{A}(L)}
for all
L\in\mathfrak{B}
. Let
L,M,N\in\mathfrak{B}
such that
L\subset M\subset N
, and write

L=D_{r_{1}}(z_{1})\sqcup\cdots\sqcup D_{r_{l}}(z_{l}),\quad M=D_{s_{1}}(w_{1})\sqcup\cdots\sqcup D_{s_{m}}(w_{m}),\quad N=D_{R_{1}}(\zeta_{1})\sqcup\cdots\sqcup D_{R_{n}}(\zeta_{n}).

Take
a=a_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{l}\in F^{\mathrm{loc}}_{A}(L)
. Then,

(F^{\mathrm{loc}}_{A})^{M}_{N}(F^{\mathrm{loc}}_{A})^{L}_{M}(a)=\otimes_{k=1}^{n}\prod_{j\in J_{k}}\prod_{i\in I_{j}}a_{i},

where
I_{1}\sqcup\cdots\sqcup I_{m}=[l]
and
J_{1}\sqcup\cdots\sqcup J_{n}=[m]
are the decompositions satisfying

\bigsqcup_{i\in I_{j}}D_{r_{i}}(z_{i})\subset D_{s_{j}}(w_{j}),\quad\bigsqcup_{j\in J_{k}}D_{s_{j}}(w_{j})\subset D_{R_{k}}(\zeta_{k}).

On the other hand,

(F^{\mathrm{loc}}_{A})^{L}_{N}(a)=\otimes_{k=1}^{n}\prod_{i\in I_{k}^{\prime}}a_{i},

where
I_{1}^{\prime}\sqcup\cdots\sqcup I_{n}^{\prime}=[l]
is the decomposition satisfying

\bigsqcup_{i\in I_{k}^{\prime}}D_{r_{i}}(z_{i})\subset D_{R_{k}}(\zeta_{k}).

Since
\bigsqcup_{j\in J_{k}}I_{j}=I_{k}^{\prime}
for each
k\in[n]
, we have
(F^{\mathrm{loc}}_{A})^{M}_{N}(F^{\mathrm{loc}}_{A})^{L}_{M}=(F^{\mathrm{loc}}_{A})^{L}_{N}
.
∎

Lemma 2.2.3
.

For
L_{1},L_{2},M_{1},M_{2}\in\mathfrak{B}
such that
L_{1}\subset M_{1}
,
L_{2}\subset M_{2}
and
M_{1}\cap M_{2}=\varnothing
, we have

(F^{\mathrm{loc}}_{A})^{L_{1}\sqcup L_{2}}_{M_{1}\sqcup M_{2}}=(F^{\mathrm{loc}}_{A})^{L_{1}}_{M_{1}}\otimes(F^{\mathrm{loc}}_{A})^{L_{2}}_{M_{2}}.

Proof.

Write

\displaystyle L_{1}=D_{r_{1,1}}(z_{1,1})\sqcup\cdots\sqcup D_{r_{1,l_{1}}}(z_{1,l_{1}}),\hskip 31.0ptL_{2}=D_{r_{2,1}}(z_{2,1})\sqcup\cdots\sqcup D_{r_{2,l_{2}}}(z_{2,l_{2}}),

\displaystyle M_{1}=D_{R_{1,1}}(w_{1,1})\sqcup\cdots\sqcup D_{R_{1,m_{1}}}(w_{1,m_{1}}),\quad M_{2}=D_{R_{2,1}}(w_{2,1})\sqcup\cdots\sqcup D_{R_{2,m_{2}}}(w_{2,m_{2}}),

and denote by

I_{1,1}\sqcup\cdots\sqcup I_{1,m_{1}}=[l_{1}],\quad I_{2,1}\sqcup\cdots\sqcup I_{2,m_{2}}=[l_{2}]

the decompositions satisfying

\bigsqcup_{i\in I_{1,j}}D_{r_{1,i}}(z_{1,i})\subset D_{R_{1,j}}(w_{1,j}),\quad\bigsqcup_{i\in I_{2,j}}D_{r_{2,i}}(z_{2,i})\subset D_{R_{2,j}}(w_{2,j}).

Take
a^{1}=a_{1,1}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{1,l_{1}}\in F^{\mathrm{loc}}_{A}(L_{1})
and
a^{2}=a_{2,1}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{2,l_{2}}\in F^{\mathrm{loc}}_{A}(L_{2})
. Then,

(F^{\mathrm{loc}}_{A})^{L_{1}}_{M_{1}}(a^{1})\otimes(F^{\mathrm{loc}}_{A})^{L_{2}}_{M_{2}}(a^{2})=\bigl(\prod_{i\in I_{1,1}}a_{1,i}\bigr)\otimes\cdots\otimes\bigl(\prod_{i\in I_{1,m_{1}}}a_{1,i}\bigr)\otimes\bigl(\prod_{i\in I_{2,1}}a_{2,i}\bigr)\otimes\cdots\otimes\bigl(\prod_{i\in I_{2,m_{2}}}a_{2,i}\bigr).

Now, set for the symbol
x\in\{a,r,z\}
and
y\in\{R,w\}
,

x_{i}\coloneqq\begin{cases}x_{1,i}&(1\leq i\leq l_{1})\\
x_{2,i-l_{1}}&(l_{1}<i\leq l_{1}+l_{2}),\end{cases}\quad y_{j}\coloneqq\begin{cases}y_{1,j}&(1\leq j\leq m_{1})\\
y_{2,j-m_{1}}&(m_{1}<j\leq m_{1}+m_{2}),\end{cases}

and denote

I_{j}\coloneqq\begin{cases}I_{1,j}&(1\leq j\leq m_{1})\\
l_{1}+I_{2,j-m_{1}}&(m_{1}<j\leq m_{1}+m_{2}).\end{cases}

Then, we find that
I_{1}\sqcup\cdots\sqcup I_{m_{1}+m_{2}}=[l_{1}+l_{2}]
is the decomposition satisfying

\bigsqcup_{i\in I_{j}}D_{r_{i}}(z_{i})\subset D_{R_{j}}(w_{j}).

Thus, we have

(F^{\mathrm{loc}}_{A})^{L_{1}\sqcup L_{2}}_{M_{1}\sqcup M_{2}}(a^{1}\otimes a^{2})=\bigl(\prod_{i\in I_{1}}a_{i}\bigr)\otimes\cdots\otimes\bigl(\prod_{i\in I_{m_{1}+m_{2}}}a_{i}\bigr)=(F^{\mathrm{loc}}_{A})^{L_{1}}_{M_{1}}(a^{1})\otimes(F^{\mathrm{loc}}_{A})^{L_{2}}_{M_{2}}(a^{2}).\qed

Lemma 2.2.4
.

The precosheaf
F^{\mathrm{loc}}_{A}\colon\mathfrak{B}\to\mathsf{Mod}\,\mathbb{C}
is a prefactorization algebra by the multiplication

(F^{\mathrm{loc}}_{A})^{L,M}_{N}\coloneqq(F^{\mathrm{loc}}_{A})^{L\sqcup M}_{N}\colon F^{\mathrm{loc}}_{A}(L)\otimes F^{\mathrm{loc}}_{A}(M)\to F^{\mathrm{loc}}_{A}(N)\quad(L,M,N\in\mathfrak{B},L\sqcup M\subset N)

and the unit
\id_{\mathbb{C}}\colon\mathbb{C}\to F^{\mathrm{loc}}_{A}(\varnothing)
.

Proof.

We check the conditions (i)-(iv) in
Definition

1.2.1
.

1

The first part is clear by the definition of
(F^{\mathrm{loc}}_{A})^{L,M}_{N}
. Also, the second part follows from
Lemma

2.2.3
.

2

Let
L_{1},L_{2},M\in\mathfrak{B}
such that
L_{1}\sqcup L_{2}\subset M
, and write

L_{1}=D_{r_{1,1}}(z_{1,1})\sqcup\cdots\sqcup D_{r_{1,l_{1}}}(z_{1,l_{1}}),\quad L_{2}=D_{r_{2,1}}(z_{2,1})\sqcup\cdots\sqcup D_{r_{2,l_{2}}}(z_{2,l_{2}}).

Take
a^{1}=a_{1,1}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{1,l_{1}}\in F^{\mathrm{loc}}_{A}(L_{1})
and
a^{2}=a_{2,1}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{2,l_{2}}\in F^{\mathrm{loc}}_{A}(L_{2})
. For the symbol
x\in\{a,r,z\}
, set

x_{i}\coloneqq\begin{cases}x_{1,i}&(1\leq i\leq l_{1})\\
x_{2,i-l_{1}}&(l_{1}<i\leq l_{1}+l_{2}),\end{cases}

then

(F_{A}^{\mathrm{loc}})^{L_{2},L_{1}}_{M}(a^{2}\otimes a^{1})=(F_{A}^{\mathrm{loc}})^{L_{1}\sqcup L_{2}}_{M}(a_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{l_{1}+l_{2}})=(F_{A}^{\mathrm{loc}})^{L_{1},L_{2}}_{M}(a^{1}\otimes a^{2}).

3

This follows from
Lemma

2.2.3
and the commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{A}^{\mathrm{loc}}(L_{1}\sqcup L_{2}\sqcup L_{3})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{A}^{\mathrm{loc}}(L_{1}\sqcup L_{2}\sqcup L_{3})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{A}^{\mathrm{loc}}(M_{1}\sqcup L_{3})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{A}^{\mathrm{loc}}(L_{1}\sqcup M_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{A}^{\mathrm{loc}}(N)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(F_{A}^{\mathrm{loc}})^{L_{1}\sqcup L_{2}\sqcup L_{3}}_{M_{1}\sqcup L_{3}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(F_{A}^{\mathrm{loc}})^{L_{1}\sqcup L_{2}\sqcup L_{3}}_{L_{1}\sqcup M_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(F_{A}^{\mathrm{loc}})^{M_{1}\sqcup L_{3}}_{N}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(F_{A}^{\mathrm{loc}})^{L_{1}\sqcup M_{2}}_{N}}

4

This follows from the definition of
(F_{A}^{\mathrm{loc}})^{L,M}_{N}
.
∎

By
Lemma

2.2.4
and the construction in
§​

1.4
, we have a prefactorization algebra

\mathbf{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{A}\coloneqq\mathcal{E}_{F_{A}^{\mathrm{loc}}}\colon\mathfrak{U}_{\mathbb{C}}\to\mathsf{Mod}\,\mathbb{C}.

Recall that for an open subset
U\subset\mathbb{C}
,

\mathbf{F}_{A}^{\mathrm{loc}}(U)=\varinjlim_{L\in\mathfrak{B}(U)}F^{\mathrm{loc}}_{A}(L),

and that the canonical morphism is denoted by

(\widetilde{F}_{A}^{\mathrm{loc}})^{L}_{U}\colon F_{A}^{\mathrm{loc}}(L)\to\mathbf{F}_{A}^{\mathrm{loc}}(U)\quad(L\in\mathfrak{B}(U)).

Proposition 2.2.5
.

For a commutative algebra
A
, the prefactorization algebra
\mathbf{F}_{A}^{\mathrm{loc}}\colon\mathfrak{U}_{\mathbb{C}}\to\mathsf{Mod}\,\mathbb{C}
is a locally constant factorization algebra.

Proof.

First, we prove that
\mathbf{F}_{A}^{\mathrm{loc}}
is a factorization algebra. By
Proposition

1.4.1
, we only need to check the condition (i) in
Definition

1.2.6
. Let
U\subset\mathbb{C}
be an open subset, and
\{U_{i}\}_{i\in I}
a Weiss cover of
U
. We use the notations
p,q,\pi
defined in (
1.2.1
) for
\mathbf{F}_{A}^{\mathrm{loc}}
. Fix a linear space
E
and a linear map
f\colon\bigoplus_{i\in I}\mathbf{F}_{A}^{\mathrm{loc}}(U_{i})\to E
such that
f\circ p=f\circ q
. For
L=D_{r_{1}}(z_{1})\sqcup\cdots\sqcup D_{r_{l}}(z_{l})\in\mathfrak{B}(U)
, define a linear map
f_{L}\colon F_{A}^{\mathrm{loc}}(L)\to E
as the composition

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{\mathrm{loc}}_{A}(L)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{\mathrm{loc}}_{A}(D_{r}(z_{1})\sqcup\cdots\sqcup D_{r}(z_{l}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{F}_{A}^{\mathrm{loc}}(U_{i})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigoplus_{i\in I}\mathbf{F}_{A}^{\mathrm{loc}}(U_{i})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\widetilde{F}_{A}^{\mathrm{loc}})^{D_{r}(z_{1})\sqcup\cdots\sqcup D_{r}(z_{l})}_{U_{i}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

where we take
i\in I
and
r\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
satisfying

z_{1},\ldots,z_{l}\in U_{i},\quad D_{r}(z_{1})\sqcup\cdots\sqcup D_{r}(z_{l})\subset U_{i}.

Notice that the condition
f\circ p=f\circ q
and the commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{F}_{A}^{\mathrm{loc}}(U_{i}\cap U_{j})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{F}_{A}^{\mathrm{loc}}(U_{i})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigoplus_{i,j\in I}\mathbf{F}_{A}^{\mathrm{loc}}(U_{i}\cap U_{j})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigoplus_{i\in I}\mathbf{F}_{A}^{\mathrm{loc}}(U_{i})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{F}_{A}^{\mathrm{loc}}(U_{i}\cap U_{j})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{F}_{A}^{\mathrm{loc}}(U_{j})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathbf{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{A})^{U_{i}\cap U_{j}}_{U_{i}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathbf{F}_{A}^{\mathrm{loc}})^{U_{i}\cap U_{j}}_{U_{j}}}

guarantee that the definition of
f_{L}
is independent of the choice of
i\in I
and
r\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
. Since
f_{M}\circ(F_{A}^{\mathrm{loc}})^{L}_{M}=f_{L}
holds for
L,M\in\mathfrak{B}(U)
such that
L\subset M
, the universality of
\mathbf{F}_{A}^{\mathrm{loc}}(U)=\varinjlim_{L\in\mathfrak{B}(U)}F_{A}^{\mathrm{loc}}(L)
yields a unique linear map
\widetilde{f}\colon\mathbf{F}_{A}^{\mathrm{loc}}(U)\to E
satisfying
\widetilde{f}\circ(\widetilde{F}_{A}^{\mathrm{loc}})^{L}_{U}=f_{L}
for all
L\in\mathfrak{B}(U)
. We find that
\widetilde{f}
is a unique linear map satisfying
\widetilde{f}\circ\pi=f
. Thus
(\mathbf{F}_{A}^{\mathrm{loc}}(U),\pi)
is a coequalizer of
(p,q)
.

It is now clear that
\mathbf{F}_{A}^{\mathrm{loc}}
is locally constant. In fact, by
Lemma

1.5.2
, it is enough to show that the linear map
(\mathbf{F}_{A}^{\mathrm{loc}})^{D_{r}(z)}_{D_{R}(w)}
is an isomorphism for
0<r<R<\infty
and
z,w\in\mathbb{C}
such that
D_{r}(z)\subset D_{R}(w)
, but this is obvious since
(F_{A}^{\mathrm{loc}})^{D_{r}(z)}_{D_{R}(w)}
is an isomorphism.
∎

For a morphism
f\colon A\to B
of commutative algebras and
L=D_{r_{1}}(z_{1})\sqcup\cdots\sqcup D_{r_{l}}(z_{l})\in\mathfrak{B}
, define

F^{\mathrm{loc}}_{f,L}\colon F_{A}^{\mathrm{loc}}(L)\to F_{B}^{\mathrm{loc}}(L),\quad a_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{l}\mapsto f(a_{1})\otimes\cdots\otimes f(a_{l})

with the convention

F^{\mathrm{loc}}_{f,\varnothing}\coloneqq\id_{\mathbb{C}}\colon F_{A}^{\mathrm{loc}}(\varnothing)\to F_{B}^{\mathrm{loc}}(\varnothing).

Lemma 2.2.6
.

For a morphism
f\colon A\to B
of commutative algebras,

F_{f}^{\mathrm{loc}}\coloneqq\{F_{f,L}^{\mathrm{loc}}\}_{L\in\mathfrak{B}}\colon F_{A}^{\mathrm{loc}}\to F_{B}^{\mathrm{loc}}

is a morphism of prefactorization algebras.

Proof.

We proceed the following steps:

•

F_{f}^{\mathrm{loc}}\colon F_{A}^{\mathrm{loc}}\to F_{B}^{\mathrm{loc}}
is a morphism of precosheaves:
Let
L,M\in\mathfrak{B}
such that
L\subset M
. Write

L=D_{r_{1}}(z_{1})\sqcup\cdots\sqcup D_{r_{l}}(z_{l}),\quad M=D_{R_{1}}(w_{1})\sqcup\cdots\sqcup D_{R_{m}}(w_{m}),

and denote by
I_{1}\sqcup\cdots\sqcup I_{m}=[l]
the decomposition satisfying

\bigsqcup_{i\in I_{j}}D_{r_{i}}(z_{i})\subset D_{R_{j}}(w_{j}).

Then, for
a=a_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{l}\in F_{A}^{\mathrm{loc}}(L)
,

F_{f,M}^{\mathrm{loc}}(F_{A}^{\mathrm{loc}})^{L}_{M}(a)=\otimes_{j=1}^{m}f\bigl(\prod_{i\in I_{j}}a_{i}\bigr)=\otimes_{j=1}^{m}\prod_{i\in I_{j}}f(a_{i})=(F_{B}^{\mathrm{loc}})^{L}_{M}F_{f,L}^{\mathrm{loc}}(a).

•

F_{f}^{\mathrm{loc}}\colon F_{A}^{\mathrm{loc}}\to F_{B}^{\mathrm{loc}}
is a morphism of prefactorization algebras: The condition (i) in
Definition

1.2.3
follows from
F_{f,L}^{\mathrm{loc}}\otimes F_{f,M}^{\mathrm{loc}}=F_{f,L\sqcup M}^{\mathrm{loc}}
(
L,M\in\mathfrak{B}
,
L\cap M=\varnothing
). Also, the condition (ii) in
Definition

1.2.3
is clear by the definition
F_{f,\varnothing}^{\mathrm{loc}}=\id_{\mathbb{C}}
.
∎

For a morphism
f\colon A\to B
of commutative algebras, by
Lemma

2.2.6
and
§​

1.4
, we have a morphism

\mathbf{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{f}\coloneqq\mathcal{E}_{F^{\mathrm{loc}}_{f}}\colon\mathbf{F}_{A}^{\mathrm{loc}}\to\mathbf{F}_{B}^{\mathrm{loc}}

of factorization algebras. Thus, the map
A\mapsto\mathbf{F}_{A}^{\mathrm{loc}}
gives rise to the functor

\mathbf{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}\colon\mathsf{CAlg}_{\mathbb{C}}\to\mathsf{FA}^{\mathsf{loc}}(\mathbb{C}).

2.3.
Adjointness and categorical equivalence

Finally, we prove that
\mathbf{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}\colon\mathsf{CAlg}_{\mathbb{C}}\to\mathsf{PFA}^{\mathsf{loc}}(\mathbb{C})
is a fully faithful functor which is the left adjoint of
(-)(\mathbb{C})\colon\mathsf{PFA}^{\mathsf{loc}}(\mathbb{C})\to\mathsf{CAlg}_{\mathbb{C}}
, and that the essential image of
\mathbf{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}
consists of locally constant factorization algebras.

Definition 2.3.1
.

Let
A\in\Ob(\mathsf{CAlg}_{\mathbb{C}})
and
\mathcal{F}\in\Ob(\mathsf{PFA}^{\mathsf{loc}}(\mathbb{C}))
. For a morphism
\varphi\colon\mathbf{F}_{A}^{\mathrm{loc}}\to\mathcal{F}
in
\mathsf{PFA}^{\mathsf{loc}}(\mathbb{C})
, define a linear map
\theta_{A,\mathcal{F}}(\varphi)
as the composition

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{F}_{A}^{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{C})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}(\mathbb{C}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\widetilde{F}_{A}^{\mathrm{loc}})^{D_{R}(z)}_{\mathbb{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{\mathbb{C}}}

Here we take
R\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
and
z\in\mathbb{C}
, but the definition of
\theta_{A,\mathcal{F}}(\varphi)
is independent of the choice of
R
and
z
.

Lemma 2.3.2
.

Let
A\in\Ob(\mathsf{CAlg}_{\mathbb{C}})
. For
R\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
and
z\in\mathbb{C}
, the linear map
(\widetilde{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{A})^{D_{R}(z)}_{D_{R}(z)}\colon A\to\mathbf{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{A}(D_{R}(z))
is an isomorphism in
\mathsf{CAlg}_{\mathbb{C}}
. (See
Remark

2.1.5
for the commutative algebra structure on
\mathbf{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{A}(D_{R}(0))
.)

Proof.

It is enough to show that
(\widetilde{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{A})^{D_{R}(z)}_{D_{R}(z)}
is a morphism of commutative algebras.

•

(\widetilde{F}_{A}^{\mathrm{loc}})^{D_{R}(z)}_{D_{R}(z)}
preserves the multiplications: For
a,b\in A
, take
r\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
and
z_{1},z_{2}\in\mathbb{C}
such that
D_{r}(z_{1})\sqcup D_{r}(z_{2})\subset D_{R}(z)
, then

\displaystyle(\widetilde{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{A})^{D_{R}(z)}_{D_{R}(z)}(a)\cdot(\widetilde{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{A})^{D_{R}(z)}_{D_{R}(z)}(b)

\displaystyle=(\mathbf{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{A})^{D_{r}(z_{1}),D_{r}(z_{2})}_{D_{R}(z)}\bigl(\bigl((\mathbf{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{A})^{D_{r}(z_{1})}_{D_{R}(z)}\bigr)^{-1}(\widetilde{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{A})^{D_{R}(z)}_{D_{R}(z)}(a)\otimes\bigl((\mathbf{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{A})^{D_{r}(z_{2})}_{D_{R}(z)}\bigr)^{-1}(\widetilde{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{A})^{D_{R}(z)}_{D_{R}(z)}(b)\bigr)

\displaystyle=(\mathbf{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{A})^{D_{r}(z_{1}),D_{r}(z_{2})}_{D_{R}(z)}\bigl((\widetilde{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{A})^{D_{r}(z_{1})}_{D_{r}(z_{1})}(a)\otimes(\widetilde{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{A})^{D_{r}(z_{2})}_{D_{r}(z_{2})}(b)\bigr)

\displaystyle=(\widetilde{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{A})^{D_{r}(z_{1})\sqcup D_{r}(z_{2})}_{D_{R}(z)}(F^{\mathrm{loc}}_{A})^{D_{r}(z_{1}),D_{r}(z_{2})}_{D_{r}(z_{1})\sqcup D_{r}(z_{2})}(a\otimes b)=(\widetilde{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{A})^{D_{r}(z_{1})\sqcup D_{r}(z_{2})}_{D_{R}(z)}(a\otimes b)

\displaystyle=(\widetilde{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{A})^{D_{R}(z)}_{D_{R}(z)}(F^{\mathrm{loc}}_{A})^{D_{r}(z_{1})\sqcup D_{r}(z_{2})}_{D_{R}(z)}(a\otimes b)=(\widetilde{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{A})^{D_{R}(z)}_{D_{R}(z)}(ab).

•

(\widetilde{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{A})^{D_{R}(z)}_{D_{R}(z)}
preserves the units:
We have

(\mathbf{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{A})^{\varnothing}_{D_{R}(z)}(1_{\mathbf{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{A}})=(\mathbf{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{A})^{\varnothing}_{D_{R}(z)}(\widetilde{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{A})^{\varnothing}_{\varnothing}(1_{\mathbb{C}})=(\widetilde{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{A})^{D_{R}(z)}_{D_{R}(z)}(F^{\mathrm{loc}}_{A})^{\varnothing}_{D_{R}(z)}(1_{\mathbb{C}})=(\widetilde{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{A})^{D_{R}(z)}_{D_{R}(z)}(1_{A}).\qed

Lemma 2.3.3
.

Let
A\in\Ob(\mathsf{CAlg}_{\mathbb{C}})
and
\mathcal{F}\in\Ob(\mathsf{PFA}^{\mathsf{loc}}(\mathbb{C}))
. For a morphism
\varphi\colon\mathbf{F}_{A}^{\mathrm{loc}}\to\mathcal{F}
in
\mathsf{PFA}^{\mathsf{loc}}(\mathbb{C})
, the linear map

\theta_{A,\mathcal{F}}(\varphi)\colon A\to\mathcal{F}(\mathbb{C})

is a morphism in
\mathsf{CAlg}_{\mathbb{C}}
.

Proof.

It is enough to show that
(\widetilde{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{A})^{D_{R}(z)}_{\mathbb{C}}\colon A\to\mathbf{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{A}(\mathbb{C})
is a morphism of commutative algebras for
R\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
and
z\in\mathbb{C}
, but since
(\widetilde{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{A})^{D_{R}(z)}_{\mathbb{C}}=(\mathbf{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{A})^{D_{R}(z)}_{\mathbb{C}}\circ(\widetilde{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{A})^{D_{R}(z)}_{D_{R}(z)}
, this follows from
Lemma

2.3.2
.
∎

Proposition 2.3.4
.

The map

\theta_{A,\mathcal{F}}\colon\Hom_{\mathsf{PFA}^{\mathsf{loc}}(\mathbb{C})}(\mathbf{F}_{A}^{\mathrm{loc}},\mathcal{F})\to\Hom_{\mathsf{CAlg}_{\mathbb{C}}}(A,\mathcal{F}(\mathbb{C})),\quad\varphi\mapsto\theta_{A,\mathcal{F}}(\varphi)

is a bijection which is natural in
A\in\Ob(\mathsf{CAlg}_{\mathbb{C}})
and
\mathcal{F}\in\Ob(\mathsf{PFA}^{\mathsf{loc}}(\mathbb{C}))
. Thus, the functor
\mathbf{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}\colon\mathsf{CAlg}_{\mathbb{C}}\to\mathsf{PFA}^{\mathsf{loc}}(\mathbb{C})
is the left adjoint of
(-)(\mathbb{C})\colon\mathsf{PFA}^{\mathsf{loc}}(\mathbb{C})\to\mathsf{CAlg}_{\mathbb{C}}
.

We prove
Proposition

2.3.4
. It is easy to see that
\theta_{A,\mathcal{F}}
is natural in
A
and
\mathcal{F}
. For
A\in\Ob(\mathsf{CAlg}_{\mathbb{C}})
and
\mathcal{F}\in\Ob(\mathsf{PFA}^{\mathsf{loc}}(\mathbb{C}))
, we construct the inverse map of
\theta_{A,\mathcal{F}}
.

Let
f\colon A\to\mathcal{F}(\mathbb{C})
be a morphism in
\mathsf{CAlg}_{\mathbb{C}}
. For an open subset
U\subset\mathbb{C}
and
L=D_{r_{1}}(z_{1})\sqcup\cdots\sqcup D_{r_{l}}(z_{l})\in\mathfrak{B}(U)
, define a linear map
\theta^{\prime}_{A,\mathcal{F}}(f)^{L}_{U}
by

\theta^{\prime}_{A,\mathcal{F}}(f)^{L}_{U}\colon F_{A}^{\mathrm{loc}}(L)\to\mathcal{F}(U),\quad\otimes_{i=1}^{l}a_{i}\mapsto\mathcal{F}^{\{D_{r_{i}}(z_{i})\}_{i=1}^{l}}_{U}\bigl(\otimes_{i=1}^{l}(\mathcal{F}^{D_{r_{i}}(z_{i})}_{\mathbb{C}})^{-1}f(a_{i})\bigr)

with the convention

\theta^{\prime}_{A,\mathcal{F}}(f)^{\varnothing}_{U}\colon F_{A}^{\mathrm{loc}}(\varnothing)=\mathbb{C}\to\mathcal{F}(U),\quad 1_{\mathbb{C}}\mapsto\mathcal{F}^{\varnothing}_{U}(1_{\mathcal{F}})

for
l=0
.

Lemma 2.3.5
.

Let
f\colon A\to\mathcal{F}(\mathbb{C})
be a morphism in
\mathsf{CAlg}_{\mathbb{C}}
. For an open subset
U\subset\mathbb{C}
and
L,M\in\mathfrak{B}(U)
such that
L\subset M
, we have

\theta^{\prime}_{A,\mathcal{F}}(f)^{M}_{U}\circ(F_{A}^{\mathrm{loc}})^{L}_{M}=\theta^{\prime}_{A,\mathcal{F}}(f)^{L}_{U}.

Proof.

Write
