L=D_{r_{1}}(z_{1})\sqcup\cdots\sqcup D_{r_{l}}(z_{l}),\quad M=D_{R_{1}}(w_{1})\sqcup\cdots\sqcup D_{R_{m}}(w_{m}),

and denote by
I_{1}\sqcup\cdots\sqcup I_{m}=[l]
the decomposition such that

\bigsqcup_{i\in I_{j}}D_{r_{i}}(z_{i})\subset D_{R_{j}}(w_{j}).

Let
a=a_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{l}\in F_{A}^{\mathrm{loc}}(L)
. Then

\displaystyle\theta^{\prime}_{A,\mathcal{F}}(f)^{L}_{U}(a)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}^{\{D_{r_{i}}(z_{i})\}_{i=1}^{l}}_{U}\bigl(\otimes_{i=1}^{l}(\mathcal{F}^{D_{r_{i}}(z_{i})}_{\mathbb{C}})^{-1}f(a_{i})\bigr)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}^{\{D_{R_{j}}(w_{j})\}_{j=1}^{m}}_{U}\bigl(\otimes_{j=1}^{m}\mathcal{F}^{\{D_{r_{i}}(z_{i})\}_{i\in I_{j}}}_{D_{R_{j}}(w_{j})}\bigl(\otimes_{i\in I_{j}}(\mathcal{F}^{D_{r_{i}}(z_{i})}_{\mathbb{C}})^{-1}f(a_{i})\bigr)\bigr).

Since the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\otimes_{i\in I_{j}}\mathcal{F}(D_{r_{i}}(z_{i}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}(D_{R_{j}}(w_{j}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}(\mathbb{C})^{\otimes I_{j}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}(\mathbb{C})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}^{\{D_{r_{i}}(z_{i})\}_{i\in I_{j}}}_{D_{R_{j}}(w_{j})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\otimes_{i\in I_{j}}\mathcal{F}^{D_{r_{i}}(z_{i})}_{\mathbb{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}^{\{D_{r_{i}}(z_{i})\}_{i\in I_{j}}}_{\mathbb{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}^{D_{R_{j}}(w_{j})}_{\mathbb{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{I_{j}}}

commutes for each
j\in[m]
, where
\mu_{I_{j}}
denotes the linear map defined in
Definition

2.1.1
, we have by
Remark

2.1.3
,

\theta^{\prime}_{A,\mathcal{F}}(f)^{L}_{U}(a)=\mathcal{F}^{\{D_{R_{j}}(w_{j})\}_{j=1}^{m}}_{U}\bigl(\otimes_{j=1}^{m}(\mathcal{F}^{D_{R_{j}}(w_{j})}_{\mathbb{C}})^{-1}(\prod_{i\in I_{j}}f(a_{i}))\bigr).

On the other hand, we have

\theta_{A,\mathcal{F}}^{\prime}(f)^{M}_{U}(F_{A}^{\mathrm{loc}})^{L}_{M}(a)=\mathcal{F}^{\{D_{R_{j}}(w_{j})\}_{j=1}^{m}}_{U}\bigl(\otimes_{j=1}^{m}(\mathcal{F}^{D_{R_{j}}(w_{j})}_{\mathbb{C}})^{-1}f(\prod_{i\in I_{j}}a_{i})\bigr).

Thus, the lemma follows.
∎

Let
f\colon A\to\mathcal{F}(\mathbb{C})
be a morphism in
\mathsf{CAlg}_{\mathbb{C}}
. By
Lemma

2.3.5
, for an open subset
U\subset\mathbb{C}
, the universality of
\mathbf{F}_{A}^{\mathrm{loc}}(U)=\varinjlim_{L\in\mathfrak{B}(U)}F_{A}^{\mathrm{loc}}(L)
yields a unique linear map
\theta^{\prime}_{A,\mathcal{F}}(f)_{U}\colon\mathbf{F}_{A}^{\mathrm{loc}}(U)\to\mathcal{F}(U)
which satisfies

\theta^{\prime}_{A,\mathcal{F}}(f)_{U}\circ(\widetilde{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{A})^{L}_{U}=\theta^{\prime}_{A,\mathcal{F}}(f)^{L}_{U}

for all
L\in\mathfrak{B}(U)
.

Lemma 2.3.6
.

For a morphism
f\colon A\to\mathcal{F}(\mathbb{C})
in
\mathsf{CAlg}_{\mathbb{C}}
,

\theta^{\prime}_{A,\mathcal{F}}(f)\coloneqq\{\theta^{\prime}_{A,\mathcal{F}}(f)_{U}\}_{U\in\mathfrak{U}_{\mathbb{C}}}\colon\mathbf{F}_{A}^{\mathrm{loc}}\to\mathcal{F}

is a morphism in
\mathsf{PFA}^{\mathsf{loc}}(\mathbb{C})
.

Proof.

We proceed the following steps:

•

\theta^{\prime}_{A,\mathcal{F}}(f)
is a morphism of precosheaves:
Let
U,V\subset\mathbb{C}
be open subsets such that
U\subset V
. For
L=D_{r_{1}(z_{1})}\sqcup\cdots\sqcup D_{r_{l}}(z_{l})\in\mathfrak{B}(U)
and
a=a_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{l}\in F_{A}^{\mathrm{loc}}(L)
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{U}_{V}\theta^{\prime}_{A,\mathcal{F}}(f)_{U}(\widetilde{F}_{A}^{\mathrm{loc}})^{L}_{U}(a)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}^{\{D_{r_{i}}(z_{i})\}_{i=1}^{l}}_{V}\bigl(\otimes_{i=1}^{l}(\mathcal{F}^{D_{r_{i}}(z_{i})}_{\mathbb{C}})^{-1}f(a_{i})\bigr)

\displaystyle=\theta^{\prime}_{A,\mathcal{F}}(f)^{L}_{U}(a)

\displaystyle=\theta^{\prime}_{A,\mathcal{F}}(f)_{V}(\mathbf{F}_{A}^{\mathrm{loc}})^{U}_{V}(\widetilde{F}_{A}^{\mathrm{loc}})^{L}_{U}(a).

Thus, we have
\mathcal{F}^{U}_{V}\circ\theta^{\prime}_{A,\mathcal{F}}(f)_{U}=\theta^{\prime}_{A,\mathcal{F}}(f)_{V}\circ(\mathbf{F}_{A}^{\mathrm{loc}})^{U}_{V}
.

•

\theta^{\prime}_{A,\mathcal{F}}(f)
is a morphism of prefactorization algebras:
Let
U,V,W\subset\mathbb{C}
be open subsets such that
U\sqcup V\subset W
. For
L=D_{r_{1}}(z_{1})\sqcup\cdots\sqcup D_{r_{l}}(z_{l})\in\mathfrak{B}(U)
,
M=D_{s_{1}}(w_{1})\sqcup\cdots\sqcup D_{s_{m}}(w_{m})\in\mathfrak{B}(V)
and
a=a_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{l}\in F_{A}^{\mathrm{loc}}(L)
,
b=b_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes b_{m}\in F_{A}^{\mathrm{loc}}(M)
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{U,V}_{W}(\theta^{\prime}_{A,\mathcal{F}}(f)_{U}\otimes\theta^{\prime}_{A,\mathcal{F}}(f)_{V})((\widetilde{F}_{A}^{\mathrm{loc}})^{L}_{U}\otimes(\widetilde{F}_{A}^{\mathrm{loc}})^{M}_{V})(a\otimes b)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}^{\{D_{r_{i}}(z_{i}),D_{s_{j}}(w_{j})\}_{i=1,j=1}^{l,m}}_{W}\bigl(\otimes_{i=1}^{l}(\mathcal{F}^{D_{r_{i}}(z_{i})}_{\mathbb{C}})^{-1}(a_{i})\otimes\otimes_{j=1}^{m}(\mathcal{F}^{D_{s_{j}}(w_{j})}_{\mathbb{C}})^{-1}(b_{j})\bigr)

\displaystyle=\theta^{\prime}_{A,\mathcal{F}}(f)^{L\sqcup M}_{W}(a\otimes b)

\displaystyle=\theta^{\prime}_{A,\mathcal{F}}(f)_{W}(\mathbf{F}_{A}^{\mathrm{loc}})^{U,V}_{W}((\widetilde{F}_{A}^{\mathrm{loc}})^{L}_{U}\otimes(\widetilde{F}_{A}^{\mathrm{loc}})^{M}_{V})(a\otimes b).

Thus, we have
\mathcal{F}^{U,V}_{W}\circ(\theta^{\prime}_{A,\mathcal{F}}(f)_{U}\otimes\theta^{\prime}_{A,\mathcal{F}}(f)_{V})=\theta^{\prime}_{A,\mathcal{F}}(f)_{W}\circ(\mathbf{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{A})^{U,V}_{W}
. Also, we have

\theta^{\prime}_{A,\mathcal{F}}(f)_{\varnothing}(1_{\mathbf{F}_{A}^{\mathrm{loc}}})=\theta^{\prime}_{A,\mathcal{F}}(f)^{\varnothing}_{\varnothing}(1_{\mathbb{C}})=1_{\mathcal{F}}.\qed

By
Lemma

2.3.6
, for
A\in\Ob(\mathsf{CAlg}_{\mathbb{C}})
and
\mathcal{F}\in\Ob(\mathsf{PFA}^{\mathsf{loc}}(\mathbb{C}))
, we have a map

\theta_{A,\mathcal{F}}^{\prime}\colon\Hom_{\mathsf{CAlg}_{\mathbb{C}}}(A,\mathcal{F}(\mathbb{C}))\to\Hom_{\mathsf{PFA}^{\mathsf{loc}}(\mathbb{C})}(\mathbf{F}_{A}^{\mathrm{loc}},\mathcal{F}),\quad f\mapsto\theta_{A,\mathcal{F}}^{\prime}(f).

Lemma 2.3.7
.

For
A\in\Ob(\mathsf{CAlg}_{\mathbb{C}})
and
\mathcal{F}\in\Ob(\mathsf{PFA}^{\mathsf{loc}}(\mathbb{C}))
, the map
\theta^{\prime}_{A,\mathcal{F}}
is the inverse of
\theta_{A,\mathcal{F}}
.

Proof.

Let
\varphi\colon\mathbf{F}_{A}^{\mathrm{loc}}\to\mathcal{F}
be a morphism in
\mathsf{PFA}^{\mathsf{loc}}(\mathbb{C})
. For an open subset
U\subset\mathbb{C}
,
L=D_{r_{1}}(z_{1})\sqcup\cdots\sqcup D_{r_{l}}(z_{l})\in\mathfrak{B}(U)
and
a=a_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{l}\in F_{A}^{\mathrm{loc}}(L)
,

\displaystyle\theta^{\prime}_{A,\mathcal{F}}(\theta_{A,\mathcal{F}}(\varphi))_{U}(\widetilde{F}_{A}^{\mathrm{loc}})^{L}_{U}(a)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}^{\{D_{r_{i}}(z_{i})\}_{i=1}^{l}}_{U}\bigl(\otimes_{i=1}^{l}(\mathcal{F}^{D_{r_{i}}(z_{i})}_{\mathbb{C}})^{-1}\varphi_{\mathbb{C}}(\widetilde{F}_{A}^{\mathrm{loc}})^{D_{r_{i}}(z_{i})}_{\mathbb{C}}(a_{i})\bigr)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}^{\{D_{r_{i}}(z_{i})\}_{i=1}^{l}}_{U}\bigl(\otimes_{i=1}^{l}\varphi_{D_{r_{i}}(z_{i})}((\mathbf{F}_{A}^{\mathrm{loc}})^{D_{r_{i}}(z_{i})}_{\mathbb{C}})^{-1}(\widetilde{F}_{A}^{\mathrm{loc}})^{D_{r_{i}}(z_{i})}_{\mathbb{C}}(a_{i})\bigr)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}^{\{D_{r_{i}}(z_{i})\}_{i=1}^{l}}_{U}\bigl(\otimes_{i=1}^{l}\varphi_{D_{r_{i}}(z_{i})}(\widetilde{F}_{A}^{\mathrm{loc}})^{D_{r_{i}}(z_{i})}_{D_{r_{i}}(z_{i})}(a_{i})\bigr)

\displaystyle=\varphi_{U}(\mathbf{F}_{A}^{\mathrm{loc}})^{\{D_{r_{i}}(z_{i})\}_{i=1}^{l}}_{U}\bigl(\otimes_{i=1}^{l}(\widetilde{F}_{A}^{\mathrm{loc}})^{D_{r_{i}}(z_{i})}_{D_{r_{i}}(z_{i})}(a_{i})\bigr)

\displaystyle=\varphi_{U}(\widetilde{F}_{A}^{\mathrm{loc}})^{L}_{U}(a).

Thus, we have
\theta^{\prime}_{A,\mathcal{F}}\theta_{A,\mathcal{F}}(\varphi)=\varphi
.

Let
f\colon A\to\mathcal{F}(\mathbb{C})
be a morphism in
\mathsf{CAlg}_{\mathbb{C}}
. For
a\in A
, take some
R\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
, then

\theta_{A,\mathcal{F}}(\theta^{\prime}_{A,\mathcal{F}}(f))(a)=\theta^{\prime}_{A,\mathcal{F}}(f)_{\mathbb{C}}(\widetilde{F}_{A}^{\mathrm{loc}})^{D_{R}(0)}_{\mathbb{C}}(a)=\mathcal{F}^{D_{R}(0)}_{\mathbb{C}}=f(a).

Thus, we have
\theta_{A,\mathcal{F}}\theta^{\prime}_{A,\mathcal{F}}(f)=f
.
∎

By
Lemma

2.3.7
, the proof of
Proposition

2.3.4
is complete.

Now, let us denote by

\eta_{A}\colon A\to\mathbf{F}_{A}^{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{C})\quad(A\in\Ob(\mathsf{CAlg}_{\mathbb{C}})),\quad\epsilon_{\mathcal{F}}\colon\mathbf{F}_{\mathcal{F}(\mathbb{C})}^{\mathrm{loc}}\to\mathcal{F}\quad(\mathcal{F}\in\Ob(\mathsf{PFA}^{\mathsf{loc}}(\mathbb{C})))

the unit and counit of the adjoint functors
\mathbf{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}\colon\mathsf{CAlg}_{\mathbb{C}}\rightleftarrows\mathsf{PFA}^{\mathsf{loc}}(\mathbb{C})\colon(-)(\mathbb{C})
.

Proposition 2.3.8
.

The functor
\mathbf{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}\colon\mathsf{CAlg}_{\mathbb{C}}\to\mathsf{PFA}^{\mathsf{loc}}(\mathbb{C})
is fully faithful.

Proof.

For
A\in\Ob(\mathsf{CAlg}_{\mathbb{C}})
, the morphism
\eta_{A}
is equal to
(\widetilde{F}_{A}^{\mathrm{loc}})^{D_{R}(z)}_{\mathbb{C}}=(\mathbf{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{A})^{D_{R}(z)}_{\mathbb{C}}\circ(\widetilde{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{A})^{D_{R}(z)}_{D_{R}(z)}
. Thus, the proposition follows from
Lemma

2.3.2
.
∎

Lemma 2.3.9
.

If
\mathcal{F}\in\Ob(\mathsf{PFA}^{\mathsf{loc}}(\mathbb{C}))
is a factorization algebra, then the counit
\epsilon_{\mathcal{F}}\colon\mathbf{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{\mathcal{F}(\mathbb{C})}\to\mathcal{F}
is an isomorphism in
\mathsf{PFA}^{\mathsf{loc}}(\mathbb{C})
.

Proof.

It is enough to show that
\epsilon_{\mathcal{F},U}\colon\mathbf{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{\mathcal{F}(\mathbb{C})}\to\mathcal{F}(U)
is a linear isomorphism for all open subsets
U\subset\mathbb{C}
. We proceed the following steps:

•

For a connected open subset
U\subset\mathbb{C}
, the linear map
\epsilon_{\mathcal{F},U}
is an isomorphism:
Take
R\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
and
z\in\mathbb{C}
such that
D_{R}(z)\subset U
, then we have

\epsilon_{\mathcal{F},U}\circ(\widetilde{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{\mathcal{F}(\mathbb{C})})^{D_{R}(z)}_{U}=\theta^{\prime}_{\mathcal{F}(\mathbb{C}),\mathcal{F}}(\id_{\mathcal{F}(\mathbb{C})})^{D_{R}(z)}_{U}

Thus, it is sufficient to check that both
(\widetilde{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{\mathcal{F}(\mathbb{C})})^{D_{R}(z)}_{U}
and
\theta^{\prime}_{\mathcal{F}(\mathbb{C}),\mathcal{F}}(\id_{\mathcal{F}(\mathbb{C})})^{D_{R}(z)}_{U}
are isomorphisms, but this follows from
Lemma

1.5.4
.

•

For an open subset
U\subset\mathbb{C}
which has finite many connected components, the linear map
\epsilon_{\mathcal{F},U}
is an isomorphism: In fact, if
U=U_{1}\sqcup\cdots\sqcup U_{l}
is the decomposition into connected components, then

\epsilon_{\mathcal{F},U}\circ(\mathbf{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{\mathcal{F}(\mathbb{C})})^{\{U_{i}\}_{i=1}^{l}}_{U}=\mathcal{F}^{\{U_{i}\}_{i=1}^{l}}_{U}\circ(\otimes_{i=1}^{l}\epsilon_{\mathcal{F},U_{i}}).

•

For arbitrary open subset
U\subset\mathbb{C}
, the linear map
\epsilon_{\mathcal{F},U}
is an isomorphism: First, note that
\mathfrak{B}(U)
is a Weiss cover of
U
. To construct the inverse map of
\epsilon_{\mathcal{F},U}
, we use the coequalizer diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle\bigoplus_{L,M\in\mathfrak{B}(U)}\mathcal{F}(L\cap M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle\bigoplus_{L\in\mathfrak{B}(U)}\mathcal{F}(L)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}(U),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}

where
p,q,\pi
are linear maps defined as in (
1.2.1
). Let
f\colon\bigoplus_{L\in\mathfrak{B}(U)}\mathcal{F}(L)\to\mathbf{F}_{\mathcal{F}(\mathbb{C})}^{\mathrm{loc}}(U)
be the linear map which commutes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}(L)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigoplus_{L\in\mathfrak{B}(U)}\mathcal{F}(L)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{\mathcal{F}(\mathbb{C})}(L)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{\mathcal{F}(\mathbb{C})}(U)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon_{\mathcal{F},L}^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathbf{F}_{\mathcal{F}(\mathbb{C})}^{\mathrm{loc}})^{L}_{U}}

then one can check
f\circ p=f\circ q
since for
L,M\in\mathfrak{B}(U)
, the intersection
L\cap M
has finite many connected components. Thus, by the universality of the coequalizer
\mathcal{F}(U)
, there exists a unique linear map
\widetilde{f}\colon\mathcal{F}(U)\to\mathbf{F}_{\mathcal{F}(\mathbb{C})}^{\mathrm{loc}}(U)
such that
\widetilde{f}\circ\pi=f
. We find that
\widetilde{f}
is the inverse map of
\epsilon_{\mathcal{F},U}
.
∎

Proposition 2.3.10
.

The functors

\mathbf{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}\colon\mathsf{CAlg}_{\mathbb{C}}\rightleftarrows\mathsf{FA}^{\mathsf{loc}}(\mathbb{C})\colon(-)(\mathbb{C})

give categorical equivalences.

Proof.

This follows from
Proposition

2.3.4
,
Proposition

2.3.8
and
Lemma

2.3.9
.
∎

3.
Constructions of vertex algebras and factorization algebras

We continue to work over the field
\mathbb{C}
of complex numbers, so that all linear spaces and linear maps defined over
\mathbb{C}
unless otherwise stated.

3.1.
Preliminaries on vertex algebras

In this subsection we recall the definition of vertex algebras. We refer to
[
FBZ04
]
for details on the theory of vertex algebras.

Let
V
be a linear space. A formal series

a(z)=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}z^{n}a_{n}\in(\End_{\mathbb{C}}V)\llbracket z^{\pm 1}\rrbracket

is called a field on
V
if
a(z)v\in V(\!(z)\!)
for each
v\in V
. We denote by
\Fld V
the linear space consisting of all fields on
V
.

Definition 3.1.1

(
[
FBZ04
, §1.3.1]
)
.

A
vertex algebra
is a tuple
(V,\ket{0},T,Y)
consisting of

•

a linear space
V
, called the state space,

•

an element
\ket{0}\in V
, called the vacuum,

•

a linear map
T\colon V\to V
, called the translation operator,

•

a linear map

Y\colon V\to\Fld V,\quad a\mapsto Y(a,z)=\sum_{z\in\mathbb{Z}}z^{-n-1}a_{(n)},

called the state-field correspondence,

which satisfies following axioms:

1

(Vacuum axiom)
For
a\in V
, we have
Y(\ket{0},z)=\id_{V}
. Also, for
a\in V
, we have
Y(a,z)\ket{0}\in V\llbracket z\rrbracket
and
Y(a,z)\ket{0}|_{z=0}=a
.

2

(Translation invariance)
We have
T\ket{0}=0
and
[T,Y(a,z)]=\partial_{z}Y(a,z)
for
a\in V
.

3

(Locality)
For
a,b\in V
, there exists
N\in\mathbb{N}
such that

(z-w)^{N}[Y(a,z),Y(b,w)]=0

in
(\End_{\mathbb{C}}V)\llbracket z^{\pm 1},w^{\pm 1}\rrbracket
.

Definition 3.1.2

(
[
FBZ04
, Definition 1.3.4]
)
.

Let
V
and
W
be vertex algebras. A linear map
f\colon V\to W
is called a
morphism of vertex algebras
if

f(\ket{0}_{V})=\ket{0}_{W},\quad f\circ T_{V}=T_{W}\circ f,\quad f(Y_{V}(a,z)b)=Y_{W}(f(a),z)f(b)\quad(a,b\in V).

Example 3.1.3

(
[
FBZ04
, §1.4]
)
.

A vertex algebra
V
is called commutative if
[Y(a,z),Y(b,w)]=0
for all
a,b\in V
. This condition is equivalent to
a_{(n)}=0
(as an element of
\End_{\mathbb{C}}V
) for all
a\in V
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

For a commutative vertex algebra
V
,

\mu\colon V\times V\to V,\quad(a,b)\mapsto a_{(-1)}b

is a commutative and associative multiplication, and the translation operator
T
is a derivation with respect to
\mu
. Hence we obtain a commutative differential algebra
(V,\mu,T)
with the unit
\ket{0}
.
Conversely, for a commutative differential algebra
(A,\cdot,\partial)
with the unit
1_{A}
, let

Y\colon A\otimes A\to A\llbracket z\rrbracket,\quad a\otimes b\mapsto Y(a,z)b\coloneqq\sum_{n\geq 0}\frac{z^{n}}{n!}(\partial^{n}a)\cdot b,

then
(A,1_{A},\partial,Y)
is a commutative vertex algebra. Thus, the notion of commutative vertex algebras is equivalent to that of commutative differential algebras. In what follows, we does not distinguish commutative vertex algebras and commutative differential algebras.

Definition 3.1.4
.

A vertex algebra
V=\bigoplus_{\Delta\in\mathbb{Z}}V_{\Delta}
with a direct sum decomposition as a linear space is called a
\mathbb{Z}
-graded vertex algebra
if the following conditions hold:

1

\ket{0}\in V_{0}
.

2

For
a\in V_{\Delta}
,
b\in V_{\Delta^{\prime}}
and
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, we have
a_{(n)}b\in V_{\Delta+\Delta^{\prime}-n-1}
.

Let
V=\bigoplus_{\Delta\in\mathbb{Z}}V_{\Delta}
be a
\mathbb{Z}
-graded vertex algebras. For
a\in V_{\Delta}
, we say that
a
is a homogeneous element of
V
, and denote
\Delta(a)\coloneqq\Delta
.

Definition 3.1.5
.

Let
V=\bigoplus_{\Delta\in\mathbb{Z}}V_{\Delta}
and
W=\bigoplus_{\Delta\in\mathbb{Z}}W_{\Delta}
be
\mathbb{Z}
-graded vertex algebras. A morphism
f\colon V\to W
of vertex algebras is called a
morphism of
\mathbb{Z}
-graded vertex algebras
if
f(V_{\Delta})\subset W_{\Delta}
for all
\Delta\in\mathbb{Z}
.

We denote by
\mathsf{grComVA}^{\mathsf{bb}}
the category whose objects are commutative
\mathbb{Z}
-graded vertex algebras
V=\bigoplus_{\Delta\in\mathbb{Z}}V_{\Delta}
such that
V_{\Delta}=0
for
\Delta\ll 0
, and morphisms are the morphisms of
\mathbb{Z}
-graded vertex algebras. Also, we denote by
\mathsf{grComVA}^{\mathsf{bb},\mathsf{fin}}
the full subcategory of
\mathsf{grComVA}^{\mathsf{bb}}
consisting of
V=\bigoplus_{\Delta\in\mathbb{Z}}V_{\Delta}\in\Ob(\mathsf{grComVA}^{\mathsf{bb}})
such that
\dim_{\mathbb{C}}V_{\Delta}<\infty
for all
\Delta\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Example 3.1.6
.

It is known that the forgetful functor
\mathsf{grComVA}^{\mathsf{bb}}\to\mathsf{CAlg}
has the left adjoint
\mathcal{J}\colon\mathsf{CAlg}\to\mathsf{grComVA}^{\mathsf{bb}}
. For a commutative algebra
A
, the differential algebra
\mathcal{J}\!A
is called the jet algebra of
A
.

For example, let us describe the construction of the jet algebra of a polynomial ring
A=\mathbb{C}[x_{\lambda}]_{\lambda\in\Lambda}
. As a commutative algebra, the jet algebra is just a polynomial ring
\mathcal{J}\!A\coloneqq\mathbb{C}[x_{\lambda}^{(m)}]_{\lambda\in\Lambda,m\in\mathbb{N}}
of infinite many variables. Define a derivation
\partial\colon\mathcal{J}\!A\to\mathcal{J}\!A
by

\partial(x_{\lambda_{1}}^{(m_{1})}\cdots x_{\lambda_{l}}^{(m_{l})})\coloneqq\sum_{i=1}^{l}x_{\lambda_{1}}^{(m_{1})}\cdots x_{\lambda_{i}}^{(m_{i}+1)}\cdots x_{\lambda_{l}}^{(m_{l})}.

Also, for
\Delta\in\mathbb{N}
, define

(\mathcal{J}\!A)_{\Delta}\coloneqq\Spn_{\mathbb{C}}\bigl\{x_{\lambda_{1}}^{(m_{1})}\cdots x_{\lambda_{l}}^{(m_{l})}\mid l\in\mathbb{N},\lambda_{i}\in\Lambda,m_{i}\in\mathbb{N},\,\sum_{i=1}^{l}(m_{i}+1)=\Delta\bigr\}.

Then, we have
\mathcal{J}\!A=\bigoplus_{\Delta\in\mathbb{N}}(\mathcal{J}\!A)_{\Delta}\in\Ob(\mathsf{grComVA}^{\mathsf{bb}})
. Notice that if
A
is a polynomial ring of finite many variables, then
\mathcal{J}\!A\in\Ob(\mathsf{grComVA}^{\mathsf{bb},\mathsf{fin}})
.
This holds in general, i.e., we have
\mathcal{J}\!A\in\Ob(\mathsf{grComVA}^{\mathsf{bb},\mathsf{fin}})
for any finitely generated commutative algebra
A
.

3.2.
A general construction of vertex algebras from prefactorization algebras

In this subsection we construct a vertex algebra from a prefactorization algebra on the complex number plane
\mathbb{C}
called holomorphic (
Theorem

3.2.11
, see also
Definition

3.2.7
). We use the following notations:

•

We denote by
S^{1}\coloneqq\{q\in\mathbb{C}\mid|q|=1\}
the unit circle, and consider the natural action of the group
S^{1}\ltimes\mathbb{C}
of isometric affine transformations on the plane
\mathbb{C}
.

•

We regard
S^{1}\subset S^{1}\ltimes\mathbb{C}
by
q\mapsto(q,0)
.

We will consider factorization algebras with values in the category
\mathsf{LCS}_{\mathbb{C}}
of locally convex spaces over
\mathbb{C}
, whose morphisms are continuous linear maps, so we first remark on the categorical properties of
\mathsf{LCS}_{\mathbb{C}}
.

•

\mathsf{LCS}_{\mathbb{C}}
admits projective limits:
For a projective system
(\{X_{\lambda}\}_{\lambda\in\Lambda},\{f_{\lambda,\mu}\colon X_{\mu}\to X_{\lambda}\}_{\lambda\leq\mu})
in
\mathsf{LCS}_{\mathbb{C}}
, the projective limit
\varprojlim X_{\lambda}
in
\mathsf{Mod}\,\mathbb{C}
equipped with the initial topology induced by the canonical morphisms
\varprojlim_{\lambda\in\lambda}X_{\lambda}\to X_{\lambda}
is a locally convex space. Thus, the linear space
\varprojlim_{\lambda\in\Lambda}X_{\lambda}
gives rise to the projective limit in
\mathsf{LCS}_{\mathbb{C}}
.

•

\mathsf{LCS}_{\mathbb{C}}
admits kernels:
For a continuous linear map
f\colon X\to Y
, the linear space
\Ker f=\{x\in X\mid f(x)=0\}\subset X
equipped with the subspace topology is a locally convex space. Thus, the linear space
\Ker f
gives rise to the kernel in
\mathsf{LCS}_{\mathbb{C}}
.

•

\mathsf{LCS}_{\mathbb{C}}
admits inductive limits:
For a inductive system
(\{X_{\lambda}\}_{\lambda\in\Lambda},\{f_{\lambda,\mu}\colon X_{\lambda}\to X_{\mu}\}_{\lambda\leq\mu})
in
\mathsf{LCS}_{\mathbb{C}}
, the inductive limit
\varinjlim_{\lambda\in\Lambda}X_{\lambda}
in
\mathsf{Mod}\,\mathbb{C}
has the structure of a locally convex space as follows: Let
\mathfrak{B}
be the set consisting of balanced absorbent convex set
A\subset\varinjlim_{\lambda\in\Lambda}X_{\lambda}
such that
\iota_{\lambda}^{-1}(A)\subset X_{\lambda}
is a neighborhood of
0\in X_{\lambda}
for all
\lambda\in\Lambda
. Here
\iota_{\lambda}\colon X_{\lambda}\to\varinjlim_{\lambda\in\Lambda}X_{\lambda}
denotes the canonical morphism of the inductive limit. Then, there exists a unique locally convex topology on
\varinjlim_{\lambda\in\Lambda}X_{\lambda}
such that
\mathfrak{B}
is a neighborhood basis at
0\in\varinjlim_{\lambda\in\Lambda}X_{\lambda}
. The linear space
\varinjlim_{\lambda\in\Lambda}X_{\lambda}
equipped with this locally convex topology gives the inductive limit in
\mathsf{LCS}_{\mathbb{C}}
.

•

\mathsf{LCS}_{\mathbb{C}}
admits cokernels:
For a continuous linear map
f\colon X\to Y
, the linear space
\Cok f=Y/f(X)
equipped with the quotient topology is a locally convex space. Thus, the linear space
\Cok f
gives rise to the cokernel in
\mathsf{LCS}_{\mathbb{C}}
.

•

\mathsf{LCS}_{\mathbb{C}}
admits a symmetric monoidal structure:
For locally convex spaces
X
and
Y
, the algebraic tensor product
X\otimes Y
has the structure of a locally convex space as follows: Let
\mathfrak{B}
be the set consisting of balanced absorbent convex set
A\subset X\otimes Y
such that
\otimes^{-1}(A)\subset X\times Y
is a neighborhood of
(0,0)\in X\times Y
. Here
\otimes\colon X\times Y\to X\otimes Y
denotes the canonical bilinear map. Then, there exists a unique locally convex topology on
X\otimes Y
such that
\mathfrak{B}
is a neighborhood basis at
0\in X\otimes Y
. The linear space
X\otimes Y
equipped with this topology is called the projective tensor product, and denote by
X\otimes_{\pi}Y
. The topology of
X\otimes_{\pi}Y
is the finest locally convex topology on
X\otimes Y
such that
\otimes\colon X\times Y\to X\otimes Y
is continuous.
The category
\mathsf{LCS}_{\mathbb{C}}
has the structure of a symmetric monoidal category by letting
\otimes_{\pi}
be the tensor product and
\mathbb{C}
be the unit. In this note, we does not use any other locally convex topologies on
X\otimes Y
, so we just denote
\otimes=\otimes_{\pi}
.

In particular, the category
\mathsf{LCS}_{\mathbb{C}}
is a complete and cocomplete symmetric monoidal category.

Remark 3.2.1
.

For locally convex spaces
X
and
Y
, there is the finest locally convex topology on
X\otimes Y
such that
\otimes\colon X\times Y\to X\otimes Y
is separately continuous. The linear space
X\otimes Y
equipped with this topology is called the inductive tensor product, and denote by
X\otimes_{\iota}Y
. The inductive tensor product also give
\mathsf{LCS}_{\mathbb{C}}
a symmetric monoidal structure. This
§​

3.2
and next
§​

3.3
are valid if we adopt the inductive tensor product as a symmetric monoidal structure on
\mathsf{LCS}_{\mathbb{C}}
.

Let
\mathcal{F}\colon\mathfrak{U}_{\mathbb{C}}\to\mathsf{LCS}_{\mathbb{C}}
be an
S^{1}\ltimes\mathbb{C}
-equivariant prefactorization algebra. We denote its structure morphism by

\sigma_{(q,z),U}\colon\mathcal{F}(U)\to\mathcal{F}((q,z)U)\quad((q,z)\in S^{1}\ltimes\mathbb{C},U\in\mathfrak{U}_{\mathbb{C}}).

Notice that the costalk
\mathcal{F}^{z}
at
z\in\mathbb{C}
is given by

\mathcal{F}^{z}=\Bigl\{(a_{R})_{R\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}}\in\prod_{R\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}}\mathcal{F}(D_{R}(z))\mid\forall r,R\in\mathbb{R}_{>0},\ r<R\,\Longrightarrow\,\mathcal{F}^{D_{r}(z)}_{D_{R}(z)}(a_{r})=a_{R}\Bigr\}

since
\{D_{R}(z)\}_{R\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}}
is an open neighborhood basis of
z
which is closed under finite intersections.

Definition 3.2.2
.

For
R\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
and
(z_{1},\ldots,z_{l})\in\Conf_{l}(D_{R}(0))
, we define a morphism
\mu_{z_{1},\ldots,z_{l}}^{R}
of locally convex spaces as the composition

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathcal{F}^{0})^{\otimes l}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigotimes_{i=1}^{l}\mathcal{F}(D_{r}(0))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigotimes_{i=1}^{l}\mathcal{F}(D_{r}(z_{i}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}(D_{R}(0))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\otimes_{i=1}^{l}\mathcal{F}^{0}_{D_{r}(0)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\otimes_{i=1}^{l}\sigma_{(1,z_{i}),D_{r}(0)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}^{D_{r}(z_{1}),\ldots,D_{r}(z_{l})}_{D_{R}(0)}}

where
r
is any positive real number satisfying

D_{r}(z_{1})\sqcup\cdots\sqcup D_{r}(z_{l})\subset D_{R}(0).

Notice that the definition of
\mu_{z_{1},\ldots,z_{l}}^{R}
is independent of the choice of
r
because of the second part of
Definition

1.2.1
(i) and the equation
\mathcal{F}^{D_{r}(z)}_{D_{s}(z)}\circ\mathcal{F}^{z}_{D_{r}(z)}=\mathcal{F}^{z}_{D_{s}(z)}
for
0<r<s
.

Remark 3.2.3
.

(1)

When
l=1
, the morphism
\mu_{z}^{R}\colon\mathcal{F}^{0}\to\mathcal{F}(D_{R}(0))
is defined for
z\in D_{R}(0)
, and we have, for
a\in\mathcal{F}^{0}
,

\mu_{z}^{R}(a)=\mathcal{F}^{D_{r}(z)}_{D_{R}(0)}\sigma_{(1,z),D_{r}(0)}\mathcal{F}^{0}_{D_{r}(0)}(a),

where
r
is any positive real number satisfying
|z|<R-r
.

(2)

When
l=2
, the morphism
\mu_{z,0}^{R}\colon\mathcal{F}^{0}\otimes\mathcal{F}^{0}\to\mathcal{F}(D_{R}(0))
is defined for
z\in D_{R}^{\times}(0)\coloneqq D_{R}(0)\mathbin{\setminus}\{0\}
, and we have, for
a,b\in\mathcal{F}^{0}
,

\mu_{z,0}^{R}(a\otimes b)=\mathcal{F}^{D_{r}(z),D_{r}(0)}_{D_{R}(0)}\bigl(\sigma_{(1,z),D_{r}(0)}\mathcal{F}^{0}_{D_{r}(0)}(a)\otimes\mathcal{F}^{0}_{D_{r}(0)}(b)\bigr),

where
r
is any positive real number satisfying
2r<|z|<R-r
.

Definition 3.2.4
.

For
\Delta\in\mathbb{Z}
, define

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}(D_{R}(0))_{\Delta}\coloneqq\{a\in\mathcal{F}(D_{R}(0))\mid\forall q\in S^{1},\,\sigma_{q,D_{R}(0)}a=q^{\Delta}a\}\quad(R\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}),

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{0}_{\Delta}\coloneqq\Bigl\{(a_{R})_{R\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}}\in\prod_{R\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}}\mathcal{F}(D_{R}(0))_{\Delta}\mid\forall r,R\in\mathbb{R}_{>0},\ r<R\,\Longrightarrow\,\mathcal{F}^{D_{r}(0)}_{D_{R}(0)}(a_{r})=a_{R}\Bigr\}.

Note that
\sum_{\Delta\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathcal{F}(D_{r}(0))_{\Delta}\subset\mathcal{F}(D_{r}(0))
and
\sum_{\Delta\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathcal{F}^{0}_{\Delta}\subset\mathcal{F}^{0}
are direct sums. For
a\in\mathcal{F}^{0}_{\Delta}
, we say that
a
is a homogeneous element of weight
\Delta
, and denote
\Delta(a)\coloneqq\Delta
.

Remark 3.2.5
.

Let
0<r<R
and
\Delta\in\mathbb{Z}
. If
a\in\mathcal{F}(D_{r}(0))_{\Delta}
, then
\mathcal{F}^{D_{r}(0)}_{D_{R}(0)}(a)\in\mathcal{F}(D_{R}(0))_{\Delta}
, so we have a linear map

\mathcal{F}(D_{r}(0))_{\Delta}\to\mathcal{F}(D_{R}(0))_{\Delta},\quad a\mapsto\mathcal{F}^{D_{r}(0)}_{D_{R}(0)}(a).

We abusively denote this linear map by
\mathcal{F}^{D_{r}(0)}_{D_{R}(0)}\colon\mathcal{F}(D_{r}(0))_{\Delta}\to\mathcal{F}(D_{R}(0))_{\Delta}
.

Lemma 3.2.6
.

Let
R\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
and
(z_{1},\ldots,z_{l})\in\Conf_{l}(D_{R}(0))
. For
q\in S^{1}
and homogeneous
a_{1},\ldots,a_{l}\in\mathcal{F}^{0}
, we have

\sigma_{q,D_{R}(0)}\mu_{z_{1},\ldots,z_{l}}^{R}(a_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{l})=q^{\Delta(a_{1})+\cdots+\Delta(a_{l})}\mu_{qz_{1},\ldots,qz_{l}}^{R}(a_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{l}).

Proof.

Take
r\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
such that

D_{r}(z_{1})\sqcup\cdots\sqcup D_{r}(z_{l})\subset D_{R}(0),

then

\sigma_{q,D_{R}(0)}\mu_{z_{1},\ldots,z_{l}}^{R}(a_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{l})=\mathcal{F}^{\{D_{r}(qz_{i})\}_{i=1}^{l}}_{D_{R}(0)}\bigl(\otimes_{i=1}^{l}\sigma_{q(1,z_{i}),D_{r}(0)}\mathcal{F}^{0}_{D_{r}(0)}(a_{i})\bigr).

Since
q(1,z)=(1,qz)q
in
S^{1}\ltimes\mathbb{C}
and
qD_{r}(0)=D_{r}(0)
, we have

\displaystyle\sigma_{q,D_{R}(0)}\mu_{z_{1},\ldots,z_{l}}^{R}(a_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{l})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}^{\{D_{r}(qz_{i})\}_{i=1}^{l}}_{D_{R}(0)}\bigl(\otimes_{i=1}^{l}\sigma_{(1,qz_{i}),D_{r}(0)}\sigma_{q,D_{r}(0)}\mathcal{F}^{0}_{D_{r}(0)}(a_{i})\bigr)

\displaystyle=q^{\Delta(a_{1})+\cdots+\Delta(a_{l})}\mu_{qz_{1},\ldots,qz_{l}}(a_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{l}).\qed

The following definition is motivated by the assumptions in
[
CG17
, Theorem 5.3.3]
. Notice that the discreteness condition (see condition (iii) in
[
CG17
, Theorem 5.3.3]
and the first paragraph of
[
B20a
, §1.3]
) on the weight spaces
\mathcal{F}(D_{R}(0))_{\Delta}
does not required.

Definition 3.2.7
.

An
S^{1}\ltimes\mathbb{C}
-equivalent prefactorization algebra
\mathcal{F}\colon\mathfrak{U}_{\mathbb{C}}\to\mathsf{LCS}_{\mathbb{C}}
is called a holomorphic prefactorization algebra if it satisfies the following conditions:

1

For
0<r<R
and
\Delta\in\mathbb{Z}
, the map
\mathcal{F}^{D_{r}(0)}_{D_{R}(0)}\colon\mathcal{F}(D_{r}(0))_{\Delta}\to\mathcal{F}(D_{R}(0))_{\Delta}
is a linear isomorphism.

2

For
R\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
, we have
\mathcal{F}(D_{R}(0))_{\Delta}=0
(
\Delta\ll 0
).

3

For
R\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
, the locally convex space
\mathcal{F}(D_{R}(0))
is quasi-complete and Hausdorff.

4

For
R\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
and homogeneous
a_{1},\ldots,a_{l}\in\mathcal{F}^{0}
, the function

\Conf_{l}(D_{R}(0))\to\mathcal{F}(D_{R}(0)),\quad(z_{1},\ldots,z_{l})\mapsto\mu^{R}_{z_{1},\ldots,z_{l}}(a_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{l})

is holomorphic in the sense of
Definition

A.1.3
.

We denote by
\mathsf{HolPFA}_{\mathbb{C}}
the category whose objects are holomorphic prefactorization algebras, and morphisms are the morphisms of
S^{1}\ltimes\mathbb{C}
-equivariant prefactorization algebras. Also, we denote by
\mathsf{HolFA}_{\mathbb{C}}
the full sub-category of
\mathsf{HolPFA}_{\mathbb{C}}
consisting of holomorphic factorization algebras.

Remark 3.2.8
.

Let
\mathcal{F}\colon\mathfrak{U}_{\mathbb{C}}\to\mathsf{LCS}_{\mathbb{C}}
be a holomorphic prefactorization algebra.
For
R\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
and
\Delta\in\mathbb{Z}
, notice that

\mathcal{F}^{0}_{D_{R}(0)}\colon\mathcal{F}^{0}_{\Delta}\to\mathcal{F}(D_{R}(0))_{\Delta}

is a linear isomorphism because of (i) in
Definition

3.2.7
.

In the remaining of this subsection, let
\mathcal{F}\colon\mathfrak{U}_{\mathbb{C}}\to\mathsf{LCS}_{\mathbb{C}}
be a holomorphic prefactorization algebra. We will construct the structure of a vertex algebra on
\mathbf{V}(\mathcal{F})\coloneqq\bigoplus_{\Delta\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathcal{F}^{0}_{\Delta}
.

First, we define

(3.2.1)

\ket{0}=\ket{0}_{\mathcal{F}}\coloneqq\{\mathcal{F}^{\varnothing}_{D_{R}(0)}(1_{\mathcal{F}})\}_{R\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}}\in\mathcal{F}^{0},

where
\mathbb{C}\to\mathcal{F}(\varnothing)
,
1\mapsto 1_{\mathcal{F}}
denotes the unit. Since

\sigma_{q,D_{R}(0)}\mathcal{F}^{\varnothing}_{D_{R}(0)}(1_{\mathcal{F}})=\mathcal{F}^{\varnothing}_{D_{R}(0)}\sigma_{q,\varnothing}(1_{\mathcal{F}})=\mathcal{F}^{\varnothing}_{D_{R}(0)}(1_{\mathcal{F}})

for all
q\in S^{1}
and
r\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
, we have
\Delta(\ket{0})=0
. In particular
\ket{0}\in\mathbf{V}(\mathcal{F})
.

Next, we define a linear map
T\colon\mathbf{V}(\mathcal{F})\to\mathbf{V}(\mathcal{F})
:
Let
a\in\mathcal{F}^{0}
be homogeneous. For
R\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
, by
Definition

3.2.7
(iv), the function

D_{R}(0)\to\mathcal{F}(D_{R}(0)),\quad z\mapsto\mu_{z}^{R}(a).

is holomorphic.
Thus, we have

(Ta)_{R}\coloneqq\partial_{z}\mu_{z}^{R}(a)|_{z=0}\in\mathcal{F}(D_{R}(0)).

Lemma 3.2.9
.

Let
a\in\mathcal{F}^{0}
be homogeneous.

(1)

For
R\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
, we have
(Ta)_{R}\in\mathcal{F}(D_{R}(0))_{\Delta(a)+1}
.

(2)

For
0<r<R
, we have
\mathcal{F}^{D_{r}(0)}_{D_{R}(0)}(Ta)_{r}=(Ta)_{R}
.

Proof.

(1) For
q\in S^{1}
, we have

\sigma_{q,D_{R}(0)}(Ta)_{R}=\partial_{z}\sigma_{q,D_{R}(0)}\mu_{z}^{R}(a)|_{z=0}=q^{\Delta(a)}\partial_{z}\mu_{qz}^{R}(a)|_{z=0}=q^{\Delta(a)+1}(Ta)_{R},

where we used
Lemma

3.2.6
in the second equality and
Lemma

A.1.10
in the third equality.

(2) For
z\in D_{r}(0)
, since
\mathcal{F}^{D_{r}(0)}_{D_{R}(0)}\mu_{z}^{r}(a)=\mu_{z}^{R}(a)
, we have

(Ta)_{R}=\partial_{z}\mu_{z}^{R}(a)|_{z=0}=\mathcal{F}^{D_{r}(0)}_{D_{R}(0)}\partial_{z}\mu_{z}^{r}(a)|_{z=0}=\mathcal{F}^{D_{r}(0)}_{D_{R}(0)}(Ta)_{r}.\qed

For homogeneous
a\in\mathcal{F}^{0}
, by
Lemma

3.2.9
, one can define

Ta\coloneqq\{(Ta)_{R}\}_{R\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}}\in\mathcal{F}^{0}_{\Delta(a)+1}.

Now, we have a linear map

(3.2.2)

T\colon\mathbf{V}(\mathcal{F})\to\mathbf{V}(\mathcal{F}),\quad a\mapsto Ta.

Finally, we define a linear map
Y\colon\mathbf{V}(\mathcal{F})\otimes\mathbf{V}(\mathcal{F})\to\mathbf{V}(\mathcal{F})(\!(z)\!)
:
Let
a,b\in\mathcal{F}^{0}
be homogeneous.
For
R\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
, we have a holomorphic function

D_{R}(0)^{\times}\to\mathcal{F}(D_{R}(0)),\quad z\mapsto\mu_{z,0}^{R}(a\otimes b).

Thus, by
Theorem

A.5.4
, the function
\mu_{z,0}^{R}(a\otimes b)
can be expanded in the form of Laurent series

\mu_{z,0}^{R}(a\otimes b)=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}z^{-n-1}(a_{(n)}b)_{R}\quad(z\in D_{R}(0)^{\times}),\quad(a_{(n)}b)_{R}\in\mathcal{F}(D_{R}(0)).

Lemma 3.2.10
.

Let
a,b\in\mathcal{F}^{0}
be homogeneous, and
n\in\mathbb{Z}
.

(1)

For
R\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
, we have
(a_{(n)}b)_{R}\in\mathcal{F}(D_{R}(0))_{\Delta(a)+\Delta(b)-n-1}
.

(2)

For
0<r<R
, we have
\mathcal{F}^{D_{r}(0)}_{D_{R}(0)}(a_{(n)}b)_{r}=(a_{(n)}b)_{R}
.

Proof.

(1) Let
C=\partial D_{R/2}(0)
be the positivity oriented circle of radius
R/2
, then

(a_{(n)}b)_{R}=\frac{1}{2\pi\sqrt{-1}}\int_{C}z^{n}\mu_{z,0}^{R}(a\otimes b)\,dz.

Thus, for
q\in S^{1}
, we have

\displaystyle\sigma_{q,D_{R}(0)}(a_{(n)}b)_{R}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi\sqrt{-1}}\int_{C}z^{n}\sigma_{q,D_{R}(0)}\mu_{z,0}^{R}(a\otimes b)\,dz=\frac{1}{2\pi\sqrt{-1}}\int_{C}z^{n}q^{\Delta(a)+\Delta(b)}\mu_{qz,0}^{R}(a\otimes b)\,dz

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi\sqrt{-1}}\int_{C}z^{n}q^{\Delta(a)+\Delta(b)-n-1}\mu_{z,0}^{R}(a\otimes b)\,dz=q^{\Delta(a)+\Delta(b)-n-1}(a_{(n)}b)_{R},

where we used
Lemma

3.2.6
in the second equality.

(2) For
z\in D_{r}(0)
, since
\mathcal{F}^{D_{r}(0)}_{D_{R}(0)}\mu^{r}_{z,0}(a\otimes b)=\mu_{z,0}^{R}(a\otimes b)
, we have

\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}z^{-m-1}\mathcal{F}^{D_{r}(0)}_{D_{R}(0)}(a_{(m)}b)_{r}=\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}z^{-m-1}(a_{(m)}b)_{R}.

Thus, the claim follows.
∎

For homogeneous
a,b\in\mathcal{F}^{0}
and
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, by
Lemma

3.2.10
, one can define

a_{(n)}b\coloneqq\{(a_{(n)}b)_{R}\}\in\mathcal{F}^{0}_{\Delta(a)+\Delta(b)-n-1}.

Note that
Definition

3.2.7
(ii) implies
a_{(n)}b=0
for
n\gg 0
. Thus, we have a linear map

(3.2.3)

Y\colon\mathbf{V}(\mathcal{F})\otimes\mathbf{V}(\mathcal{F})\to\mathbf{V}(\mathcal{F})(\!(z)\!),\quad a\otimes b\mapsto Y(a,z)b\coloneqq\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}z^{-n-1}a_{(n)}b.

Theorem 3.2.11
.

For a holomorphic prefactorization algebra
\mathcal{F}\colon\mathfrak{U}_{\mathbb{C}}\to\mathsf{LCS}_{\mathbb{C}}
, the linear space

\mathbf{V}(\mathcal{F})=\bigoplus_{\Delta\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathcal{F}^{0}_{\Delta}

has the structure of a
\mathbb{Z}
-graded vertex algebra as follows:

•

The vacuum is
\ket{0}
defined in
3.2.1
.

•

The translation operator is
T\colon\mathbf{V}(\mathcal{F})\to\mathbf{V}(\mathcal{F})
defined in
3.2.2
.

•

The state-field correspondence is
Y\colon\mathbf{V}(\mathcal{F})\otimes\mathbf{V}(\mathcal{F})\to\mathbf{V}(\mathcal{F})(\!(z)\!)
defined in (
3.2.3
)

We prove
Theorem

3.2.11
.
The conditions (i), (ii) in
Definition

3.1.4
have already been proved, so it remains to show that the above datum satisfy the axiom of vertex algebras.

Lemma 3.2.12
.

Let
R\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
. For
z\in D_{R}^{\times}(0)
and
a\in\mathbf{V}(\mathcal{F})
, we have

\mu_{z,0}^{R}(\ket{0}\otimes a)=\mathcal{F}^{0}_{D_{R}(0)}(a),\quad\mu_{z,0}^{R}(a\otimes\ket{0})=\mu_{z}^{R}(a).

Proof.

Take
r\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
satisfying
2r<|z|<R-r
, then

\displaystyle\mu_{z,0}^{R}(\ket{0}\otimes a)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}^{D_{r}(z),D_{r}(0)}_{D_{R}(0)}(\sigma_{(1,z),D_{r}(0)}\mathcal{F}^{\varnothing}_{D_{r}(0)}(1_{\mathcal{F}})\otimes\mathcal{F}^{0}_{D_{r}(0)}(a))

\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}^{D_{r}(z),D_{r}(0)}_{D_{R}(0)}(\mathcal{F}^{\varnothing}_{D_{r}(z)}\sigma_{(1,z),\varnothing}(1_{\mathcal{F}})\otimes\mathcal{F}^{0}_{D_{r}(0)}(a))

\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}^{D_{r}(z),D_{r}(0)}_{D_{R}(0)}(\mathcal{F}^{\varnothing}_{D_{r}(z)}(1_{\mathcal{F}})\otimes\mathcal{F}^{0}_{D_{r}(0)}(a))

\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}^{\varnothing,D_{r}(0)}_{D_{R}(0)}(1_{\mathcal{F}}\otimes\mathcal{F}^{0}_{D_{r}(0)}(a))

\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}^{D_{r}(0)}_{D_{R}(0)}\mathcal{F}^{0}_{D_{r}(0)}(a)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}^{0}_{D_{R}(0)}(a).

Here we used the fact that
\sigma_{(1,z)}\colon\mathcal{F}\to(1,z)\mathcal{F}
is a morphism of precosheaves in the second equality,
Definition

1.3.2
(iv) in the third equality,
Definition

1.1.1
(ii) and the second part of
Definition

1.2.1
(i) in the forth equality, the first part of
Definition

1.2.1
(i) and
Definition

1.2.1
(iv) in the fifth equality. Similarly, one can prove
\mu_{z,0}^{R}(a\otimes\ket{0})=\mu_{z}^{R}(a)
.
∎

Proof of vacuum axiom.

For
R\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
and
a\in\mathbf{V}(\mathcal{F})
, the first equation in
Lemma

3.2.12
yields that

(\ket{0}_{(n)}a)_{R}=\begin{cases}\mathcal{F}^{0}_{D_{R}(0)}(a)&(n=-1)\\
0&(n\neq-1).\end{cases}

Thus, we have
Y(\ket{0},z)=\id_{\mathbf{V}(\mathcal{F})}
. Also, for
R\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
and
a\in\mathbf{V}(\mathcal{F})
, the second equation in
Lemma

3.2.12
yields that

(a_{(n)}\ket{0})_{R}=\begin{cases}0&(n\geq 0)\\
\mu_{0}^{R}(a)&(n=-1)\end{cases}=\begin{cases}0&(n\geq 0)\\
\mathcal{F}^{0}_{D_{R}(0)}(a)&(n=-1).\end{cases}

Thus, we have
Y(a,z)\ket{0}\in\mathbf{V}(\mathcal{F})\llbracket z\rrbracket
and
Y(a,z)\ket{0}|_{z=0}=a
for each
a\in\mathbf{V}(\mathcal{F})
.
∎

Lemma 3.2.13
.

Let
R\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
. For
z\in D_{R}^{\times}(0)
and
a,b\in\mathbf{V}(\mathcal{F})
, we have

\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}z^{-n-1}(T(a_{(n)}b))_{R}=\partial_{z}\mu_{z,0}^{R}(a\otimes b)+\mu_{z,0}^{R}(a\otimes Tb).

Proof.

Take
r,s\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
satisfying

s<R,\quad 2r<|z|<s-r,

then for
w\in\mathbb{C}
such that
|w|<R-s
,

\displaystyle\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}z^{-n-1}\mu_{w}^{R}(a_{(n)}b)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}z^{-n-1}\mathcal{F}^{D_{s}(w)}_{D_{R}(0)}\sigma_{(1,w),D_{s}(0)}(a_{(n)}b)_{s}=\mathcal{F}^{D_{s}(w)}_{D_{R}(0)}\sigma_{(1,w),D_{s}(0)}\mu_{z,0}^{s}(a\otimes b)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}^{D_{s}(w)}_{D_{R}(0)}\sigma_{(1,w),D_{s}(0)}\mathcal{F}^{D_{r}(z),D_{r}(0)}_{D_{s}(0)}(\sigma_{(1,z),D_{r}(0)}\mathcal{F}^{0}_{D_{r}(0)}(a)\otimes\mathcal{F}^{0}_{D_{r}(0)}(b))

\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}^{D_{s}(w)}_{D_{R}(0)}\mathcal{F}^{D_{r}(z+w),D_{r}(w)}_{D_{s}(w)}(\sigma_{(1,z+w),D_{r}(0)}\mathcal{F}^{0}_{D_{r}(0)}(a)\otimes\sigma_{(1,w),D_{r}(0)}\mathcal{F}^{0}_{D_{r}(0)}(b))

\displaystyle=\mu_{z+w,w}^{R}(a\otimes b).

Thus, we have

\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}z^{-n-1}(T(a_{(n)}b))_{R}=\partial_{w}\mu_{z+w,w}^{R}(a\otimes b)|_{w=0}=\partial_{z}\mu_{z,0}^{R}(a\otimes b)+\partial_{w}\mu_{z,w}^{R}(a\otimes b)|_{w=0},

where we used
Lemma

A.1.10
in the second equality.

Next, we calculate
\mu_{z,0}^{R}(a\otimes Tb)
. Take
s\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
satisfying
2s<|z|<R-s
, then

\displaystyle\mu_{z,0}^{R}(a\otimes Tb)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}^{D_{s}(z),D_{s}(0)}_{D_{R}(0)}(\sigma_{(1,z),D_{s}(0)}\mathcal{F}^{0}_{D_{s}(0)}(a)\otimes(Tb)_{s})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}^{D_{s}(z),D_{s}(0)}_{D_{R}(0)}(\sigma_{(1,z),D_{s}(0)}\mathcal{F}^{0}_{D_{s}(0)}(a)\otimes\partial_{w}\mu^{s}_{w}(b)|_{w=0}).

Now, take
0<r<s
, then for
w\in\mathbb{C}
such that
|w|<s-r
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{D_{s}(z),D_{s}(0)}_{D_{R}(0)}(\sigma_{(1,z),D_{s}(0)}\mathcal{F}^{0}_{D_{s}(0)}(a)\otimes\mu_{w}^{s}(b))

\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}^{D_{s}(z),D_{s}(0)}_{D_{R}(0)}(\sigma_{(1,z),D_{s}(0)}\mathcal{F}^{0}_{D_{s}(0)}(a)\otimes\mathcal{F}^{D_{r}(w)}_{D_{s}(0)}\sigma_{(1,w),D_{r}(0)}\mathcal{F}^{0}_{D_{r}(0)}(b))

\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}^{D_{s}(z),D_{s}(0)}_{D_{R}(0)}(\sigma_{(1,z),D_{s}(0)}\mathcal{F}^{D_{r}(0)}_{D_{s}(0)}\mathcal{F}^{0}_{D_{r}(0)}(a)\otimes\mathcal{F}^{D_{r}(w)}_{D_{s}(0)}\sigma_{(1,w),D_{r}(0)}\mathcal{F}^{0}_{D_{r}(0)}(b))

\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}^{D_{s}(z),D_{s}(0)}_{D_{R}(0)}(\mathcal{F}^{D_{r}(z)}_{D_{s}(z)}\sigma_{(1,z),D_{r}(0)}\mathcal{F}^{0}_{D_{r}(0)}(a)\otimes\mathcal{F}^{D_{r}(w)}_{D_{s}(0)}\sigma_{(1,w),D_{r}(0)}\mathcal{F}^{0}_{D_{r}(0)}(b))

\displaystyle=\mu^{R}_{z,w}(a\otimes b).

Thus, we have

\mu_{z,0}^{R}(a\otimes Tb)=\partial_{w}\mu_{z,w}^{R}(a\otimes b)|_{w=0},

which completes the proof.
∎

Proof of translation axiom.

For
R\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
by
Lemma

3.2.12
,

\mu_{z}^{R}(\ket{0})=\mu_{z,0}^{R}(\ket{0}\otimes\ket{0})=\mathcal{F}^{0}_{D_{R}(0)}(\ket{0}),

which implies
(T\ket{0})_{R}=0
. Thus, we have
T\ket{0}=0
. Also, for
R\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
and
a,b\in\mathbf{V}(\mathcal{F})
,
Lemma

3.2.13
yields that

(T(a_{(n)}b))_{R}=-n(a_{(n-1)}b)_{R}+(a_{(n)}Tb)_{R}.

Thus, we have
[T,Y(a,z)]=\partial_{z}Y(a,z)
for each
a\in\mathbf{V}(\mathcal{F})
.
∎

Lemma 3.2.14
.

Let
a,b\in\mathbf{V}(\mathcal{F})
. There exists
N\in\mathbb{N}
such that the function

D_{R}^{\times}(0)^{2}\mathbin{\setminus}\{z=w\}\to\mathcal{F}(D_{R}(0)),\quad(z,w)\mapsto(z-w)^{N}\mu_{z,w,0}^{R}(a\otimes b\otimes c)

for each
c\in\mathbf{V}(\mathcal{F})
extends to the separately holomorphic function on
D_{R}^{\times}(0)^{2}
.

Proof.

Take
N\in\mathbb{N}
such that
a_{(n)}b=0
and
b_{(n)}a=0
for
n\geq N
.
Fix
z\in D_{R}^{\times}(0)
. Take
r\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
such that
2r<|z|<R-r
, then for
w\in D_{r}^{\times}(z)
, a direct calculation shows that

\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}(w-z)^{-n-1}\mu_{z,0}^{R}(b_{(n)}a\otimes c)=\mu_{z,w,0}^{R}(a\otimes b\otimes c).

Thus, by
Theorem

A.5.6
, the point
w=z
is removable singularity of the function
D_{r}^{\times}(z)\to\mathcal{F}(D_{R}(0))
,
w\mapsto(z-w)^{N}\mu_{z,w,0}^{R}(a\otimes b\otimes c)
. Similarly, one can show that for fixed
w\in D_{R}^{\times}(0)
, the point
z=w
is removable singularity of the function
z\mapsto(z-w)^{N}\mu_{z,w,0}^{R}(a\otimes b\otimes c)
.
∎

Proof of locality.

Let
a,b\in\mathbf{V}(\mathcal{F})
, and
N\in\mathbb{N}
be as in
Lemma

3.2.14
. It is enough to show that

(3.2.4)

\sum_{k=0}^{N}(-1)^{k}\binom{N}{k}a_{(m+N-k)}(b_{(n+k)}c)=\sum_{k=0}^{N}(-1)^{k}\binom{N}{k}b_{(n+k)}(a_{(m+N-k)}c).

for
m,n\in\mathbb{Z}
and
c\in\mathbf{V}(\mathcal{F})
. First, a direct calculation shows the following (1) and (2):

(1)

For
R\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
and
z\in D_{R}^{\times}(0)
, take
r\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
such that
2r<|z|<R-r
. Then for
w\in D_{r}^{\times}(0)
,

\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}w^{-n-1}\mu_{z,0}^{R}(a\otimes b_{(n)}c)=\mu_{z,w,0}^{R}(a\otimes b\otimes c).

(2)

For
R\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
and
w\in D_{R}^{\times}(0)
, take
r\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
such that
2r<|w|<R-r
, then for
z\in D_{r}^{\times}(0)
,

\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}z^{-m-1}\mu_{w,0}^{R}(b\otimes a_{(m)}c)=\mu_{z,w,0}^{R}(a\otimes b\otimes c).

For
R\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
, by
Lemma

3.2.14
, we have

\Res_{z=0}\Res_{w=0}z^{m}w^{n}(z-w)^{N}\mu_{z,w,0}^{R}(a\otimes b\otimes c)=\Res_{w=0}\Res_{z=0}z^{m}w^{n}(z-w)^{N}\mu_{z,w,0}^{R}(a\otimes b\otimes c),

which together with above (1) and (2) implies that

\sum_{k=0}^{N}(-1)^{k}\binom{N}{k}(a_{(m+N-k)}(b_{(n+k)}c))_{R}=\sum_{k=0}^{N}(-1)^{k}\binom{N}{k}(b_{(n+k)}(a_{(m+N-k)}c))_{R}.

Thus, (
3.2.4
) follows.
∎

Now the proof of
Theorem

3.2.11
is complete.

Let
\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}\colon\mathfrak{U}_{\mathbb{C}}\to\mathsf{LCS}_{\mathbb{C}}
be holomorphic prefactorization algebras. For a morphism
\varphi\colon\mathcal{F}\to\mathcal{G}
of holomorphic prefactorization algebras, we have a continuous linear map
\varphi^{0}\colon\mathcal{F}^{0}\to\mathcal{G}^{0}
. Since
\varphi^{0}(\mathcal{F}^{0}_{\Delta})\subset\mathcal{G}^{0}_{\Delta}
for all
\Delta\in\mathbb{Z}
, we get a linear map

\mathbf{V}(\varphi)\coloneqq\varphi^{0}|_{\mathbf{V}(\mathcal{F})}\colon\mathbf{V}(\mathcal{F})\to\mathbf{V}(\mathcal{G}).

Proposition 3.2.15
.

For a morphism
\varphi\colon\mathcal{F}\to\mathcal{G}
of holomorphic prefactorization algebras, the linear map
\mathbf{V}(\varphi)\colon\mathbf{V}(\mathcal{F})\to\mathbf{V}(\mathcal{G})
is a morphism of
\mathbb{Z}
-graded vertex algebras. Hence, the map
\mathcal{F}\mapsto\mathbf{V}(\mathcal{F})
gives rise to the functor

\mathbf{V}\colon\mathsf{HolPFA}_{\mathbb{C}}\to\mathsf{grVA}^{\mathsf{bb}}.

Proof.

It is enough to show that
\mathbf{V}(\varphi)
is a morphism of vertex algebras.

•

\mathbf{V}(\varphi)
preserves the vacuums:
we have

\mathbf{V}(\varphi)(\ket{0}_{\mathcal{F}})=\{\varphi_{D_{R}(z)}\mathcal{F}^{\varnothing}_{D_{R}(0)}(1_{\mathcal{F}})\}_{R\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}}=\{\mathcal{G}^{\varnothing}_{D_{R}(0)}\varphi_{\varnothing}(1_{\mathcal{F}})\}_{R\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}}=\{\mathcal{G}^{\varnothing}_{D_{R}(0)}(1_{\mathcal{G}})\}=\ket{0}_{\mathcal{G}}.

•

\mathbf{V}(\varphi)
commutes with the translation operators: Let
a\in\mathbf{V}(\mathcal{F})
. For
R\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
, since a direct calculation yields that

\varphi_{D_{R}(0)}\mu^{R}_{z}(a)=\mu^{R}_{z}(\varphi^{0}(a))\quad(z\in D_{R}(0)),

we have

\varphi_{D_{R}(0)}(Ta)_{R}=\partial_{z}\varphi_{D_{R}(0)}\mu^{R}_{z}(a)|_{z=0}=\partial_{z}\mu_{z}^{R}(\varphi^{0}(a))=(T\varphi^{0}(a))_{R}.

Thus
\mathbf{V}(\varphi)(Ta)=T\mathbf{V}(\varphi)(a)
follows.

•

\mathbf{V}(\varphi)
commutes with the state-field correspondences: Let
a,b\in\mathbf{V}(\varphi)
. For
R\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
, since a direct calculation yields that

\displaystyle\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}z^{-n-1}\varphi_{D_{R}(0)}(a_{(n)}b)_{R}

\displaystyle=\varphi_{D_{R}(0)}\mu^{R}_{z,0}(a\otimes b)=\mu^{R}_{z,0}(\varphi^{0}(a)\otimes\varphi^{0}(b))

\displaystyle=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}z^{-n-1}(\varphi^{0}(a)_{(n)}\varphi^{0}(b))_{R}\quad(z\in D_{R}^{\times}(0)),

we have

\varphi_{D_{R}(0)}(a_{(n)}b)_{R}=(\varphi^{0}(a)_{(n)}\varphi^{0}(b))_{R}\quad(n\in\mathbb{Z}).

Thus
\mathbf{V}(\varphi)(a_{(n)}b)=\mathbf{V}(\varphi)(a)_{(n)}\mathbf{V}(\varphi)(b)
follows for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
.
∎

3.3.
Commutative vertex algebras from locally constant prefactorization algebras

In this subsection we show that if the holomorphic prefactorization algebra
\mathcal{F}\colon\mathfrak{U}_{\mathbb{C}}\to\mathsf{LCS}_{\mathbb{C}}
is locally constant (
Definition

1.5.1
), then the vertex algebra
\mathbf{V}(\mathcal{F})
is commutative.

Let
\mathcal{F}\colon\mathfrak{U}_{\mathbb{C}}\to\mathsf{LCS}_{\mathbb{C}}
be a locally constant holomorphic prefactorization algebra. By applying the functor
(-)(\mathbb{C})\colon\mathsf{PFA}^{\mathsf{loc}}(\mathbb{C})\to\mathsf{CAlg}_{\mathbb{C}}
constructed in
§​

2.1
, we have a commutative algebra
\mathcal{F}(\mathbb{C})
. For
R\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
, since
\mathcal{F}^{D_{R}(0)}_{\mathbb{C}}\colon\mathcal{F}(D_{R}(0))\to\mathcal{F}(\mathbb{C})
is a linear isomorphism, the linear space
\mathcal{F}(D_{R}(0))
inherits the structure of a commutative algebra from
\mathcal{F}(\mathbb{C})
. (See
Remark

2.1.5
.)

Lemma 3.3.1
.

Let
R\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
. For
z\in D_{R}(0)^{\times}
and
a,b\in\mathcal{F}^{0}
, we have

\mu^{R}_{z,0}(a\otimes b)=\mu^{R}_{z}(a)\cdot\mathcal{F}^{0}_{D_{R}(0)}(b).

Proof.

Take
r\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
such that
2r<|z|<R-r
, then

\displaystyle\mu^{R}_{z,0}(a\otimes b)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}^{D_{r}(z),D_{r}(0)}_{D_{R}(0)}\bigl(\sigma_{(1,z),D_{r}(0)}\mathcal{F}^{0}_{D_{r}(0)}(a)\otimes\mathcal{F}^{0}_{D_{r}(0)}(b)\bigr)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}^{D_{r}(z),D_{r}(0)}_{D_{R}(0)}\bigl((\mathcal{F}^{D_{r}(z)}_{D_{R}(0)})^{-1}\mu^{R}_{z}(a)\otimes(\mathcal{F}^{D_{r}(0)}_{D_{R}(0)})^{-1}\mathcal{F}^{0}_{D_{R}(0)}(b)\bigr)

\displaystyle=\mu^{R}_{z}(a)\cdot\mathcal{F}^{0}_{D_{R}(0)}(b).\qed

Proposition 3.3.2
.

If
\mathcal{F}\colon\mathfrak{U}_{\mathbb{C}}\to\mathsf{LCS}_{\mathbb{C}}
is a locally constant holomorphic prefactorization algebra, then the vertex algebra
\mathbf{V}(\mathcal{F})
is commutative.

Proof.

It is enough to show that
a_{(n)}b=0
for
a,b\in\mathbf{V}(\mathcal{F})
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
, but this follows from
Lemma

3.3.1
and
Theorem

A.5.6
.
∎

Remark 3.3.3
.

Let
\mathcal{F}\colon\mathfrak{U}_{\mathbb{C}}\to\mathsf{LCS}_{\mathbb{C}}
be a locally constant holomorphic prefactorization algebra. Notice that by
Lemma

3.3.1
, the multiplication of the commutative vertex algebra
\mathbf{V}(\mathcal{F})
is equal to

a\cdot b=\{(a_{(-1)}b)_{R}\}_{R\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}}=\bigl\{\mathcal{F}^{0}_{D_{R}(0)}(a)\cdot\mathcal{F}^{0}_{D_{R}(0)}(b)\bigr\}_{R\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}}.

3.4.
Locally constant factorization algebras from commutative vertex algebras

In this subsection we construct a locally constant holomorphic factorization algebra from a commutative vertex algebra (
Proposition

3.4.6
,
Proposition

3.4.9
), and discuss the relationship with the jet algebras of commutative algebras (
Proposition

3.4.11
).
We continue to use the notation
\mathfrak{B}
defined in (
1.5.1
). Note that
\mathfrak{B}
is an open basis of
\mathbb{C}
satisfying:

1

\varnothing\in\mathfrak{B}
.

2

For
L,M\in\mathfrak{B}
such that
L\cap M=\varnothing
, we have
L\sqcup M\in\mathfrak{B}
.

3

For
a\in S^{1}\ltimes\mathbb{C}
and
L\in\mathfrak{B}
, we have
aL\in\mathfrak{B}
.

Let
V=\bigoplus_{\Delta\in\mathbb{Z}}V_{\Delta}
be a commutative
\mathbb{Z}
-graded vertex algebra such that

V_{\Delta}=0
for
\Delta\ll 0
.
We denote
\overline{V}\coloneqq\prod_{\Delta\in\mathbb{Z}}V_{\Delta}
and by
\pi_{\Delta}\colon\overline{V}\to V_{\Delta}
the projection. We naturally regard
V\subset\overline{V}
.

As we explained in
Example

3.1.3
, the commutative vertex algebra
V
is a commutative
\mathbb{C}
-algebra by the multiplication

V\times V\to V,\quad(a,b)\mapsto ab\coloneqq a_{(-1)}b

and the unit
\ket{0}
.
The condition
V_{\Delta}=0
(
\Delta\ll 0
) guarantees that one can define a multiplication on
\overline{V}
by

\overline{V}\times\overline{V}\to\overline{V},\quad(a,b)\mapsto ab\coloneqq\bigl\{\sum_{\Delta^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}}\pi_{\Delta^{\prime}}(a)\pi_{\Delta-\Delta^{\prime}}(b)\bigr\}_{\Delta\in\mathbb{Z}}.

The linear space
\overline{V}
has the structure of a commutative
\mathbb{C}
-algebra by this multiplication and the unit
\ket{0}
. Thus, by applying the functor
\mathbf{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}\colon\mathsf{CAlg}_{\mathbb{C}}\to\mathsf{FA}^{\mathsf{loc}}(\mathbb{C})
constructed in
§​

2.2
, we have a locally constant factorization algebra
\mathbf{F}_{\overline{V}}^{\mathrm{loc}}\colon\mathfrak{U}_{\mathbb{C}}\to\mathsf{Mod}\,\mathbb{C}
.

To define an
S^{1}\ltimes\mathbb{C}
-equivariant structure on
\mathbf{F}_{\overline{V}}^{\mathrm{loc}}
, we use the translation operator
T\colon V\to V
. Recall that
T
is a derivation with respect to the multiplication on
V
.

For
q\in S^{1}
, we have a linear map

q^{L_{0}}\colon\overline{V}\to\overline{V},\quad a\mapsto\{q^{\Delta}\pi_{\Delta}(a)\}_{\Delta\in\mathbb{Z}}.

Also, for
z\in\mathbb{C}
, we have a linear map

e^{zT}\colon\overline{V}\to\overline{V},\quad a\mapsto\bigl\{\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\frac{z^{n}}{n!}T^{n}\pi_{\Delta-n}(a)\bigr\}_{\Delta\in\mathbb{Z}}.

Note that both
q^{L_{0}}
and
e^{zT}
are morphisms of commutative
\mathbb{C}
-algebras.

Definition 3.4.1
.

Let
(q,z)\in S^{1}\ltimes\mathbb{C}
. For
L=D_{r_{1}}(z_{1})\sqcup\cdots\sqcup D_{r_{l}}(z_{l})\in\mathfrak{B}
, define

\sigma_{(q,z),L}\colon F_{\overline{V}}^{\mathrm{loc}}(L)\to F_{\overline{V}}^{\mathrm{loc}}((q,z)L),\quad a_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{l}\mapsto e^{zT}q^{L_{0}}a_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes e^{zT}q^{L_{0}}a_{l},

with the convention

\sigma_{(q,z),\varnothing}\coloneqq\id_{\mathbb{C}}\colon F_{\overline{V}}^{\mathrm{loc}}(\varnothing)\to F_{\overline{V}}^{\mathrm{loc}}((q,z)\varnothing).

for
l=0
.

Lemma 3.4.2
.

The prefactorization algebra
F_{\overline{V}}^{\mathrm{loc}}\colon\mathfrak{B}\to\mathsf{Mod}\,\mathbb{C}
is an
S^{1}\ltimes\mathbb{C}
-equivariant prefactorization algebra by the structure morphism
\sigma_{(q,z)}\coloneqq\{\sigma_{(q,z),L}\}_{L\in\mathfrak{B}}\colon F_{\overline{V}}^{\mathrm{loc}}\to(q,z)F_{\overline{V}}^{\mathrm{loc}}
.

Proof.

First, for
(q,z)\in S^{1}\ltimes\mathbb{C}
, it is easy to see that
\sigma_{(q,z)}\colon F_{\overline{V}}^{\mathrm{loc}}\to(q,z)F_{\overline{V}}^{\mathrm{loc}}
is a morphism of precosheaves since both
q^{L_{0}},e^{zT}\colon\overline{V}\to\overline{V}
are morphisms of commutative
\mathbb{C}
-algebras. We check the conditions (i)-(iv) in
Definition

1.3.1
.

1

Let
(q,z),(q^{\prime},z^{\prime})\in S^{1}\ltimes\mathbb{C}
and
L=D_{r_{1}}(z_{1})\sqcup\cdots\sqcup D_{r_{l}}(z_{l})\in\mathfrak{B}
. For
a=a_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{l}\in F_{\overline{V}}^{\mathrm{loc}}(L)
,

\displaystyle\sigma_{(q,z),(q^{\prime},z^{\prime})L}\circ\sigma_{(q^{\prime},z^{\prime}),L}(a)

\displaystyle=e^{zT}q^{L_{0}}e^{z^{\prime}T}q^{\prime L_{0}}a_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes e^{zT}q^{L_{0}}e^{z^{\prime}T}q^{\prime L_{0}}a_{l}

\displaystyle=e^{zT}e^{qz^{\prime}T}q^{L_{0}}q^{\prime L_{0}}a_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes e^{zT}e^{qz^{\prime}T}q^{L_{0}}q^{\prime L_{0}}a_{l}

\displaystyle=\sigma_{(q,z)(q^{\prime},z^{\prime}),L}(a).

2

This is clear by the definition of
\sigma_{(q,z),L}
.

3

For
(q,z)\in S^{1}\ltimes\mathbb{C}
and
L,M,N\in\mathfrak{B}
such that
L\sqcup M\subset N
, since
\sigma_{(q,z)}
is a morphism of precosheaves,

\displaystyle\sigma_{(q,z),N}\circ(F_{\overline{V}}^{\mathrm{loc}})^{L,M}_{N}

\displaystyle=\sigma_{(q,z),N}\circ(F_{\overline{V}}^{\mathrm{loc}})^{L\sqcup M}_{N}=(F_{\overline{V}}^{\mathrm{loc}})^{(q,z)(L\sqcup M)}_{(q,z)N}\circ\sigma_{(q,z),L\sqcup M}

\displaystyle=(F_{\overline{V}}^{\mathrm{loc}})^{(q,z)L,(q,z)M}_{(q,z)N}\circ(\sigma_{(q,z),L}\otimes\sigma_{(q,z),M}).

4

Clear by the convention
\sigma_{(q,z),\varnothing}=\id_{\mathbb{C}}
.
∎

By
Lemma

3.4.2
and the construction in
§​

1.4
, the factorization algebra
\mathbf{F}_{\overline{V}}^{\mathrm{loc}}
has the
S^{1}\ltimes\mathbb{C}
-equivariant structure. We denote it by
\widetilde{\sigma}_{(q,z)}\colon\mathbf{F}_{\overline{V}}^{\mathrm{loc}}\to(q,z)\mathbf{F}_{\overline{V}}^{\mathrm{loc}}
.

Lemma 3.4.3
.

For
R\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
and
\Delta\in\mathbb{Z}
, we have
(\widetilde{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{\overline{V}})^{D_{R}(0)}_{D_{R}(0)}(V_{\Delta})=\mathbf{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{\overline{V}}(D_{R}(0))_{\Delta}
.

Proof.

For
a\in\overline{V}
, we have

\widetilde{\sigma}_{q,\mathbb{C}}(\widetilde{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{\overline{V}})^{D_{R}(0)}_{D_{R}(0)}(a)=(\widetilde{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{\overline{V}})^{D_{R}(0)}_{D_{R}(0)}\sigma_{q,\mathbb{C}}(a)\quad(q\in S^{1}).

Thus, the lemma is clear since
V_{\Delta}=\{a\in\overline{V}\mid\forall q\in S^{1},\,\sigma_{q,D_{R}(0)}(a)=q^{\Delta}a\}
.
∎

Let
V=\bigoplus_{\Delta\in\mathbb{Z}}V_{\Delta}
(resp.
W=\bigoplus_{\Delta\in\mathbb{Z}}W_{\Delta}
) be a commutative
\mathbb{Z}
-graded vertex algebra such that
V_{\Delta}=0
(resp.
W_{\Delta}=0
) for
\Delta\ll 0
. For a morphism
f\colon V\to W
of commutative
\mathbb{Z}
-graded vertex algebras, define

\overline{f}\colon\overline{V}\to\overline{W},\quad a\mapsto\{f(\pi_{\Delta}(a))\}_{\Delta\in\mathbb{Z}},

then
\overline{f}
is a morphism of commutative
\mathbb{C}
-algebras. Thus, we have a morphism
\mathbf{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{\overline{f}}\colon\mathbf{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{\overline{V}}\to\mathbf{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{\overline{W}}
of factorization algebras.

Lemma 3.4.4
.

For a morphism
f\colon V\to W
of commutative
\mathbb{Z}
-graded vertex algebras, the morphism
F^{\mathrm{loc}}_{\overline{f}}\colon F^{\mathrm{loc}}_{\overline{V}}\to F^{\mathrm{loc}}_{\overline{W}}
is a morphism of
S^{1}\ltimes\mathbb{C}
-equivariant prefactorization algebras.

Proof.

Let
(q,z)\in S^{1}\ltimes\mathbb{C}
and
L=D_{r_{1}}(z_{1})\sqcup\cdots\sqcup D_{r_{l}}(z_{l})\in\mathfrak{B}
. We abuse the notation
\sigma_{(q,z),L}
for
F^{\mathrm{loc}}_{\overline{V}}
and
F^{\mathrm{loc}}_{\overline{W}}
. Then, for
a=a_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{l}\in F_{\overline{V}}^{\mathrm{loc}}(L)
,

\displaystyle F^{\mathrm{loc}}_{\overline{f},(q,z)L}\sigma_{(q,z),L}(a)

\displaystyle=\overline{f}(e^{zT}q^{L_{0}}a_{1})\otimes\cdots\otimes\overline{f}(e^{zT}q^{L_{0}}a_{l})

\displaystyle=e^{zT}q^{L_{0}}\overline{f}(a_{1})\otimes\cdots\otimes e^{zT}q^{L_{0}}\overline{f}(a_{l})

\displaystyle=\sigma_{(q,z),L}F^{\mathrm{loc}}_{\overline{f},L}(a).\qed

For a morphism
f\colon V\to W
of commutative
\mathbb{Z}
-graded vertex algebras, by
Lemma

3.4.4
, the morphism
\mathbf{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{\overline{f}}\colon\mathbf{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{\overline{V}}\to\mathbf{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{\overline{W}}
is a morphism of
S^{1}\ltimes\mathbb{C}
-equivariant factorization algebras. The map
V\mapsto\mathbf{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{\overline{V}}
gives rise to the functor

\mathbf{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{\overline{(\,\cdot\,)}}\colon\mathsf{grComVA}^{\mathsf{bb}}\to\mathsf{FA}^{\mathsf{loc}}_{S^{1}\ltimes\mathbb{C}}(\mathbb{C},\mathsf{Mod}\,\mathbb{C}),

where
\mathsf{FA}^{\mathsf{loc}}_{S^{1}\ltimes\mathbb{C}}(\mathbb{C},\mathsf{Mod}\,\mathbb{C})
denotes the full subcategory of
\mathsf{PFA}_{S^{1}\ltimes\mathbb{C}}(\mathbb{C},\mathsf{Mod}\,\mathbb{C})
whose objects are locally constant
S^{1}\ltimes\mathbb{C}
-equivariant factorization algebras.

To make
\mathbf{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{\overline{V}}
a holomorphic factorization algebra (
Definition

3.2.7
), we need to introduce a locally convex topology on
\mathbf{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{\overline{V}}(U)
.
In order to do this, suppose that
V=\bigoplus_{\Delta\in\mathbb{Z}}V_{\Delta}
is a commutative
\mathbb{Z}
-graded vertex algebra which satisfies (i)
V_{\Delta}=0
for
\Delta\ll 0
and (ii)
\dim_{\mathbb{C}}V_{\Delta}<\infty
for all
\Delta\in\mathbb{Z}
.

For each
\Delta\in\mathbb{Z}
, the finite-dimensional linear space
V_{\Delta}
has a unique topology which makes
V_{\Delta}
a Hausdorff topological vector space. This topology is actually defined by a single norm
\|\cdot\|_{\Delta}
, and hence is a locally convex topology. Since the topological vector space
V_{\Delta}
is complete and Hausdorff, the linear space
\overline{V}=\prod_{\Delta\in\mathbb{Z}}V_{\Delta}
equipped with the product topology is a complete Hausdorff locally convex space. Note that this locally convex topology on
\overline{V}
is defined by the set of seminorms

\Gamma=\{\|\cdot\|_{\Delta}\circ\pi_{\Delta}\mid\Delta\in\mathbb{Z}\}.

In particular,
\overline{V}
is a Fréchet space.

Lemma 3.4.5
.

(1)

The multiplication
\overline{V}\times\overline{V}\to\overline{V}
is continuous.

(2)

For
q\in S^{1}
, the linear map
q^{L_{0}}\colon\overline{V}\to\overline{V}
is continuous.

(3)

For
z\in\mathbb{C}
, the linear map
e^{zT}\colon\overline{V}\to\overline{V}
is continuous.

Proof.

(1) Since
\overline{V}
is a Fréchet space, it is enough to show that
\overline{V}\times\overline{V}\to\overline{V}
is separately continuous. Fix
a\in\overline{V}
, and take a sequence
\{b_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
in
\overline{V}
which converges to
0\in\overline{V}
. For
\Delta\in\mathbb{Z}
, note that

\pi_{\Delta}(ab_{n})=\sum_{\Delta^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}}\pi_{\Delta^{\prime}}(a)\pi_{\Delta-\Delta^{\prime}}(b_{n})

is a finite sum.
Also, note that the bilinear map
V_{\Delta_{1}}\times V_{\Delta_{2}}\to V_{\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{2}}
,
(x,y)\mapsto xy
is continuous because
\dim_{\mathbb{C}}V_{\Delta}<\infty
. Thus, we have

\lim_{n\to\infty}\pi_{\Delta}(ab_{n})=\sum_{\Delta^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}}\lim_{n\to\infty}\pi_{\Delta^{\prime}}(a)\pi_{\Delta-\Delta^{\prime}}(b_{n})=\sum_{\Delta^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}}\pi_{\Delta^{\prime}}(a)\lim_{n\to\infty}\pi_{\Delta-\Delta^{\prime}}(b_{n})=0,

which shows the claim.

(2) It is enough to show that
\pi_{\Delta}\circ q^{L_{0}}\colon\overline{V}\to V_{\Delta}
,
a\mapsto q^{\Delta}\pi_{\Delta}(a)
is continuous for each
\Delta\in\mathbb{Z}
, but this is obvious.

(3) It is enough to show that
\pi_{\Delta}\circ e^{zT}\colon\overline{V}\to V_{\Delta}
,
a\mapsto\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}(z^{n}/n!)T^{n}\pi_{\Delta-n}(a)
is continuous for each
\Delta\in\mathbb{Z}
. This is clear since the condition
\dim_{\mathbb{C}}V_{\Delta-n}<\infty
yields that
T^{n}\colon V_{\Delta-n}\to V_{\Delta}
is continuous.
∎

By
Lemma

3.4.5
, the
S^{1}\ltimes\mathbb{C}
-equivariant prefactorization algebra
F_{\overline{V}}^{\mathrm{loc}}\colon\mathfrak{B}\to\mathsf{Mod}\,\mathbb{C}
gives rise to an
S^{1}\ltimes\mathbb{C}
-equivariant prefactorization algebra
F_{\overline{V}}^{\mathrm{loc}}\colon\mathfrak{B}\to\mathsf{LCS}_{\mathbb{C}}
. Thus, we have an
S^{1}\ltimes\mathbb{C}
-equivarint factorization algebra
\mathbf{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{\overline{V}}\colon\mathfrak{U}_{\mathbb{C}}\to\mathsf{LCS}_{\mathbb{C}}
.

Proposition 3.4.6
.

For a commutative
\mathbb{Z}
-graded vertex algebra
V=\bigoplus_{\Delta\in\mathbb{Z}}V_{\Delta}
such that
V_{\Delta}=0
(
\Delta\ll 0
) and
\dim_{\mathbb{C}}V_{\Delta}<\infty
(
\Delta\in\mathbb{Z}
), the
S^{1}\ltimes\mathbb{C}
-equivariant factorization algebra
\mathbf{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{\overline{V}}\colon\mathfrak{U}_{\mathbb{C}}\to\mathsf{LCS}_{\mathbb{C}}
is holomorphic.

Proof.

We check the conditions (i)-(iv) in
Definition

3.2.7
:

1

This is clear since
\mathbf{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{\overline{V}}
is locally constant.

2

This follows from
Lemma

3.4.3
.

3

For
R\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
, by the definition, the locally convex space
F^{\mathrm{loc}}_{\overline{V}}(D_{R}(0))=\overline{V}
is complete and Hausdorff. Since
(\widetilde{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{\overline{V}})\colon F^{\mathrm{loc}}_{\overline{V}}(D_{R}(0))\to(\mathbf{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{\overline{V}})(D_{R}(0))
is an isomorphism of locally convex spaces, the condition holds.

4

For
a_{1},\ldots,a_{l}\in(\mathbf{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{\overline{V}})^{0}
, we have

((\widetilde{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{\overline{V}})^{D_{R}(0)}_{D_{R}(0)})^{-1}\mu^{R}_{z_{1},\ldots,z_{l}}(a_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{l})=\prod_{i=1}^{l}e^{z_{i}T}((\widetilde{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{\overline{V}})^{D_{r}(0)}_{D_{r}(0)})^{-1}(\mathbf{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{\overline{V}})^{0}_{D_{r}(0)}(a_{i}).

Thus, it is enough to show that for
a_{1},\ldots,a_{l}\in V
, the function

\Conf_{l}(D_{R}(0))\to\overline{V},\quad(z_{1},\ldots,z_{l})\mapsto\prod_{i=1}^{l}e^{z_{i}T}a_{i}

is holomorphic, or equivalently,

\Conf_{l}(D_{R}(0))\to V_{\Delta},\quad(z_{1},\ldots,z_{l})\mapsto\pi_{\Delta}\bigl(\prod_{i=1}^{l}e^{z_{i}T}a_{i}\bigr)

is holomorphic for each
\Delta\in\mathbb{Z}
, but this is clear since

\displaystyle\pi_{\Delta}\bigl(\prod_{i=1}^{l}e^{z_{i}T}a_{i}\bigr)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\Delta_{1},\ldots,\Delta_{l}\in\mathbb{Z}\\
\Delta_{1}+\cdots+\Delta_{l}=\Delta\end{subarray}}\prod_{i=1}^{l}\pi_{\Delta_{i}}(e^{z_{i}T}a_{i})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\Delta_{1},\ldots,\Delta_{l}\in\mathbb{Z}\\
\Delta_{1}+\cdots+\Delta_{l}=\Delta\end{subarray}}\prod_{i=1}^{l}\Bigl(\sum_{m_{i}\in\mathbb{N}}\frac{z_{i}^{m_{i}}}{m_{i}!}T^{m_{i}}\pi_{\Delta_{i}-m_{i}}(a_{i})\Bigr)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\Delta_{1},\ldots,\Delta_{l}\in\mathbb{Z}\\
\Delta_{1}+\cdots+\Delta_{l}=\Delta\end{subarray}}\sum_{m_{1},\ldots,m_{l}\in\mathbb{N}}\,\prod_{i=1}^{l}\Bigl(\frac{z_{i}^{m_{i}}}{m_{i}!}T^{m_{i}}\pi_{\Delta_{i}-m_{i}}(a_{i})\Bigr).\qed

Let
V=\bigoplus_{\Delta\in\mathbb{Z}}V_{\Delta}
(resp.
W=\bigoplus_{\Delta\in\mathbb{Z}}W_{\Delta}
) be a commutative
\mathbb{Z}
-graded vertex algebra such that
V_{\Delta}=0
(resp.
W_{\Delta}=0
) for
\Delta\ll 0
and
\dim_{\mathbb{C}}V_{\Delta}<\infty
(resp.
\dim_{\mathbb{C}}W_{\Delta}<\infty
) for all
\Delta\in\mathbb{Z}
. For a morphism
f\colon V\to W
of
\mathbb{Z}
-graded vertex algebras, since the linear map
\overline{f}\colon\overline{V}\to\overline{W}
is continuous, the morphism
\mathbf{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{\overline{f}}\colon\mathbf{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{\overline{V}}\to\mathbf{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{\overline{W}}
is a morphism of holomorphic factorization algebras.
Thus, the map
V\mapsto\mathbf{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{\overline{V}}
gives rise to the functor

\mathbf{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{\overline{(\,\cdot\,)}}\colon\mathsf{grComVA}^{\mathsf{bb},\mathsf{fin}}\to\mathsf{HolFA}^{\mathsf{loc}}_{\mathbb{C}},

where
\mathsf{HolFA}^{\mathsf{loc}}_{\mathbb{C}}
denotes the full subcategory of
\mathsf{HolFA}_{\mathbb{C}}
consisting of locally constant holomorphic factorization algebras.

Remark 3.4.7
.

Let
V\in\Ob(\mathsf{grComVA}^{\mathsf{bb},\mathsf{fin}})
. For
R\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
:

(1)

By
Lemma

3.4.3
, the linear isomorphism
(\widetilde{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{\overline{V}})^{D_{R}(0)}_{D_{R}(0)}\colon\overline{V}\to\mathbf{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{\overline{V}}(D_{R}(0))
gives a linear isomorphism
(\widetilde{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{\overline{V}})^{D_{R}(0)}_{D_{R}(0)}\colon V\to\bigoplus_{\Delta\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathbf{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{\overline{V}}(D_{R}(0))_{\Delta}
.

(2)

By
Remark

3.2.8
, the linear map
(\mathbf{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{\overline{V}})^{0}_{D_{R}(0)}\colon(\mathbf{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{\overline{V}})^{0}\to\mathbf{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{\overline{V}}(D_{R}(0))
gives a linear isomorphism
(\mathbf{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{\overline{V}})^{0}_{D_{R}(0)}\colon\mathbf{V}\mathbf{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{\overline{V}}\to\bigoplus_{\Delta\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathbf{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{\overline{V}}(D_{R}(0))_{\Delta}
.

Definition 3.4.8
.

For
V\in\Ob(\mathsf{grComVA}^{\mathsf{bb},\mathsf{fin}})
, define a linear isomorphism
\eta_{V}
as the composition

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle\bigoplus_{\Delta\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathbf{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{\overline{V}}(D_{R}(0))_{\Delta}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{V}\mathbf{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{\overline{V}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\widetilde{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{\overline{V}})^{D_{R}(0)}_{D_{R}(0)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces((\mathbf{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{\overline{V}})^{0}_{D_{R}(0)})^{-1}}

Here we take
R\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
, but the definition of
\eta_{V}
is independent of the choice of
R
.

Proposition 3.4.9
.

The linear isomorphism

\eta_{V}\colon V\to\mathbf{V}\mathbf{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{\overline{V}}

is an isomorphism in
\mathsf{grComVA}^{\mathsf{bb},\mathsf{fin}}
which is natural in
V\in\Ob(\mathsf{grComVA}^{\mathsf{bb},\mathsf{fin}})
.

Proof.

The naturality of
\eta_{V}
is easy to see. It is enough to show that
\eta_{V}
is a morphism of commutative vertex algebras.

•

\eta_{V}
is a morphism of commutative algebras: For
a,b\in V
, we have

\displaystyle\eta_{V}(a)\cdot\eta_{V}(b)

\displaystyle=\bigl\{(\mathbf{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{\overline{V}})^{0}_{D_{R}(0)}\eta_{V}(a)\cdot(\mathbf{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{\overline{V}})^{0}_{D_{R}(0)}f(b)\bigr\}_{R\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}}

\displaystyle=\bigl\{(\widetilde{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{\overline{V}})^{D_{R}(0)}_{D_{R}(0)}(a)\cdot(\widetilde{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{\overline{V}})^{D_{R}(0)}_{D_{R}(0)}(b)\bigr\}_{R\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}}

\displaystyle=\bigl\{(\widetilde{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{\overline{V}})^{D_{R}(0)}_{D_{R}(0)}(ab)\bigr\}_{R\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}}=\eta_{V}(ab),

where we used
Remark

3.3.3
in the first equality, and
Lemma

2.3.2
in the third equality. Also, since the vacuum
\ket{0}
of
\mathbf{V}\mathbf{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{\overline{V}}
is defined by
\ket{0}=\bigl\{(\mathbf{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{\overline{V}})^{\varnothing}_{D_{R}(0)}(1_{\mathbf{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{\overline{V}}})\bigr\}_{R\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}}
, we have

\displaystyle\ket{0}

\displaystyle=\bigl\{(\widetilde{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{\overline{V}})^{\varnothing}_{D_{R}(0)}(1_{\mathbb{C}})\bigr\}_{R\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}}=\bigl\{(\widetilde{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{\overline{V}})^{D_{R}(0)}_{D_{R}(0)}(F^{\mathrm{loc}}_{\overline{V}})^{\varnothing}_{D_{R}(0)}(1_{\mathbb{C}})\bigr\}_{R\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}}

\displaystyle=\bigl\{(\widetilde{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{\overline{V}})^{D_{R}(0)}_{D_{R}(0)}(\ket{0}_{V})\bigr\}=f(\ket{0}_{V}).

•

\eta_{V}
commutes with the translation operators: Recall the definition of the translation operator
T
of
\mathbf{V}\mathbf{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{\overline{V}}
. Then, for
a\in V
, we have

Tf(a)=\{(Tf(a))_{R}\}_{R\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}}=\{\partial_{z}\mu^{R}_{z}(f(a))|_{z=0}\}_{R\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}}.

Here we used the notation
\mu_{z}^{R}
defined in
Definition

3.2.2
for
\mathbf{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{\overline{V}}
. Denote by

\sigma_{(q,z)}\colon F^{\mathrm{loc}}_{\overline{V}}\to(q,z)F^{\mathrm{loc}}_{\overline{V}},\quad\widetilde{\sigma}_{(q,z)}\colon\mathbf{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{\overline{V}}\to(q,z)\mathbf{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{\overline{V}}

the
S^{1}\ltimes\mathbb{C}
-equivariant structures. For
R\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
and
z\in D_{R}(0)
, take
r\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
such that
|z|<R-r
, then

\displaystyle\mu_{z}^{R}(f(a))

\displaystyle=(\mathbf{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{\overline{V}})^{D_{r}(z)}_{D_{R}(0)}\widetilde{\sigma}_{(1,z),D_{r}(0)}(\mathbf{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{\overline{V}})^{0}_{D_{r}(0)}(f(a))=(\mathbf{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{\overline{V}})^{D_{r}(z)}_{D_{R}(0)}\widetilde{\sigma}_{(1,z),D_{r}(0)}(\widetilde{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{\overline{V}})^{D_{r}(0)}_{D_{r}(0)}(a)

\displaystyle=(\mathbf{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{\overline{V}})^{D_{r}(z)}_{D_{R}(0)}(\widetilde{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{\overline{V}})^{D_{r}(z)}_{D_{r}(z)}\sigma_{(1,z),D_{r}(0)}(a)=(\widetilde{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{\overline{V}})^{D_{r}(z)}_{D_{R}(0)}e^{zT_{V}a}=(\widetilde{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{\overline{V}})^{D_{R}(0)}_{D_{R}(0)}e^{zT_{V}a}

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{z^{n}}{n!}(\widetilde{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{\overline{V}})^{D_{R}(0)}_{D_{R}(0)}(T^{n}_{V}a).

Thus, we get

\partial_{z}\mu_{z}^{R}(a)|_{z=0}=(\widetilde{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{\overline{V}})^{D_{R}(0)}_{D_{R}(0)}T_{V}a,

and hence

Tf(a)=\bigl\{(\widetilde{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{\overline{V}})^{D_{R}(0)}_{D_{R}(0)}T_{V}a\bigr\}=f(T_{V}a).\qed

Proposition 3.4.10
.

The functor
\mathbf{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{\overline{(\,\cdot\,)}}\colon\mathsf{grComVA}^{\mathsf{bb},\mathsf{fin}}\to\mathsf{HolFA}^{\mathsf{loc}}_{\mathbb{C}}
is fully faithfull.

Proof.

The faithfulness follows from
Proposition

3.4.9
. We prove that
\mathbf{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{\overline{(\,\cdot\,)}}
is full.

Let
V,W\in\Ob(\mathsf{grComVA}^{\mathsf{bb},\mathsf{fin}})
, and
\varphi\colon\mathbf{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{\overline{V}}\to\mathbf{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{\overline{W}}
be a morphism in
\mathsf{HolFA}^{\mathsf{loc}}_{\mathbb{C}}
. Define a morphism
f\colon V\to W
in
\mathsf{grComVA}^{\mathsf{bb},\mathsf{fin}}
by
f\coloneqq\eta_{W}^{-1}\circ\mathbf{V}(\varphi)\circ\eta_{V}
. To prove that
\mathbf{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{\overline{f}}=\varphi
, it is enough to show that
\mathbf{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{\overline{f},D_{R}(0)}=\varphi_{D_{R}(0)}
for all
R\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
since
\mathbf{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{\overline{V}}
and
\mathbf{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{\overline{W}}
are
S^{1}\ltimes\mathbb{C}
-equivariant factorization algebras. By the definition of
f
, we have

\mathbf{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{\overline{f},D_{R}(0)}\circ(\widetilde{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{\overline{V}})^{D_{R}(0)}_{D_{R}(0)}|_{V}=(\widetilde{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{\overline{W}})^{D_{R}(0)}_{D_{R}(0)}\circ f=\varphi_{D_{R}(0)}\circ(\widetilde{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{\overline{V}})^{D_{R}(0)}_{D_{R}(0)}|_{V}.

Since
V\subset\overline{V}
is dence subspace, we get

\mathbf{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{\overline{f},D_{R}(0)}\circ(\widetilde{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{\overline{V}})^{D_{R}(0)}_{D_{R}(0)}=\varphi_{D_{R}(0)}\circ(\widetilde{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{\overline{V}})^{D_{R}(0)}_{D_{R}(0)},

or equivalently
\mathbf{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{\overline{f},D_{R}(0)}=\varphi_{D_{R}(0)}
.
∎

Proposition 3.4.11
.

Let
A
be a finitely generated commutative algebra. For
V\in\Ob(\mathsf{grComVA}^{\mathsf{bb},\mathsf{fin}})
and a morphism
\varphi\colon\mathbf{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{A}\to\mathbf{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{V}
in
\mathsf{FA}^{\mathsf{loc}}(\mathbb{C})
, there exists a unique morphism
\widetilde{\varphi}\colon\mathbf{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{\overline{\mathcal{J}\!A}}\to\mathbf{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{\overline{V}}
in
\mathsf{HolFA}^{\mathsf{loc}}_{\mathbb{C}}
which commutes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{\overline{\mathcal{J}\!A}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{V}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{\overline{V}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\varphi}}

Here horizontal arrows denote the morphism in
\mathsf{FA}^{\mathsf{loc}}(\mathbb{C})
induced from the canonical injections
A\hookrightarrow\mathcal{J}\!A\hookrightarrow\overline{\mathcal{J}\!A}
and
V\hookrightarrow\overline{V}
.

Proof.

Since
\mathbf{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}\colon\mathsf{CAlg}\to\mathsf{FA}^{\mathsf{loc}}(\mathbb{C})
is a categorical equivalence (
Proposition

2.3.10
), there is a unique morphism
f_{0}\colon A\to V
in
\mathsf{CAlg}
such that
\varphi=\mathbf{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{f_{0}}
. Also, by the universality of jet algebra, there is a unique morphism
f\colon\mathcal{J}\!A\to V
in
\mathsf{grComVA}^{\mathsf{bb},\mathsf{fin}}
such that
f|_{A}=f_{0}
. The morphism
\widetilde{\varphi}\coloneqq\mathbf{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{\overline{f}}\colon\mathbf{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{\overline{\mathcal{J}\!A}}\to\mathbf{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{\overline{V}}
in
\mathsf{HolFA}^{\mathsf{loc}}_{\mathbb{C}}
commutes the diagram in the proposition. To prove the uniqueness, let
\psi\colon\mathbf{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{\overline{\mathcal{J}A}}\to\mathbf{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{\overline{V}}
be a morphism in
\mathsf{HolFA}^{\mathsf{loc}}_{\mathbb{C}}
which commutes the diagram. Then, by the fullness of
\mathbf{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{\overline{(\,\cdot\,)}}
, there exists a morphism
g\colon\mathcal{J}\!A\to V
in
\mathsf{grComVA}^{\mathsf{bb},\mathsf{fin}}
such that
\psi=\mathbf{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{\overline{g}}
. Also, the faithfulness of
\mathbf{F}^{\mathrm{loc}}_{\overline{(\,\cdot\,)}}
and uniqueness of
f
imply
g=f
. Thus, we get
\psi=\widetilde{\varphi}
.
∎

Appendix A
Calculus of functions with values in a locally convex space

In this appendix we develop the calculus of functions with values in a locally convex space to ensure the completeness of this note and to make content more accessible to algebraists. For a locally convex space
E
, we use the following notations:

•

The set
\Gamma
of semi-norms on
E
that defines the topology of
E
is called a fundamental system of semi-norms for
E
.

•

For a semi-norm
p\colon E\to\mathbb{R}
and
t\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
, we denote by
B(p,t)\coloneqq\{x\in E\mid p(x)<t\}
the open ball.

•

For a subset
A\subset E
, we denote by
\conv A
the convex hull.

A.1.
Holomorphic functions

In this subsection we define holomorphic functions with values in a locally convex space. Fix a Hausdorff locally convex space
E
over
\mathbb{C}
.

Let
U\subset\mathbb{C}^{n}
be an open subset. For
a\in U
, the set
U\mathbin{\setminus}\{a\}
is
directed by letting

z\preceq w\ratio\Longleftrightarrow|z-a|\geq|w-a|\quad(z,w\in U\mathbin{\setminus}\{a\}).

Lemma A.1.1
.

Take a fundamental system
\Gamma
of semi-norms for
E
. Let
U\subset\mathbb{C}^{n}
be an open subset and
a\in U
. For a net
\{\xi_{z}\}_{z\in U\mathbin{\setminus}\{a\}}\subset E
and
\xi\in E
, the following (i) and (ii) are equivalent:

1

The net
\{\xi_{z}\}_{z\in U\mathbin{\setminus}\{a\}}
converges to
\xi
.

2

For
p\in\Gamma
and
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
, there is
\delta\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
such that

z\in U\mathbin{\setminus}\{a\},\,|z-a|<\delta\,\Longrightarrow\,p(\xi_{z}-\xi)<\varepsilon.

Proof.

(i)
\Longrightarrow
(ii) is clear since
B(p,\varepsilon)\subset E
is a neighborhood of
0\in E
. Let us prove the converse. Since
\{B(p_{1},\varepsilon_{1})\cap\cdots\cap B(p_{l},\varepsilon_{l})\mid l\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0},p_{i}\in\Gamma,\varepsilon_{i}\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}\}
is a neighborhood basis of
0\in E
, it is enough to show that for
p\in\Gamma
and
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
, there exists
z_{0}\in U\mathbin{\setminus}\{a\}
such that

z\in U\mathbin{\setminus}\{a\},\,z\succeq z_{0}\,\Longrightarrow\,\xi_{z}-\xi\in B(p,\varepsilon).

By the condition (ii), there is some
\delta\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
satisfying
z\in U\mathbin{\setminus}\{a\}
,
|z-a|<\delta

\Longrightarrow

p(\xi_{z}-\xi)<\varepsilon
. Also, since
U\subset\mathbb{C}
is open, there is
\delta^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
satisfying
D_{\delta^{\prime}}(a)\subset U
. Take
z_{0}\in(D_{\delta}(a)\cap D_{\delta^{\prime}}(a))\mathbin{\setminus}\{a\}
, then
z_{0}\in U\mathbin{\setminus}\{a\}
, and
z_{0}
satisfies that
z\in U\mathbin{\setminus}\{a\}
,
z\succeq z_{0}

\Longrightarrow

\xi_{z}-\xi\in B(p,\varepsilon)
.
∎

Remark A.1.2
.

Let
U\subset\mathbb{C}^{n}
be an open subset. By
Lemma

A.1.1
, a function
f\colon U\to E
is continuous at
a\in U
if and only if the net
\{f(z)\}_{z\in U\mathbin{\setminus}\{a\}}
converges to
f(a)
.

Definition A.1.3
.

Let
U\subset\mathbb{C}^{n}
be an open subset, and
f\colon U\to E
a function.

(1)

For
a\in U
, we say that
f
is complex differentiable at
a
, if there exists
\xi\in E^{n}
such that the net

U\mathbin{\setminus}\{a\}\to E,\quad z\mapsto\frac{f(z)-f(a)-(z-a)\cdot{}^{t}\xi}{|z-a|}

converges to
0\in E
. Here we used the matrix notation

z\cdot{}^{t}\xi=\sum_{i=1}^{n}z_{i}\xi_{i}\in E

for
z=(z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})\in\mathbb{C}^{n}
and
\xi=(\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{n})\in E^{n}
.

(2)

We say that
f
is holomorphic on
U
if
f
is complex differentiable for all
z\in U
.

Lemma A.1.4
.

Take a fundamental system
\Gamma
of semi-norms for
E
, and let
U\subset\mathbb{C}^{n}
be an open subset.
A function
f\colon U\to E
is complex differentiable at
a\in U
if and only if there exists
\xi\in E^{n}
satisfying the following condition:
For
p\in\Gamma
and
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
, there exists
\delta\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
such that

z\in U\mathbin{\setminus}\{a\},\,|z-a|<\delta\,\Longrightarrow\,p\Bigl(\frac{f(z)-f(a)-(z-a)\cdot{}^{t}\xi}{|z-a|}\Bigr)<\varepsilon.

Proof.

This follows from
Lemma

A.1.1
.
∎

Lemma A.1.5
.

Let
U\subset\mathbb{C}^{n}
be an open subset. If a function
f\colon U\to E
is complex differentiable at
a\in U
, then an element
\xi\in E^{n}
satisfying the condition in
Definition

A.1.3
is unique.

Proof.

Let
\xi,\xi^{\prime}\in E^{n}
are elements satisfying the condition in
Definition

A.1.3
, and take a fundamental system
\Gamma
of semi-norms for
E
. It is enough to show that
p(\xi_{i}-\xi^{\prime}_{i})=0
for all
p\in\Gamma
and
i\in[n]
. For
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
, there exists
\delta\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
such that
D_{\delta}(a)\subset U
and

z\in\mathbb{C}^{n},\,0<|z-a|<\delta\,\Longrightarrow\,p\Bigl(\frac{f(z)-f(a)-(z-a)\cdot{}^{t}\xi}{|z-a|}\Bigr),\,p\Bigl(\frac{f(z)-f(a)-(z-a)\cdot{}^{t}\xi^{\prime}}{|z-a|}\Bigr)<\frac{\varepsilon}{2}.

Set
z=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{i}+\delta/2,\ldots,a_{n})
, then we get

p(f(z)-f(a)-\xi_{i}),\,p(f(z)-f(a)-\xi^{\prime}_{i})<\varepsilon/2.

Thus, we have
p(\xi_{i}-\xi_{i}^{\prime})<\varepsilon
for any
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
, which shows the claim.
∎

Let
U\subset\mathbb{C}^{n}
be an open subset. If a function
f\colon U\to E
is complex differentiable at
a\in U
, the element

f^{\prime}(a)\coloneqq{}^{t}\xi,

where
\xi\in E^{n}
is the element satisfying the condition in
Definition

A.1.3
(1), is called the derivative of
f
at
a
.

For a semi-norm
p
, define

p_{n}\colon E^{n}\to\mathbb{R},\quad\xi\mapsto\Bigl(\sum_{i=1}^{n}p(\xi_{i})^{2}\Bigr)^{1/2},

then
p_{n}
is a semi-norm on
E^{n}
. Note that this semi-norm satisfies

p(z\cdot{}^{t}\xi)\leq|z|p_{n}(\xi)

for all
z\in\mathbb{C}^{n}
and
\xi\in E^{n}
.

Lemma A.1.6
.

Let
U\subset\mathbb{C}^{n}
be an open subset, and
f\colon U\to E
a function. If
f
is complex differentiable at
a\in U
, then
f
is continuous at
a
.

Proof.

It is enough to show that for
p\in\Gamma
and
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
, there exists
\delta\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
satisfying

z\in U\mathbin{\setminus}\{a\},\,|z-a|<\delta\,\Longrightarrow\,p(f(z)-f(a))<\varepsilon.

Since
f
is complex differentiable at
a
, there is some
\delta_{0}\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
such that

z\in U\mathbin{\setminus}\{a\},\,|z-a|<\delta_{0}\,\Longrightarrow\,p\Bigl(\frac{f(z)-f(a)-(z-a)\cdot f^{\prime}(a)}{|z-a|}\Bigr)<1.

Hence, if
z\in U\mathbin{\setminus}\{a\}
satisfies
|z-a|<\delta_{0}
, we have

p(f(z)-f(a))<p((z-a)\cdot f^{\prime}(a))+|z-a|\leq(p_{n}(f^{\prime}(a))+1)|z-a|.

Thus
