\delta\coloneqq\min\{\delta_{0},\varepsilon/(p_{n}(f^{\prime}(a))+1)\}\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
satisfies the claim.
∎

Definition A.1.7
.

Let
U\subset\mathbb{C}^{n}
be an open subset, and
f\colon U\to E
a function. For
i\in[n]
and
a=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})\in U
, we say that
f
is complex partial differentiable at
a
with respect to the
i
-coordinate if the function

U_{i}\mapsto E,\quad z\mapsto f(a_{1},\ldots,a_{i-1},z,a_{i+1},\ldots,a_{n})

is complex differentiable at
a_{i}
, where

U_{i}\coloneqq\{z\in\mathbb{C}\mid(a_{1},\ldots,a_{i-1},z,a_{i+1},\ldots a_{n})\in U\}.

In this case, we denote the derivative of this function at
a_{i}
by

\partial_{i}f(a)=\partial_{z_{i}}f(z)|_{z=a}\coloneqq\lim_{z\in U_{i}\mathbin{\setminus}\{a_{i}\}}\frac{f(a_{1},\ldots,a_{i-1},z,a_{i+1},\ldots,a_{n})-f(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})}{z-a_{i}}\in E.

Lemma A.1.8
.

Let
U\subset\mathbb{C}^{n}
be an open subset, and
f\colon U\to E
a function. If
f
is complex differentiable at
a\in U
, then
f
is complex partial differentiable at
a
with respect to all the
i
-coordinates. Moreover, we have

f^{\prime}(a)={}^{t}(\partial_{1}f(a),\ldots,\partial_{n}f(a)).

Proof.

Take a fundamental system
\Gamma
of semi-norms for
E
. For
p\in\Gamma
and
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
, since
f
is complex differentiable at
a
, there exists
\delta\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
such that

z\in U\mathbin{\setminus}\{a\},\,|z-a|<\delta\,\Longrightarrow\,p\Bigl(\frac{f(z)-f(a)-(z-a)\cdot f^{\prime}(a)}{|z-a|}\Bigr)<\varepsilon

Now, let us denote
f^{\prime}(a)={}^{t}(\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{n})
. For
i\in[n]
, set
U_{i}\coloneqq\{z\in\mathbb{C}\mid(a_{1},\ldots,a_{i-1},z,a_{i+1},\ldots,a_{n})\in U\}
, then

z\in U_{i}\mathbin{\setminus}\{a_{i}\},\,|z-a_{i}|<\delta\,\Longrightarrow\,p\Bigl(\frac{f(a_{1},\ldots,a_{i-1},z,a_{i+1},\ldots,a_{n})-f(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})}{z-a_{i}}-\xi_{i}\Bigr)<\varepsilon,

which shows
\partial_{i}f(a)=\xi_{i}
.
∎

Lemma A.1.9
.

Let
U\subset\mathbb{C}^{n}
be an open subset. If functions
\varphi\colon U\to\mathbb{C}
and
f\colon U\to E
are complex differentiable at
a\in U
, then the function

\varphi f\colon U\to E,\quad z\mapsto\varphi(z)f(z)

is also complex differentiable at
a
. Moreover, we have

(\varphi f)(a)=\varphi^{\prime}(a)f(a)+\varphi(a)f^{\prime}(a),

or equivalently,

\partial_{i}(\varphi f)(a)=(\partial_{i}\varphi(a))f(a)+\varphi(a)(\partial_{i}f(a)).

Proof.

For
z\in U\mathbin{\setminus}\{a\}
,

\displaystyle\frac{(\varphi f)(z)-(\varphi f)(a)-(z-a)\cdot(\varphi^{\prime}(a)f(a)+\varphi(a)f^{\prime}(a))}{|z-a|}

\displaystyle=\frac{(\varphi(z)-\varphi(a)-(z-a)\cdot\varphi^{\prime}(a))f(z)}{|z-a|}+\frac{\varphi(a)(f(z)-f(a)-(z-a)\cdot f^{\prime}(a))}{|z-a|}+\frac{(z-a)\cdot\varphi^{\prime}(a)(f(z)-f(a))}{|z-a|}.

Denote by
F_{1}(z)
(resp.
F_{2}(z)
,
F_{3}(z)
) the first (resp. second, third) term on the right hand side of this equation. It is enough to show that
\lim_{z\in U\mathbin{\setminus}\{a\}}F_{1}(z)=\lim_{z\in U\mathbin{\setminus}\{a\}}F_{2}(z)=\lim_{z\in U\mathbin{\setminus}\{a\}}F_{3}(z)=0
. Take a fundamental system
\Gamma
of semi-norms for
E
.

The equation
\lim_{z\in U\mathbin{\setminus}\{a\}}F_{2}(z)=0
is clear by the differentiability of
f
. Also
\lim_{z\in U\mathbin{\setminus}\{a\}}F_{3}(z)=0
follows from the continuity of
f
. In fact, for
p\in\Gamma
, we have

p(F_{3}(z))\leq p_{n}\bigl(\varphi^{\prime}(a)(f(z)-f(a))\bigr)\leq\Bigl(\sum_{i=1}^{n}|\partial_{i}\varphi(a)|^{2}p(f(z)-f(a))^{2}\Bigr)^{1/2}.

We prove
\lim_{z\in U\mathbin{\setminus}\{a\}}F_{1}(z)=0
. Let
p\in\Gamma
and
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
. Since
\varphi
is differentiable at
a
, there exists some
\delta_{1}\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
such that

z\in U\mathbin{\setminus}\{a\},\,|z-a|<\delta_{1}\,\Longrightarrow\,\frac{|\varphi(z)-\varphi(a)-(z-a)\cdot\varphi^{\prime}(a)|}{|z-a|}<\frac{\varepsilon}{1+p(f(a))}.

Also, since
f
is continuous at
a
, there exists
\delta_{2}\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
such that

z\in U,\,|z-a|<\delta_{2}\,\Longrightarrow\,p(f(z))<1+p(f(a)).

Set
\delta\coloneqq\{\delta_{1},\delta_{2}\}\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
, then for
z\in U\mathbin{\setminus}\{a\}
satisfying
|z-a|<\delta
, we have

p(F_{1}(z))=\frac{|\varphi(z)-\varphi(a)-(z-a)\cdot\varphi^{\prime}(a)|}{|z-a|}p(f(z))<\varepsilon,

which shows
\lim_{z\in U\mathbin{\setminus}\{a\}}F_{1}(z)=0
.
∎

Lemma A.1.10
.

Let
U\subset\mathbb{C}^{m}
and
V\subset\mathbb{C}^{n}
be open subsets. For functions
\varphi\colon U\to V
and
f\colon V\to E
, if
\varphi
is complex differentiable at
a\in U
, and
f
is complex differentiable at
\varphi(a)\in V
, then the function
\varphi\circ f\colon U\to E
is complex differentiable at
a
. Moreover, we have

(f\circ\varphi)^{\prime}(a)=\varphi^{\prime}(a)f^{\prime}(\varphi(a)),

or equivalently,

\partial_{i}(f\circ\varphi)(a)=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\partial_{i}\varphi_{j}(a)\cdot\partial_{j}f(\varphi(a))\quad(i\in[n]).

Here we denote
\varphi=(\varphi_{1},\ldots,\varphi_{n})
.

Proof.

For
z\in U\mathbin{\setminus}\{a\}
,

\displaystyle\frac{(f\circ\varphi)(z)-(f\circ\varphi)(a)-(z-a)\cdot\varphi^{\prime}(a)f^{\prime}(\varphi(a))}{|z-a|}

\displaystyle=\frac{f(\varphi(z))-f(\varphi(a))-(\varphi(z)-\varphi(a))\cdot f^{\prime}(\varphi(a))}{|z-a|}+\frac{(\varphi(z)-\varphi(a)-(z-a)\cdot\varphi^{\prime}(a))\cdot f^{\prime}(\varphi(a))}{|z-a|}.

Denote by
F_{1}(z)
(resp.
F_{2}(z)
) the first (resp. second) term on the right hand side of this equation. It is enough to show that
\lim_{z\in U\mathbin{\setminus}\{a\}}F_{1}(z)=\lim_{z\in U\mathbin{\setminus}\{a\}}F_{2}(z)=0
. Take a fundamental system
\Gamma
of semi-norms for
E
.

The equation
\lim_{z\in U\mathbin{\setminus}\{a\}}F_{2}(z)=0
follows from differentiability of
\varphi
at
a
. In fact, for
p\in\Gamma
, we have

p(F_{2}(z))\leq\frac{|\varphi(z)-\varphi(a)-(z-a)\cdot\varphi^{\prime}(a)|}{|z-a|}p_{n}(f^{\prime}(\varphi(a))).

We prove
\lim_{z\in U\mathbin{\setminus}\{a\}}F_{1}(z)=0
. Define a function
\widetilde{F}_{1}
by

\widetilde{F}_{1}\colon U\to E,\quad z\mapsto\begin{cases}\displaystyle\frac{f(\varphi(z))-f(\varphi(a))-(\varphi(z)-\varphi(a))\cdot f^{\prime}(\varphi(a))}{|\varphi(z)-\varphi(a)|}&(\varphi(z)\neq\varphi(a))\\
0&(\varphi(z)=\varphi(a)),\end{cases}

then we have

F_{1}(z)=\frac{|\varphi(z)-\varphi(a)|}{|z-a|}\widetilde{F}_{1}(z)\quad(z\in U\mathbin{\setminus}\{a\}).

Since
\varphi
is differentiable at
a
, there is
\delta_{1}\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
satisfying

z\in U\{a\},\,|z-a|<\delta_{1}\,\Longrightarrow\frac{|\varphi(z)-\varphi(a)|}{|z-a|}<1+|\varphi^{\prime}(a)|.

Here
|\varphi^{\prime}(a)|
denotes the matrix norm defined by

|A|\coloneqq\Bigl(\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sum_{j=1}^{n}|a_{i,j}|^{2}\Bigr)^{1/2}\quad(A=\{a_{i,j}\}_{i=1,j=1}^{m,n}).

Now, let
p\in\Gamma
and
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
. Since
f
is differentiable at
\varphi(a)
, there exists
\delta_{2}\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
such that

w\in V\mathbin{\setminus}\{\varphi(a)\},\,|w-\varphi(a)|<\delta_{2}\,\Longrightarrow\,p\Bigl(\frac{f(w)-f(\varphi(a))-(w-\varphi(a))\cdot f^{\prime}(\varphi(a))}{|w-\varphi(a)|}\Bigr)<\frac{\varepsilon}{1+|\varphi^{\prime}(a)|}.

Also, since
\varphi\colon U\to V
is continuous at
a
, there exists
\delta_{2}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
such that

z\in U,\,|z-a|<\delta_{2}^{\prime}\,\Longrightarrow\,|\varphi(z)-\varphi(a)|<\delta_{2}.

Set
\delta\coloneqq\min\{\delta_{1},\delta_{2}^{\prime}\}\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
, then for
z\in U\mathbin{\setminus}\{a\}
satisfying
|z-a|<\delta
, we have

p(F_{1}(z))\leq(1+|\varphi(a)|)p(\widetilde{F}_{1}(z))<\varepsilon,

which shows
\lim_{z\in U\mathbin{\setminus}\{a\}}F_{1}(z)=0
.
∎

A.2.
Uniform convergence

Let
X
be a set, and
E
a locally convex space over
\mathbb{C}
.
For a semi-norm
p\colon E\to\mathbb{R}
and a map
f\colon X\to E
, define

p_{X}(f)\coloneqq\sup\{p(f(x))\mid x\in X\}\in[0,\infty].

Definition A.2.1
.

For a net
\{f_{\lambda}\}_{\lambda\in\Lambda}
consisting of maps
f_{\lambda}\colon X\to E
and a map
f\colon X\to E
, we say that
\{f_{\lambda}\}_{\lambda\in\Lambda}
converges uniformly to
f
if the following condition holds:
For any
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
and
p\in\Gamma
, where
\Gamma
is a fundamental system of semi-norms for
E
, there exists
\lambda_{0}\in\Lambda
such that

\lambda\in\Lambda,\ \lambda\geq\lambda_{0}\ \Longrightarrow\ q_{X}(f_{\lambda}-f)<\varepsilon.

Note that this definition of uniform convergence is independent of the choice of
\Gamma
.

Lemma A.2.2
.

Let
X
be a set, and
E
a quasi-complete locally convex space.
For a sequence
\{f_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of maps
f_{n}\colon X\to E
, the following conditions are equivalent:

1

The sequence
\{f_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
converges uniformly.

2

For any
p\in\Gamma
and
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
, where
\Gamma
is a fundamental system of semi-norms for
E
, there exists
n_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
such that

m,n\in\mathbb{N},\,m,n\geq n_{0}\,\Longrightarrow\,p_{X}(f_{m}-f_{n})<\varepsilon.

Proof.

(i)
\Longrightarrow
(ii) is easy to see. We prove the converse. For
x\in X
, the condition (ii) implies that
\{f_{n}(x)\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is a bounded Cauchy sequence in
E
. Thus, by the quasi-completeness of
E
, there exists a limit point of
\{f_{n}(x)\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
. For
x\in X
, choose a limit point
f(x)
of
\{f_{n}(x)\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
, then we have a map
f\colon X\to E
. We claim that
\{f_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
converges uniformly to
f
.
Let
p\in\Gamma
and
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
. By the condition (ii), there exists
n_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
such that

m,n\in\mathbb{N},\,m,n\geq n_{0}\ \Longrightarrow\,p(f_{m}(x)-f_{n}(x))<\varepsilon/2\quad(x\in X).

Since
q\colon E\to\mathbb{R}
is continuous, we have by letting
\mu\to\infty

m\in\mathbb{N},\,m\geq n_{0}\ \Longrightarrow\ p(f_{m}(x)-f(x))\leq\varepsilon/2\quad(x\in X),

which yields that

m\in\mathbb{N},\,m\geq n_{0}\ \Longrightarrow\ p_{X}(f_{m}-f)\leq\varepsilon/2<\varepsilon.\qed

Proposition A.2.3
.

Let
X
be a set,
E
a quasi-complete locally convex space, and
\{f_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
a sequence of maps
f_{n}\colon X\to E
. For each
p\in\Gamma
, where
\Gamma
is a fundamental system of semi-norms for
E
, if there exists a sequence
\{M_{p}(n)\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of non-negative real numbers satisfying the following conditions (i) and (ii), then
\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}f_{n}
converges uniformly.

1

For any
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we have
p_{X}(f_{n})\leq M_{p}(n)
.

2

The series
\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}M_{p}(n)
converges.

Proof.

For
n\in\mathbb{N}
, set
s_{n}\coloneqq\sum_{k=0}^{n}f_{k}
. It is enough to show that
\{s_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
satisfies the condition (ii) in
Lemma

A.2.2
. Let
p\in\Gamma
and
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
. Since
\{t_{n}\coloneqq\sum_{k=0}^{n}M_{p}(k)\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is a Cauchy sequence, there exists
n_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
such that

m,n\in\mathbb{N},\ m,n\geq n_{0}\ \Longrightarrow\ |t_{m}-t_{n}|<\varepsilon.

Thus, if
m,n\in\mathbb{N}
,
n>m\geq n_{0}
, then

p_{X}(s_{m}-s_{n})\leq\sum_{k=m+1}^{n}p_{X}(f_{k})\leq\sum_{k=m+1}^{n}t_{p}(k)=|t_{m}-t_{n}|<\varepsilon.\qed

Now, fix a topological space
X
and a locally convex space
E
.

Definition A.2.4
.

For a net
\{f_{\lambda}\}_{\lambda\in\Lambda}
consisting of maps
f_{\lambda}\colon X\to E
and a map
f\colon X\to E
, we say that
\{f_{\lambda}\}_{\lambda\in\Lambda}
converges locally uniformly to
f
if every point
x\in X
has a neighborhood
N\subset X
such that
\{f_{\lambda}|_{N}\}_{\lambda\in\Lambda}
converges uniformly to
f|_{N}
.

Proposition A.2.5
.

Let
\{f_{\lambda}\}_{\lambda\in\Lambda}
be a net consisting of continuous maps
f_{\lambda}\colon X\to E
, and
f\colon E\to X
be a map. If
\{f_{\lambda}\}_{\lambda\in\Lambda}
converges locally uniformly to
f
, then
f\colon X\to E
is continuous.

Proof.

Take a directed fundamental system
\Gamma
of semi-norms for
X
, and fix
x\in X
. It is enough to show that for
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
and
p\in\Gamma
, there is a neighborhood
N\subset X
of
x\in X
such that
f(N)\subset f(x)+B(p,\varepsilon)
. Since
\{f_{\lambda}\}_{\lambda\in\Lambda}
converges locally uniformly to
f
, there exists a neighborhood
M\subset X
of
x\in X
and
\lambda_{0}\in\Lambda
such that

\lambda\in\Lambda,\ \lambda\geq\lambda_{0}\ \Longrightarrow\ p_{M}(f_{\lambda}-f)<\varepsilon/3.

Also, since
f_{\lambda_{0}}
is continuous, one can find a neighborhood
M^{\prime}\subset X
of
x\in X
such that
f_{\lambda_{0}}(M^{\prime})\subset f_{\lambda_{0}}(x)+B(p,\varepsilon/3)
. Thus, for
y\in M\cap M^{\prime}
,

p(f(y)-f(x))\leq p(f(y)-f_{\lambda_{0}}(y))+p(f_{\lambda_{0}}(y)-f_{\lambda_{0}}(x))+p(f_{\lambda_{0}}(x)-f(x))<\varepsilon,

which means that
f(M\cap M^{\prime})\subset f(x)+B(p,\varepsilon)
.
∎

A.3.
Riemann integrals

To define complex integrals of functions with values in a locally convex space, we need to expand the notion of Riemann integrals to locally convex settings.

Fix a Hausdorff locally convex space
E
over
\mathbb{R}
. For a bounded closed interval
I=[a,b]
(
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
,
a<b
), we denote by

D(I)\coloneqq\{(x_{i})_{i=0}^{l}\mid l\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0},\,a=x_{0}<x_{1}<\cdots<x_{l}=b\}

the set of partitions of
I
. For a partition
\Delta=\{x_{i}\}_{i=0}^{l}\in D(I)
, we use the following notations:

•

We call
l(\Delta)\coloneqq l
the length of
\Delta
.

•

For
i\in[l]
, set
\Delta_{i}\coloneqq x_{i}-x_{i-1}
. We call
|\Delta|\coloneqq\max\{\Delta_{i}\mid i\in[l]\}
the width of
\Delta
.

•

For
i\in[l]
, set
I_{i}(\Delta)\coloneqq[x_{i-1},x_{i}]
. We call an element
\{\xi_{i}\}_{i=1}^{l}\in\prod_{i=1}^{l}I_{i}(\Delta)
a representative of
\Delta
.

A pair
(\Delta,\xi)
of a partition
\Delta\in D(I)
and its representative is called a tagged partition. We denote by
\widetilde{D}(I)
the set of tagged partitions. This set
\widetilde{D}(I)
has the following two directed orders:

•

The directed order
\leq
defined by widths:

(\Delta,\xi)\leq(\Delta^{\prime},\xi^{\prime})\ratio\Longleftrightarrow|\Delta|\geq|\Delta^{\prime}|.

•

The directed order
\subset
defined by inclusions:

(\Delta,\xi)\subset(\Delta^{\prime},\xi^{\prime})\ratio\Longleftrightarrow\Delta\subset\Delta^{\prime}.

Definition A.3.1
.

Let
I\subset\mathbb{R}
be a bounded closed interval, and
f\colon I\to E
a function.

(1)

For a tagged partition
(\Delta,\xi)\in\widetilde{D}(I)
,

s_{f}(\Delta,\xi)\coloneqq\sum_{i=1}^{l(\Delta)}\Delta_{i}f(\xi_{i})\in E

is called a Riemann sum of
f
.

(2)

We say that
f
is (Riemann) integrable over
I
if the net

(\widetilde{D}(I),\leq)\to E,\quad(\Delta,\xi)\mapsto s_{f}(\Delta,\xi)

converges. In this case, we denote

\int_{I}f=\int_{I}f(x)dx\coloneqq\lim_{(\Delta,\xi)\in\widetilde{D}(I)}s_{f}(\Delta,\xi).

Remark A.3.2
.

Take a fundamental system
\Gamma
of semi-norms for
E
, and let
I\subset\mathbb{R}
be a bounded closed interval. A function
f\colon I\to E
is integrable over
I
if and only if there exists
s\in E
satisfying the following condition: For
p\in\Gamma
and
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
, there is
\delta\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
such that

(\Delta,\xi)\in\widetilde{D}(I),\,|\Delta|<\delta\,\Longrightarrow\,p(s_{f}(\Delta,\xi)-s)<\varepsilon.

Lemma A.3.3
.

Let
I\subset\mathbb{R}
be a closed interval, and
f\colon I\to E
a function. If the net

(\widetilde{D}(I),\subset)\to E,\quad(\Delta,\xi)\mapsto s_{f}(\Delta,\xi)

converges, then
f
is a bounded function, i.e.,
f(I)\subset E
is bounded.

Proof.

Take a fundamental system
\Gamma
of semi-norms for
E
. It is enough to show that for
p\in\Gamma
, there exists
t\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
such that
f(I)\subset B(p,t)
. By assumption, we have
s\in E
and
\Delta^{0}\in D(I)
satisfying

(\Delta,\xi)\in\widetilde{D}(I),\,\Delta\supset\Delta^{0}\,\Longrightarrow\,p(s_{f}(\Delta,\xi)-s)<1.

Denote
\Delta^{0}=\{x_{i}\}_{i=0}^{l}
, and set
\delta\coloneqq\min\{\Delta^{0}_{i}\mid i\in[l]\}\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
. Let us prove that

t\coloneqq\delta^{-1}\bigl(1+p(s)+\sum_{i=1}^{l}\Delta^{0}_{i}p(f(x_{i}))\bigr)\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}

satisfies
f(I)\subset B(p,t)
.

Let
x\in I
, and take
j\in[l]
such that
x\in I_{j}(\Delta^{0})
. For each
i\in[l]
, set

\xi_{i}\coloneqq\begin{cases}x_{i}&(i\neq j)\\
x&(i=j),\end{cases}

then
\xi\coloneqq\{\xi_{i}\}_{i=1}^{l}
is a representative of
\Delta^{0}
. Thus, we have

p(s_{f}(\Delta^{0},\xi))=p(s_{f}(\Delta^{0},\xi)-s+s)<1+p(s).

Since
s_{f}(\Delta^{0},\xi)=\Delta^{0}_{j}f(x)+\sum_{i=1,i\neq j}^{l}\Delta^{0}_{i}f(x_{i})
, we get

\delta p(f(x))\leq\Delta^{0}_{j}p(f(x))\leq p(s_{f}(\Delta^{0},\xi))+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i=1\\
i\neq j\end{subarray}}^{l}\Delta_{i}^{0}p(f(x_{i}))<1+p(s)+\sum_{i=1}^{l}\Delta^{0}_{i}p(f(x_{i})),

which proves
f(x)\in B(p,t)
.
∎

Proposition A.3.4
.

Let
I\subset\mathbb{R}
be a closed interval, and
f\colon I\to E
a function. The followings are equivalent:

1

The function
f
is integrable over
I
.

2

The net

(\widetilde{D}(I),\subset)\to E,\quad(\Delta,\xi)\mapsto s_{f}(\Delta,\xi)

converges.

Proof.

(i)
\Longrightarrow
(ii) is clear since
\Delta\subset\Delta^{\prime}
implies
|\Delta|\geq|\Delta^{\prime}|
. Let us prove the converse. Take a fundamental system
\Gamma
of semi-norms for
E
, and denote by
s
the limit point of the net
(\widetilde{D}(I),\subset)\to E
,
(\Delta,\xi)\mapsto s_{f}(\Delta,\xi)
. It is enough to show that for
p\in\Gamma
and
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
, there exists
\delta\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
such that

(\Delta,\xi)\in\widetilde{D}(I),\,\Delta<\delta\,\Longrightarrow\,p(s_{f}(\Delta,\xi)-s)<\varepsilon.

By assumption, there is
\Delta^{0}\in D(I)
satisfying

(\Delta,\xi)\in\widetilde{D}(I),\,\Delta\supset\Delta^{0}\,\Longrightarrow\,p(s_{f}(\Delta,\xi)-s)<\varepsilon/2.

Note that by
Lemma

A.3.3
, we have
a\coloneqq\sup_{x,y\in I}p(f(x)-f(y))\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
. We prove that

\delta\coloneqq\min\{\Delta^{0}_{i},\varepsilon/2l(\Delta^{0})(a+1)\mid i\in[l(\Delta^{0})]\}\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}

satisfies the condition in the claim.

For
(\Delta,\xi)\in\widetilde{D}(I)
such that
|\Delta|<\delta
, set
\Delta^{\prime}\coloneqq\Delta^{0}\cup\Delta
, and denote

\Delta^{0}=\{x_{i}\}_{i=0}^{l},\quad\Delta=\{y_{j}\}_{j=0}^{m},\quad\Delta^{\prime}=\{z_{k}\}_{k=0}^{n}.

For each
i\in\{0,1,\ldots,l\}
(resp.
j\in\{0,1,\ldots,m\}
), there exists a unique element
p_{i}\in\{0,1,\ldots,n\}
(
q_{j}\in\{0,1,\ldots,n\}
) satisfying
z_{p_{i}}=x_{i}
(resp.
z_{q_{j}}=y_{j}
). Set

M\coloneqq\{j\in[m]\mid\forall i\in[l],\,x_{i}\notin\mathring{I}_{j}(\Delta)\},\quad N\coloneqq\{k\in[n]\mid\exists j\in[m],\,z_{k}=y_{j}\land z_{k-1}=y_{j-1}\},

where
\mathring{I}_{j}(\Delta)
stands for the interior of
I_{j}(\Delta)\subset\mathbb{R}
, then
M\to N
,
j\mapsto q_{j}
is a bijection. In particular, one can define a representative
\xi^{\prime}=\{\xi^{\prime}_{k}\}_{k=1}^{n}
of
\Delta^{\prime}
by

\xi^{\prime}_{k}\coloneqq\begin{cases}\xi_{j}&(k\in N\text{ with }j\in M,k=q_{j})\\
z_{k}&(k\in[n]\mathbin{\setminus}N).\end{cases}

Since
\Delta^{\prime}\supset\Delta^{0}
, we have

p(s_{f}(\Delta^{\prime},\xi^{\prime})-s)<\varepsilon/2.

Thus, it suffices to prove that
p(s_{f}(\Delta,\xi)-s_{f}(\Delta^{\prime},\xi^{\prime}))<\varepsilon/2
.

By the bijectively of
M\to N
,
j\mapsto q_{j}
and the definition of
\xi^{\prime}
, note that

\displaystyle s_{f}(\Delta,\xi)-s_{f}(\Delta^{\prime},\xi^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\sum_{j\in M}(y_{j}-y_{j-1})f(\xi_{j})+\sum_{j\in[m]\mathbin{\setminus}M}(y_{j}-y_{j-1})f(\xi_{j})

\displaystyle\quad-\sum_{k\in N}(z_{k}-z_{k-1})f(\xi_{k}^{\prime})-\sum_{k\in[n]\mathbin{\setminus}N}(z_{k}-z_{k-1})f(\xi_{k}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\sum_{j\in[m]\mathbin{\setminus}M}(y_{i}-y_{j-1})f(\xi_{j})-\sum_{k\in[n]\mathbin{\setminus}N}(z_{k}-z_{k-1})f(z_{k}).

For
j\in[m]\mathbin{\setminus}M
, since
|\Delta|<\delta\leq\min\{\Delta^{0}_{i}\mid i\in[l]\}
, there exists a unique element
i_{j}\in[l]
satisfying
x_{i_{j}}\in\mathring{I}_{j}(\Delta)
. Hence, we have a bijection

[m]\mathbin{\setminus}M\to\{i\in[l]\mid\exists j\in[m],\,x_{i}\in\mathring{I}_{j}(\Delta)\},\quad j\mapsto i_{j}.

Set

N_{1}\coloneqq\{k\in[n]\mid\forall j\in[m],\,z_{k}\neq y_{j}\},\quad N_{2}\coloneqq\{k\in[n]\mid\exists j\in[m],\,z_{k}=y_{j}\land z_{k-1}\neq y_{j-1}\}

then
[n]\mathbin{\setminus}N=N_{1}\sqcup N_{2}
, and both

[m]\mathbin{\setminus}M\to N_{1},\quad j\mapsto p_{i_{j}},\quad[m]\mathbin{\setminus}M\to N_{2},\quad j\mapsto q_{j}

are bijective. Thus,

\displaystyle\sum_{k\in[n]\mathbin{\setminus}N}(z_{k}-z_{k-1})f(z_{k})

\displaystyle=\sum_{j\in[m]\mathbin{\setminus}M}(z_{p_{i_{j}}}-z_{p_{i_{j}}-1})f(z_{p_{i_{j}}})+\sum_{j\in[m]\mathbin{\setminus}M}(z_{q_{j}}-z_{q_{j}-1})f(z_{q_{j}})

\displaystyle=\sum_{j\in[m]\mathbin{\setminus}M}(x_{i_{j}}-y_{j-1})f(x_{i_{j}})+\sum_{j\in[m]\mathbin{\setminus}M}(y_{j}-x_{i_{j}})f(y_{j}),

and hence

s_{f}(\Delta,\xi)-s_{f}(\Delta^{\prime},\xi^{\prime})=\sum_{j\in[m]\mathbin{\setminus}M}(y_{j}-x_{i_{j}})(f(\xi_{j})-f(y_{j}))+\sum_{j\in[m]\mathbin{\setminus}M}(x_{i_{j}}-y_{j-1})(f(\xi_{j})-f(x_{i_{j}})).

Therefore, by the injectively of
[m]\mathbin{\setminus}M\to[l]
,
j\mapsto i_{j}
and
|\Delta|<\delta\leq\varepsilon/2l(a+1)
, we have

p(s_{f}(\Delta,\xi)-s_{f}(\Delta^{\prime},\xi^{\prime}))\leq\sum_{j\in[m]\mathbin{\setminus}M}(y_{j}-y_{j-1})a\leq\#([m]\mathbin{\setminus}M)|\Delta|a<\varepsilon/2.\qed

Lemma A.3.5
.

Let
I\subset\mathbb{R}
be a bounded closed interval. For a bounded function
f\colon I\to E
, the set
\{s_{f}(\Delta,\xi)\mid(\Delta,\xi)\in\widetilde{D}(I)\}\subset E
is bounded.

Proof.

Take a fundamental system
\Gamma
of semi-norms for
E
, and let
p\in\Gamma
. For
(\Delta,\xi)\in\widetilde{D}(I)
, we have

p(s_{f}(\Delta,\xi))\leq\sum_{i=1}^{l(\Delta)}\Delta p(f(\xi_{i}))\leq\bigl(\sum_{i=1}^{l(\Delta)}\Delta_{i}\bigr)\sup_{x\in I}p(f(x))=(b-a)\sup_{x\in I}p(f(x)),

which proves the lemma.
∎

The proof of the following proposition based on that of
[
WYY19
, Proposition 2.2]
.

Proposition A.3.6
.

Let
E
be a quasi-complete Hausdorff locally convex space, and take a fundamental system
\Gamma
of semi-norms for
E
. For a bounded closed interval
I\subset\mathbb{R}
and a function
f\colon I\to E
, the following conditions are equivalent:

1

The function
f
is integrable over
I
.

2

For
p\in\Gamma
and
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
, there exists
\delta\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
such that

(\Delta,\xi),(\Delta^{\prime},\xi^{\prime})\in\widetilde{D}(I),\,|\Delta|,|\Delta^{\prime}|<\delta\,\Longrightarrow\,p(s_{f}(\Delta,\xi)-s_{f}(\Delta^{\prime},\xi^{\prime}))<\varepsilon.

3

For
p\in\Gamma
and
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
, there exists
\Delta_{0}\in D(I)
such that

(\Delta,\xi),(\Delta^{\prime},\xi^{\prime})\in\widetilde{D}(I),\,\Delta,\Delta^{\prime}\supset\Delta^{0}\,\Longrightarrow\,p(s_{f}(\Delta,\xi)-s_{f}(\Delta^{\prime},\xi^{\prime}))<\varepsilon.

4

For
p\in\Gamma
and
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
, there exists
\Delta^{0}\in D(I)
such that if
\xi
and
\xi^{\prime}
are representatives of
\Delta^{0}
, then

p(s_{f}(\Delta^{0},\xi)-s_{f}(\Delta^{0},\xi^{\prime}))<\varepsilon.

Proof.

(i)
\Longleftrightarrow
(ii): The condition (ii) means that the net
(\widetilde{D}(I),\leq)\to E
,
(\Delta,\xi)\mapsto s_{f}(\Delta,\xi)
is a Cauchy net. Thus, this follows from
Lemma

A.3.5
and the quasi-completeness of
E
.

(i)
\Longleftrightarrow
(iii): The condition (iii) means that the net
(\widetilde{D}(I),\subset)\to E
,
(\Delta,\xi)\mapsto s_{f}(\Delta,\xi)
is a Cauchy net. Thus, this follows from
Proposition

A.3.4
,
Lemma

A.3.5
and the quasi-completeness of
E
.

(iii)
\Longrightarrow
(iv): This is trivial.

(iv)
\Longrightarrow
(iii):
For
p\in\Gamma
and
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
, there exists
\Delta^{0}\in D(I)
such that if
\xi
and
\xi^{\prime}
are representative of
\Delta^{0}
, then
p(s_{f}(\Delta^{0},\xi)-s_{f}(\Delta^{0},\xi^{\prime}))<\varepsilon/2
. Fix a representative
\xi^{0}\coloneqq\{\xi^{0}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{l}
of
\Delta^{0}
. It is enough to show that

(\Delta,\xi)\in\widetilde{D}(I),\,\Delta\supset\Delta^{0}\,\Longrightarrow\,p(s_{f}(\Delta,\xi)-s_{f}(\Delta^{0},\xi^{0}))<\varepsilon/2.

First, set

A\coloneqq\sum_{i=1}^{l}A_{i},\quad A_{i}\coloneqq\{\Delta^{0}_{i}f(x)\mid x\in I_{i}(\Delta^{0})\},

then clearly, we have
p(a-b)<\varepsilon/2
for
a,b\in A
. Hence, it follows that
p(a)<\varepsilon/2
for all
a\in\conv(A-A)
. Now, take
(\Delta,\xi)\in\widetilde{D}(I)
such that
\Delta\supset\Delta^{0}
, and denote

\Delta^{0}=\{x_{i}\}_{i=0}^{l},\quad\Delta=\{y_{j}\}_{j=0}^{m}.

For each
i\in[l]
, there is a unique element
j_{i}\in[m]
such that
y_{j_{i}}=x_{i}
.
Since
[m]=\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{l}\{j\in[m]\mid j_{i-1}<j\leq j_{i}\}
, we have

\displaystyle s_{f}(\Delta,\xi)-s_{f}(\Delta^{0},\xi^{0})

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{m}\Delta_{j}f(\xi_{j})-\sum_{i=1}^{l}\Delta_{i}^{0}f(\xi_{i}^{0})=\sum_{i=1}^{l}\sum_{j_{i-1}<j\leq j_{i}}\Delta_{j}f(\xi_{j})-\sum_{i=1}^{l}\Delta_{i}^{0}f(\xi_{i}^{0})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{l}\sum_{j_{i-1}<j\leq j_{i}}\frac{\Delta_{j}}{\Delta_{i}^{0}}(\Delta_{i}^{0}f(\xi_{j})-\Delta_{i}^{0}f(\xi_{i}^{0})).

Thus,

s_{f}(\Delta,\xi)-s_{f}(\Delta^{0},\xi^{0})\in\sum_{i=1}^{l}\conv(A_{i}-A_{i})=\conv\bigl(\sum_{i=1}^{l}(A_{i}-A_{i})\bigr)\subset\conv(A-A).

Now the claim is clear.
∎

Let
I\subset\mathbb{R}
be a bounded closed interval. For a bounded function
f\colon I\to E
, a semi-norm
p\colon E\to\mathbb{R}
and
\Delta\in D(I)
, define

a_{f}(p,\Delta)\coloneqq\sum_{i=1}^{l(\Delta)}\Delta_{i}\sup_{x,y\in I_{i}(\Delta)}p(f(x)-f(y))\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}.

Lemma A.3.7
.

Let
E
be a quasi-complete Hausdorff locally convex space, and take a fundamental system
\Gamma
of semi-norms for
E
. Also, let
I\subset\mathbb{R}
be a bounded closed interval, and
f\colon I\to E
a bounded function. The function
f
is integrable over
I
if the following condition holds: For
p\in\Gamma
and
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
, there exists
\Delta\in D(I)
such that
a_{f}(p,\Delta)<\varepsilon
.

Proof.

For
p\in\Gamma
and
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
, take
\Delta\in D(I)
such that
a_{f}(p,\Delta)<\varepsilon
. Then, if
\xi
and
\xi^{\prime}
are representative of
\Delta
, we have

p(s_{f}(\Delta,\xi)-s_{f}(\Delta,\xi^{\prime}))\leq\sum_{i=1}^{l(\Delta)}\Delta_{i}p(f(\xi_{i})-f(\xi^{\prime}_{i}))\leq a_{f}(p,\Delta)<\varepsilon.

Thus, the condition (iv) in
Proposition

A.3.6
holds.
∎

Proposition A.3.8
.

Let
E
be a quasi-complete Hausdorff locally convex space, and
I\subset\mathbb{R}
a bounded closed interval. Any continuous function
f\colon I\to E
is integrable.

Proof.

Take a fundamental system
\Gamma
of semi-norms for
E
, and denote
I=[a,b]
. Since
I\subset\mathbb{R}
is compact and
f
is continuous, the function
f
is uniformly continuous. Hence, for
p\in\Gamma
and
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
, there exists
\delta\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
such that

x,y\in I,\,|x-y|<\delta\,\Longrightarrow\,p(f(x)-f(y))<\frac{\varepsilon}{2(b-a)}.

Take a partition
\Delta\in D(I)
satisfying
|\Delta|<\delta
, then

a_{f}(p,\Delta)\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{2(b-a)}\sum_{i=1}^{l(\Delta)}\Delta_{i}=\frac{\varepsilon}{2}<\varepsilon.

Thus, the proposition follows from
Lemma

A.3.7
.
∎

Lemma A.3.9
.

Let
I\subset\mathbb{R}
be a bonded closed interval. For a continuous function
f\colon I\to E
and a continuous semi-norm
p\colon E\to\mathbb{R}
, we have

p\bigl(\int_{I}f(x)dx\bigr)\leq\int_{I}p(f(x))dx

Proof.

For a tagged partition
(\Delta,\xi)\in\widetilde{D}(I)
, notice that

p(s_{f}(\Delta,\xi))\leq s_{pf}(\Delta,\xi).

Since
p\colon E\to\mathbb{R}
is continuous, we have

p\bigl(\int_{I}f(x)dx\bigr)=\lim_{(\Delta,\xi)\in\widetilde{D}(I)}p(s_{f}(\Delta,\xi))\leq\lim_{(\Delta,\xi)\in\widetilde{D}(I)}s_{pf}(\Delta,\xi)=\int_{I}p(f(x))dx.\qed

Let
I\subset\mathbb{R}
be an interval, and
f\colon I\to E
a continuous function. For
a,b\in I
, denote

\int_{a}^{b}f(x)dx\coloneqq\begin{cases}\displaystyle\int_{[a,b]}f(x)dx&(a\leq b)\\
-\displaystyle\int_{[b,a]}f(x)dx&(a>b).\end{cases}

Then, for a continuous semi-norm
p\colon E\to\mathbb{R}
, we have

p\bigl(\int_{a}^{b}f(x)dx\bigr)\leq\left|\int_{a}^{b}p(f(x))dx\right|.

Remark A.3.10
.

Let
I\subset\mathbb{R}
be an interval, and
f\colon I\to E
a function. As in
Definition

A.1.3
, we can define (real) differentiability of
f
, and all results in
§​

A.1
hold with minor modifications.

Proposition A.3.11
.

I\subset\mathbb{R}
be an interval and
x_{0}\in I
. For a continuous function
f\colon I\to E
, the function

I\to E,\quad x\mapsto\int_{x_{0}}^{x}f(t)dt

is differentiable, and we have

\frac{d}{dx}\int_{x_{0}}^{x}f(t)dt=f(x)\quad(x\in I).

Proof.

Let us denote
\widetilde{f}\colon I\to E
,
x\mapsto\int_{x_{0}}^{x}f(t)dt
. We prove that
\widetilde{f}
is differentiable at any point
a\in I
. Take a fundamental system
\Gamma
of semi-norms for
E
. For
p\in\Gamma
and
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
, since
f
is continuous at
a
, there exists
\delta\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
such that

t\in I,\,|t-a|<\delta\,\Longrightarrow\,p(f(t)-f(a))<\varepsilon.

Thus, if
x\in I\mathbin{\setminus}\{a\}
satisfies
|x-a|<\delta
, then

p\Bigl(\frac{\widetilde{f}(x)-\widetilde{f}(a)}{x-a}-f(a)\Bigr)\leq\frac{1}{|x-a|}\left|\int_{a}^{x}p(f(t)-f(a))dt\right|<\varepsilon,

which shows
\widetilde{f}^{\prime}(a)=f(a)
.
∎

Lemma A.3.12
.

Let
I
be an interval, and
f\colon I\to E
a differentiable function. The followings are equivalent:

1

The function
f
is constant.

2

For all
x\in I
, we have
f^{\prime}(x)=0
.

Proof.

(i)
\Longrightarrow
(ii) is clear. We prove the converse.
Take a fundamental system
\Gamma
of semi-norms for
E
, and fix
x_{0}\in I
. To prove
f(x)=f(x_{0})
for
x\in I
, it is enough to show that
p(f(x)-f(x_{0}))=0
for all
p\in\Gamma
. Define a function
\widetilde{f}
by

\widetilde{f}\colon I\to\mathbb{R},\quad x\mapsto p(f(x)-f(x_{0})),

then
\widetilde{f}
is differentiable at any
a\in I
, and we have
\widetilde{f}^{\prime}(a)=0
. In fact, for
x\in I
, since

\left|\frac{\widetilde{f}(x)-\widetilde{f}(a)}{x-a}\right|\leq p\Bigl(\frac{f(x)-f(a)}{x-a}\Bigr),

the claim follows from the continuity of
p
and
f^{\prime}(a)=0
. Thus, the function
\widetilde{f}\colon I\to\mathbb{R}
is constant, and hence

p(f(x)-f(x_{0}))=\widetilde{f}(x)=\widetilde{f}(x_{0})=0.\qed

Let
I\subset\mathbb{R}
be an interval, and
f\colon I\to E
a function. A differentiable function
F\colon I\to E
is called a primitive function of
f
if it satisfies
F^{\prime}(x)=f(x)
for all
x\in I
.

Proposition A.3.13
.

Let
I=[a,b]
be an interval, and
f\colon I\to E
a continuous function. If
F\colon I\to E
is a primitive function of
f
, then we have

\int_{I}f=F(b)-F(a).

Proof.

Let us denote
\widetilde{f}\colon I\to E
,
x\mapsto\int_{a}^{x}f(t)dt
. Then, since

\widetilde{f}^{\prime}(x)=f(x)=F^{\prime}(x)\quad(x\in I),

the function
\widetilde{f}-F\colon I\to E
is constant. Thus, we have

\int_{I}f=\widetilde{f}(b)-\widetilde{f}(a)=F(b)-F(a).\qed

Remark A.3.14
.

Let
X
be a set. As in
Definition

A.2.1
, we can define a uniform convergence of a net
\{f_{\lambda}\}_{\lambda\in\Lambda}
consisting of maps
f_{\lambda}\colon X\to E
, and all results in
§​

A.2
hold with minor modification.

Proposition A.3.15
.

Let
I\subset\mathbb{R}
be a bounded closed interval. If a net
\{f_{\lambda}\colon I\to E\}_{\lambda\in\Lambda}
consisting of integrable functions converges uniformly to an integrable function
f\colon I\to E
, then we have

\lim_{\lambda\in\Lambda}\int_{I}f_{\lambda}=\int_{I}f.

Proof.

Take a fundamental system
\Gamma
of semi-norms for
E
. For
p\in\Gamma
and
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
, since
\{f_{\lambda}\}_{\lambda\in\Lambda}
converges uniformly to
f
, there exists
\lambda_{0}\in\Lambda
such that

\lambda\in\Lambda,\,\lambda\geq\lambda_{0}\,\Longrightarrow\,p_{I}(f_{\lambda}-f)<\frac{\varepsilon}{b-a}.

Thus, if
\lambda\in\Lambda
satisfies
\lambda\geq\lambda_{0}
, then

p\Bigl(\int_{I}f_{\lambda}(x)dx-\int_{I}f(x)dx\Bigr)\leq\int_{I}p(f_{\lambda}(x)-f(x))dx<\varepsilon,

where we used
Lemma

A.3.9
in the first inequality.
∎

A.4.
Cauchy-Goursat theorem and Cauchy’s integral formula

Fix a quasi-complete Hausdorff locally convex space
E
over
\mathbb{C}
. We use the following notations about curves in
\mathbb{C}
:

•

A function
\gamma\colon I\to\mathbb{C}
of class
C^{1}
, where
I\subset\mathbb{R}
is a bounded closed interval, is called a parametrized smooth curve if
\gamma^{\prime}(t)\neq 0
for all
t\in I
.

•

A continuous function
\gamma\colon I\to\mathbb{C}
, where
I\subset\mathbb{R}
is a bounded closed interval, is called a parametrized piecewise smooth curve if there exists a partition
\Delta\in D(I)
such that
\gamma\colon I_{i}(\Delta)\to\mathbb{C}
is a parametrized smooth curve for all
i\in[l(\Delta)]
.

•

For parametrized piecewise smooth curves
\gamma_{1}\colon[a_{1},b_{1}]\to\mathbb{C}
and
\gamma_{2}\colon[a_{2},b_{2}]\to\mathbb{C}
, a reparametrization from
\gamma_{1}
to
\gamma_{2}
is a bijective function
\varphi\colon[a_{2},b_{2}]\to[a_{1},b_{1}]
of class
C^{1}
satisfying (i)
\varphi(a_{2})=a_{1}
and
\varphi(b_{2})=b_{1}
, (ii)
\gamma_{2}=\gamma_{1}\circ\varphi
, (iii)
\varphi^{\prime}(t)>0
for
t\in[a_{2},b_{2}]
. We denote
\gamma_{1}\sim\gamma_{2}
if there exists a reparametrization from
\gamma_{1}
to
\gamma_{2}
. This relation
\sim
is an equivalence relation on the set of smooth parametrized curves.

•

A piecewise smooth curve in
\mathbb{C}
is an equivalence class
[\gamma]
of a piecewise smooth parametrized curve
\gamma\colon I\to\mathbb{C}
by the equivalence relation
\sim
. For a smooth curve
C
in
\mathbb{C}
, a piecewise smooth parametrized curve
\gamma\colon I\to\mathbb{C}
such that
C=[\gamma]
is called a parametrization of
C
. Note that for a piecewise smooth curve
C
, there exists a parametrization
\gamma\colon I\to\mathbb{C}
whose domain is
I=[0,1]
.

•

For a piecewise smooth curve
C
in
\mathbb{C}
, the image of
C
is
\gamma(I)\subset\mathbb{C}
, where
\gamma\colon I\to\mathbb{C}
is a parametrization of
C
. Note that the image of
C
is independent of the choice of parametrization. We abusively denote by
C
the image of
C
.

•

For a piecewise smooth curve
C
in
\mathbb{C}
, the initial (resp. final) point of
C
is
\gamma(a)
(resp.
\gamma(b)
), where
\gamma\colon[a,b]\to\mathbb{C}
is a parametrization of
C
. Note that both initial and final points are independent of the choice of parametrization.

•

For a piecewise smooth curve
C
, the length
l(C)\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
is defined by

l(C)\coloneqq\sum_{i=1}^{l(\Delta)}\int_{I_{i}(\Delta)}|\gamma^{\prime}(t)|dt.

Here we take a parametrization
\gamma\colon I\to\mathbb{C}
of
C
and a partition
\Delta\in D(I)
such that
\gamma\colon I_{i}(\Delta)\to\mathbb{C}
is a parametrized smooth curve for all
i\in[l(\Delta)]
, but the definition of
l(C)
is independent of the choices made.

•

For piecewise smooth curves
C_{1},\ldots,C_{n}
such that the final point of
C_{i}
is equal to the initial point of
C_{i+1}
, we define a smooth curve
C_{1}+\cdots+C_{n}
as follows: Take a parametrization
\gamma_{i}\colon[0,1]\to\mathbb{C}
of
C_{i}
for each
i\in[n]
, and define a piecewise smooth parametrized curve
\gamma\colon[0,1]\to\mathbb{C}
by

\gamma(t)\coloneqq\gamma_{i}(nt-i+1)\quad(i\in[n],\,(i-1)/n\leq t<i/n).

Then
C_{1}+\cdots+C_{n}\coloneqq[\gamma]
. Note that the definition of
C_{1}+\cdots+C_{n}
is independent of the choices of parametrization.

•

For a piecewise smooth curve
C
, we define a smooth curve
C^{-}
as follows: Take a parametrization
\gamma\colon[a,b]\to\mathbb{C}
, and define a piecewise smooth parametrized curve
\gamma^{-}
by

\gamma^{-}\colon[a,b]\to\mathbb{C},\quad t\mapsto\gamma(a+b-t).

Then
C^{-}\coloneqq[\gamma^{-}]
. Note that the definition of
C^{-}
is independent of the choice of parametrization.

•

For
z,w\in\mathbb{C}
, the line segment
L(z,w)
is a piecewise smooth curve defined by the piecewise smooth parametrized curve

\gamma_{z,w}\colon[0,1]\to\mathbb{C},\quad t\mapsto z+t(w-z).

•

A triangle
T
is a piecewise smooth curve
T=L(z_{1},z_{2})+L(z_{2},z_{3})+L(z_{3},z_{1})
where
z_{1},z_{2},z_{3}\in\mathbb{C}
are three different points.

Definition A.4.1
.

Let
U\subset\mathbb{C}
be a domain. For a piecewise smooth curve
C\subset U
and a continuous function
f\colon U\to E
, define

\int_{C}f=\int_{C}f(z)dz\coloneqq\sum_{i=1}^{l(\Delta)}\int_{I_{i}(\Delta)}\gamma^{\prime}(t)(f\circ\gamma)(t)dt.

Here we take a parametrization
\gamma\colon I\to\mathbb{C}
of
C
and a partition
\Delta\in D(I)
such that
\gamma\colon I_{i}(\Delta)\to\mathbb{C}
is a parametrized smooth curve for all
i\in[l(\Delta)]
, but the definition of
\int_{C}f
is independent of the choices made.

Lemma A.4.2
.

Let
U\subset\mathbb{C}
be a domain. For a piecewise smooth curve
C\subset U
, a continuous function
f\colon U\to E
, and a continuous semi-norm
p\colon E\to\mathbb{R}
, we have

p\bigl(\int_{C}fdz\bigr)\leq\sup_{z\in C}p(f(z))\cdot l(C).

Proof.

Take a parametrization
\gamma\colon I\to\mathbb{C}
of
C
and a partition
\Delta\in D(I)
such that
\gamma\colon I_{i}(\Delta)\to\mathbb{C}
is a parametrized smooth curve for all
i\in[l(\Delta)]
. Then

p\bigl(\int_{C}fdz\bigr)\leq\sum_{i=1}^{l(\Delta)}p\bigl(\int_{I_{i}(\Delta)}\gamma^{\prime}(t)(f\circ\gamma)(t)dt\bigr)\leq\sum_{i=1}^{l(\Delta)}\int_{I_{i}(\Delta)}|\gamma^{\prime}(t)|p((f\circ\gamma)(t))dt\leq\sup_{z\in C}p(f(z))\cdot l(C),

where we used
Lemma

A.3.9
in the second inequality.
∎

Proposition A.4.3
.

Let
U\subset\mathbb{C}
be a domain, and
C\subset U
a piecewise smooth curve. For a net
\{f_{\lambda}\}_{\lambda\in\Lambda}
consisting of continuous functions
f_{\lambda}\colon U\to E
and a continuous function
f\colon U\to E
, if
\{f_{\lambda}\}_{\lambda\in\Lambda}
converges uniformly to
f
on
C
, then we have

\lim_{\lambda\in\Lambda}\int_{C}f_{\lambda}=\int_{C}f.

Proof.

Take a parametrization
\gamma\colon I\to\mathbb{C}
of
C
and a partition
\Delta\in D(I)
such that
\gamma\colon I_{i}(\Delta)\to\mathbb{C}
is a parametrized smooth curve for all
i\in[l(\Delta)]
. Then for
\lambda\in\Lambda
,

\int_{I}f_{\lambda}=\sum_{i=1}^{l(\Delta)}\int_{I_{i}(\Delta)}\gamma^{\prime}(t)(f_{\lambda}\circ\gamma)(t)dt.

Since the net
\{\gamma^{\prime}(f_{\lambda}\circ\gamma)\}_{\lambda\in\Lambda}
converges uniformly to
\gamma^{\prime}(f\circ\gamma)
on
I_{i}(\Delta)
, we have

\lim_{\lambda\in\Lambda}\int_{I}f_{\lambda}=\sum_{i=1}^{l(\Delta)}\lim_{\lambda\in\Lambda}\int_{I_{i}(\Delta)}\gamma^{\prime}(t)(f_{\lambda}\circ\gamma)(t)dt=\sum_{i=1}^{l(\Delta)}\int_{I_{i}(\Delta)}\gamma^{\prime}(t)(f\circ\gamma)(t)dt=\int_{I}f,

where we used
Proposition

A.3.15
in the second equality.
∎

Proposition A.4.4
.

Let
U\subset\mathbb{C}
be a domain,
C\subset U
a piecewise smooth curve, and
f\colon U\to E
a continuous function. If
F\colon U\to E
is a primitive function of
f
, then we have

\int_{C}f=F(z_{1})-F(z_{0}),

where
z_{0}
,
z_{1}
are the initial and final points of
C
respectively.

Proof.

Take a parametrization
\gamma\colon I\to\mathbb{C}
of
C
and a partition
\Delta\in D(I)
such that
\gamma\colon I_{i}(\Delta)\to\mathbb{C}
is a parametrized smooth curve for all
i\in[l(\Delta)]
. Denote
I_{i}(\Delta)=[a_{i-1},a_{i}]
for each
i\in[l(\Delta)]
, then

\int_{C}f=\sum_{i=1}^{l(\Delta)}\int_{a_{i-1}}^{a_{i}}\gamma^{\prime}(t)(f\circ\gamma)(t)dt.

Since
F\circ\gamma\colon I_{i}(\Delta)\to E
is a primitive function of
I_{i}(\Delta)\to E
,
t\mapsto\gamma^{\prime}(t)(f\circ\gamma)(t)
, by
Proposition

A.3.13
, we have

\int_{C}f=\sum_{i=1}^{l(\Delta)}\bigl((F\circ\gamma)(a_{i})-(F\circ\gamma)(a_{i-1})\bigr)=F(z_{1})-f(z_{0}).\qed

Theorem A.4.5
.

Let
U\subset\mathbb{C}
be a domain, and
T\subset U
be a triangle whose interior is in
U
. For a holomorphic function
f\colon U\to E
,we have

\int_{T}f=0.

Proof.

Take a fundamental system
\Gamma
of semi-norms for
E
. Since
E
is Hausdorff, it is enough to show that
p(\int_{T}f)=0
for all
p\in\Gamma
. As in the scalar-valued case, one can construct a sequence
\{T_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of triangles such that:

•

T_{0}=T
.

•

For each
n\in\mathbb{N}
, the diameter
d_{n}
and length
l_{n}
of
T_{n}
satisfy
d_{n}=d_{0}/2^{n}
and
l_{n}=l_{0}/2^{n}
.

•

For each
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we have
p(\int_{T}f)\leq 4^{n}p(\int_{T_{n}}f)
.

The condition
d_{n}\leq d_{0}/2^{n}
yields that there is a point
a\in\mathbb{C}
which is in the interior or on the boundary of
T_{n}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
. For any
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
, since
f
is holomorphic at
a\in U
, there exists
\delta\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
such that
D_{\delta}(a)\subset U
and

0<|z-a|<\delta\,\Longrightarrow\,p\Bigl(\frac{f(z)-f(a)}{z-a}-f^{\prime}(a)\Bigr)<\frac{\varepsilon}{d_{0}l_{0}}.

Also, since
d_{n}\leq d_{0}/2^{n}
and
a
is in the interior or on the boundary of
T_{n}
, there exists
n\in\mathbb{N}
such that
T_{n}\subset D_{\delta}(a)
. Fix such
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
Now, notice that the function
U\to E
,
z\mapsto f(a)+(z-a)f^{\prime}(a)
has a primitive function, then by
Proposition

A.4.4
,

p\bigl(\int_{T_{n}}f\bigr)=p\bigl(\int_{T_{n}}\bigl(f(z)-f(a)-f^{\prime}(a)(z-a)\bigr)dz\bigr)<\sup_{z\in T_{n}}|z-a|\cdot\frac{\varepsilon l_{n}}{d_{0}l_{0}}\leq\sup_{z\in T_{n}}|z-a|\cdot\frac{\varepsilon}{2^{n}d_{0}}.

Since
|z-a|\leq d_{n}\leq d_{0}/2^{n}
for all
z\in T_{n}
, we have

p\bigl(\int_{T}f\bigr)\leq 4^{n}p\bigl(\int_{T_{n}}f\bigr)<\varepsilon,

which shows the claim.
∎

Corollary A.4.6
.

Let
U\subset\mathbb{C}
be a domain,
T\subset U
a triangle whose interior is in
U
, and
f\colon U\to E
a continuous function. If
f
is holomorphic on
U
except a point
z_{0}\in U
, then we have

\int_{T}f=0.

Proof.

If
z_{0}
is in the exterior of
T
, then the corollary follows immediately from
Theorem

A.4.5
, so we may assume that
z_{0}
is in the interior or on the boundary of
T
.

First, consider the case that
z_{0}
is a vertex of
T
. Take a fundamental system
\Gamma
of semi-norms for
E
. It is enough to show that
p(\int_{T}f)=0
for all
p\in\Gamma
. Since
f
is continuous and
T\subset\mathbb{C}
is compact, there exists
t\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
such that
p(f(z))\leq t
(
z\in T
). For
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
, by taking two points on the edges of
T
, one can make a sub-triangle
T^{\prime}\subset T
such that:

•

The point
z_{0}
is a vertex of
T^{\prime}
.

•

The length of
T^{\prime}
satisfies
l(T^{\prime})<\varepsilon/t
.

•

\int_{T}f=\int_{T^{\prime}}f
.

Thus, we have

p(\int_{T}f)=p(\int_{T^{\prime}}f)\leq\sup_{z\in T}f(z)\cdot l(T^{\prime})<\varepsilon,

which shows the claim.

If
z_{0}
is not a vertex of
T
, divide
T
into two or three triangles by connecting
z_{0}
and vertices of
T
, then
\int_{T}f=0
follows from the previous case.
∎

Let
U\subset\mathbb{C}
be a domain and
a\in U
. We say that
U
is star-shaped with respect to
a
if
\{a+t(z-a)\mid t\in[0,1]\}\subset U
for all
z\in U
.

Theorem A.4.7
.

Let
U\subset\mathbb{C}
be a star-shaped domain with respect to
a_{0}\in U
, and
f\colon U\to E
a continuous function. If
f
is holomorphic on
U
except a point
z_{0}\in U
, then:

(1)

There exists a primitive function
F\colon U\to E
of
f
.

(2)

For any piecewise smooth closed curve
C\subset U
, we have

\int_{C}f=0.

Proof.

(1) Note that
L(a_{0},z)\subset U
for all
z\in U
since
U
is star-shaped with respect to
a_{0}
. Define a function
F
by

F\colon U\to E,\quad z\mapsto\int_{L(a_{0},z)}f(\zeta)d\zeta.

We prove that
F
is a primitive function of
f
. Let
a\in U
, and take
R\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
such that
D_{R}(a)\subset U
. For
z\in D_{R}(a)
, we have a triangle
T(z)\coloneqq L(a_{0},a)+L(a,z)+L(z,a_{0})\subset U
whose interior in
U
. Hence, for
z\in D_{R}(a)
, by
Corollary

A.4.6
,

\int_{T(z)}f(\zeta)d\zeta=0,

or equivalently,

F(z)-F(a)=\int_{L(a,z)}f(\zeta)d\zeta.

Now, take a fundamental system
\Gamma
of semi-norms for
E
. For
p\in\Gamma
and
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
, since
f
is continuous at
a
, there exists
\delta\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
such that
\delta<R
and

\zeta\in U,\,|\zeta-a|<\delta\,\Longrightarrow\,p(f(\zeta)-f(a))<\varepsilon/2.

Thus, if
z\in U\mathbin{\setminus}\{a\}
satisfies
|z-a|<\delta
, then

p\Bigl(\frac{F(z)-F(a)}{z-a}-f(a)\Bigr)=\frac{1}{|z-a|}p\Bigl(\int_{L(a,z)}\bigl(f(\zeta)-f(a)\bigr)d\zeta\Bigr)\leq\frac{L(a,z)}{|z-a|}\sup_{z\in L(a,z)}(f(\zeta)-f(a))\leq\varepsilon/2<\varepsilon,

which shows
F^{\prime}(a)=f(a)
.

(2) This follows from (1) and
Proposition

A.4.4
.
∎

Theorem A.4.8
.

Let
U\subset\mathbb{C}
be a domain, and
f\colon U\to E
a holomorphic function. For an open disk
D
such that
\overline{D}\subset U
, we have

f(a)=\frac{1}{2\pi\sqrt{-1}}\int_{\partial D}\frac{f(z)}{z-a}dz\quad(a\in D),

where
\partial D
denotes the positively oriented boundary of
D
.

Proof.

Let
a\in D
. Define a function
\widetilde{f}
by

\widetilde{f}\colon U\to E,\quad z\mapsto\begin{cases}\displaystyle\frac{f(z)-f(a)}{z-a}&(z\neq a)\\
f^{\prime}(a)&(z=a),\end{cases}

then
\widetilde{f}
is continuous on
U
and holomorphic on
U\mathbin{\setminus}\{a\}
. Take an open disk
D^{\prime}
such that
\overline{D}\subset D^{\prime}\subset U
. Then since
D^{\prime}
is star-shaped with respect to the center, we have by
Theorem

A.4.7
(3),

\int_{\partial D}\widetilde{f}=0,

and hence

\int_{\partial D}\frac{f(z)}{z-a}dz=\int_{\partial D}\frac{f(a)}{z-a}dz=2\pi\sqrt{-1}f(a).\qed

A.5.
Laurent series expansion

Fix a quasi-complete Hausdorff locally convex space
E
over
\mathbb{C}
.

Lemma A.5.1
.

Let
a\in\mathbb{C}
and
R\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
. For a sequence
\{a_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subset E
, if the series
\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(z-a)^{n}a_{n}
converges in
E
for all
z\in D_{R}(a)
, then the series
\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}f_{n}
of functions
f_{n}\colon D_{R}(a)\to E
,
z\mapsto(z-a)^{n}a_{n}
converges uniformly on compact sets.

Proof.

Let
K\subset D_{R}(a)
be a compact set, and take a fundamental system
\Gamma
of semi-norms for
E
. Set
r\coloneqq\max\{|z-a|\mid z\in K\}
, then
r<R
. Fix
z_{0}\in D_{R}(a)
satisfying
r<|z_{0}-a|<R
. Since
\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(z_{0}-a)^{n}a_{n}
converges, we have
\lim_{n\to\infty}(z_{0}-a)^{n}a_{n}=0
. Hence, for
p\in\Gamma
, there exists
M_{p}\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
such that
p((z_{0}-a)^{n}a_{n})<M_{p}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
. For
p\in\Gamma
, we have

p((z-a)^{n}a_{n})=\frac{|z-a|^{n}}{|z_{0}-a|^{n}}p(|z_{0}-a|^{n}a_{n})\leq\frac{r^{n}}{|z_{0}-a|^{n}}M_{p}\quad(z\in K).

Thus, by
Proposition

A.2.3
, the series
\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}f_{n}
converges uniformly on
K
.
∎

Theorem A.5.2
.

Let
U\subset\mathbb{C}
be a domain, and
f\colon U\to E
a holomorphic function. For
a\in U
and
R\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
such that
D_{R}(a)\subset U
, there exists a unique expansion

f(z)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(z-a)^{n}a_{n}\quad(z\in D_{R}(a)).

Moreover, we have

a_{n}=\frac{1}{2\pi\sqrt{-1}}\int_{\partial D_{r}(a)}\frac{f(\zeta)}{(\zeta-a)^{n+1}}d\zeta\quad(r\in\mathbb{R}_{>0},r<R).

for all
n\in\mathbb{N}

Proof.

The uniqueness of expansion is clear. In fact, if we have an expansion
f(z)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(z-a)^{n}a_{n}
, then for
r\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
satisfying
r<R
, by
Lemma

A.5.1
and
Proposition

A.4.3
,

\int_{\partial D_{r}(a)}\frac{f(\zeta)}{(\zeta-a)^{n+1}}d\zeta=\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\int_{\partial D_{r}(a)}(\zeta-a)^{m-n-1}a_{m}d\zeta=\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}2\pi\sqrt{-1}\delta_{m,n}a_{m}=2\pi\sqrt{-1}a_{n}.

We prove the existence of expansion. Fix
r\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
such that
r<R
. For each
z\in D_{R}(a)
, take
r_{0}\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
satisfying
|z-a|<r_{0}<R
, then we have by
Theorem

A.4.8
,

(A.5.1)

f(z)=\frac{1}{2\pi\sqrt{-1}}\int_{\partial D_{r_{0}}(a)}\frac{f(\zeta)}{\zeta-a}d\zeta=\frac{1}{2\pi\sqrt{-1}}\int_{\partial D_{r_{0}}(a)}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\Bigl(\frac{z-a}{\zeta-a}\Bigr)^{n}\frac{f(\zeta)}{\zeta-a}d\zeta.

Note that for
p\in\Gamma
, where
\Gamma
is a fundamental system of semi-norms for
E
, we have

p\Bigl(\Bigl(\frac{z-a}{\zeta-a}\Bigr)^{n}\frac{f(\zeta)}{\zeta-a}\Bigr)\leq\frac{|z-a|^{n}}{r_{0}^{n+1}}\cdot\sup\{p(f(\zeta))\mid\zeta\in\partial D_{r_{0}}(a)\}<\infty.

Thus, by
Proposition

A.2.3
, the series on the right hand side of (
A.5.1
) converges uniformly on
\partial D_{r_{0}}(a)
, and hence by
Proposition

A.4.3
,

f(z)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(z-a)^{n}\frac{1}{2\pi\sqrt{-1}}\int_{\partial D_{r_{0}}(a)}\frac{f(\zeta)}{(\zeta-a)^{n+1}}d\zeta=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(z-a)^{n}\frac{1}{2\pi\sqrt{-1}}\int_{\partial D_{r}(a)}\frac{f(\zeta)}{(\zeta-a)^{n+1}}d\zeta,

where we used
Theorem

A.4.7
in the second equality.
∎

Lemma A.5.3
.

Let
a\in\mathbb{C}
and
R\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
. For a sequence
\{a_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
, if the series
\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(z-a)^{-n-1}a_{n}
converges in
E
for all
z\in D_{R}^{\times}(a)
, the series
\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}f_{n}
of functions
f_{n}\colon D_{R}^{\times}(a)\to E
,
z\mapsto(z-a)^{-n-1}a_{n}
converges uniformly on compact sets.

Proof.

Let
K\subset D_{R}^{\times}(a)
be a compact set, and take a fundamental system
\Gamma
of semi-norms for
E
. Set
r\coloneqq\min\{|z-a|\mid z\in K\}
, then
0<r<R
. Fix
z_{0}\in D_{R}^{\times}(a)
satisfying
0<|z_{0}-a|<r
. Since the series
\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(z_{0}-a)^{-n-1}a_{n}
converges, we have
\lim_{n\to\infty}(z_{0}-a)^{-n-1}a_{n}=0
. Hence, for
p\in\Gamma
, there exists
M_{p}\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
such that
p((z_{0}-a)^{-n-1}a_{n})<M_{p}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
. For
p\in\Gamma
, we have

p((z-a)^{-n-1}a_{n})=\frac{|z_{0}-a|^{n+1}}{|z-a|^{n+1}}p((z_{0}-a)^{-n-1}a_{n})\leq\frac{|z_{0}-a|^{n+1}}{r^{n+1}}M_{p}\quad(z\in K).

Thus, by
Proposition

A.2.3
, the series
\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}f_{n}
converges uniformly on
K
.
∎

Theorem A.5.4
.

Let
a\in\mathbb{C}
and
R\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
. For a holomorphic function
f\colon D_{R}^{\times}(a)\to E
, there exists a unique expansion, called the Laurent series expansion

f(z)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(z-a)^{-n-1}a_{-n-1}+\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(z-a)^{n}a_{n}\quad(z\in D_{R}^{\times}(a)).

Moreover, we have

a_{n}=\frac{1}{2\pi\sqrt{-1}}\int_{\partial D_{r}(a)}\frac{f(\zeta)}{(\zeta-a)^{n+1}}\quad(r\in\mathbb{R}_{>0},r<R).

for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Proof.

As in the proof of
Theorem

A.5.2
, one can prove the uniqueness of expansion using
Lemma

A.5.1
and
Lemma

A.5.3
.

We prove the existence of expansion. Fix
r\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
such that
r<R
. For
z\in D_{R}^{\times}(a)
, take
r_{1},r_{2}\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
satisfying
r_{1}<|z-a|<r_{2}<R
, then by
Theorem

A.4.7
and
Theorem

A.4.8
,

(A.5.2)

\displaystyle f(z)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2\pi\sqrt{-1}}\int_{\partial D_{r_{1}}(a)}\frac{f(\zeta)}{\zeta-z}d\zeta+\frac{1}{2\pi\sqrt{-1}}\int_{\partial D_{r_{2}}(a)}\frac{f(\zeta)}{\zeta-z}d\zeta

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi\sqrt{-1}}\int_{\partial D_{r_{1}}(a)}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\Bigl(\frac{z-a}{\zeta-a}\Bigr)^{n}\frac{f(\zeta)}{\zeta-a}d\zeta+\frac{1}{2\pi\sqrt{-1}}\int_{\partial D_{r_{2}}(a)}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\Bigl(\frac{\zeta-a}{z-a}\Bigr)^{n}\frac{f(\zeta)}{z-a}d\zeta.

As in the proof of
Theorem

A.5.2
, one can show that the series on the right hand side of (
A.5.2
) converge uniformly on
\partial D_{r_{1}}(a)
and
\partial D_{r_{2}}(a)
respectively. Thus, by
Proposition

A.4.3
,

\displaystyle f(z)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(z-a)^{-n-1}\frac{1}{2\pi\sqrt{-1}}\int_{\partial D_{r_{1}}(a)}(\zeta-a)^{n}f(\zeta)d\zeta+\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(z-a)^{n}\frac{1}{2\pi\sqrt{-1}}\int_{\partial D_{r_{2}}(a)}\frac{f(\zeta)}{(\zeta-a)^{n+1}}d\zeta

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(z-a)^{-n-1}\frac{1}{2\pi\sqrt{-1}}\int_{\partial D_{r}(a)}(\zeta-a)^{n}f(\zeta)d\zeta+\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(z-a)^{n}\frac{1}{2\pi\sqrt{-1}}\int_{\partial D_{r}(a)}\frac{f(\zeta)}{(\zeta-a)^{n+1}}d\zeta,

where we used
Theorem

A.4.7
in the second equality.
∎

Definition A.5.5
.

Let
a\in\mathbb{C}
and
R\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
. For a holomorphic function
f\colon D_{R}^{\times}(a)\to E
, denote by

f(z)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(z-a)^{-n-1}a_{-n-1}+\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(z-a)^{n}a_{n}\quad(z\in D_{R}^{\times}(a)).

the Laurent series expansion.

(1)

The point
a
is called a removable singularity of
f
if
a_{n}=0
for all negative integers
n<0
.

(2)

The point
a
is called a pole of
f
if there exists a negative integer
n<0
such that
a_{n}\neq 0
and
a_{m}=0
for all
m<n
. In this case, the element
\Res_{z=a}f(z)\coloneqq a_{-1}\in E
is called the residue of
f
at
a
.

(3)

The point
a
is called a essential singularity of
f
if it is neither removable or a pole.

Theorem A.5.6
.

Let
a\in\mathbb{C}
and
R\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
. For a holomorphic function
f
, the followings are equivalent:

1

The point
a
is a removable singularity of
f
.

2

The net
\{f(z)\}_{z\in D_{R}(a)\mathbin{\setminus}\{a\}}
converges in
E
(see
§​

A.1
for the directed order on
D_{R}(a)\mathbin{\setminus}\{a\}
).

3

For
p\in\Gamma
, where
\Gamma
is a fundamental system of semi-norms for
E
, there exists a positive real number
0<r<R
such that
\{p(f(z))\mid z\in D_{r}^{\times}(a)\}\subset\mathbb{R}
is bounded.

Proof.

Denote by
f(z)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(z-a)^{-n-1}a_{-n-1}+\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(z-a)^{n}a_{n}
(
z\in D_{R}^{\times}(a))
the Laurent series expansion.

(i)
\Longrightarrow
(ii): Since
a
is removable, we have

f(z)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(z-a)^{n}a_{n}\quad(z\in D_{R}^{\times}(a)).

By
Proposition

A.2.5
, the function of
z
on the right hand side is continuous. Thus,

\lim_{z\in D_{R}(a)\mathbin{\setminus}\{a\}}f(z)=a_{0}\in E.

(ii)
\Longrightarrow
(iii): Set
\xi\coloneqq\lim_{z\in D_{R}(a)\mathbin{\setminus}\{a\}}f(z)\in E
. For
p\in\Gamma
, there exists
r\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
such that

z\in D_{R}(a)\mathbin{\setminus}\{a\},\,|z-a|<r\,\Longrightarrow\,p(f(z)-\xi)<1.

Thus, we have
p(f(z))<1+p(f(\xi))
for all
z\in D_{r}^{\times}(a)
.

(iii)
\Longrightarrow
(i): Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
. It is enough to show that
p(a_{-n-1})=0
for all
p\in\Gamma
. By the condition (iii), there exists a positive real number
0<r_{0}<R
and
M\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
such that
p(f(z))<M
for
z\in D_{r_{0}}^{\times}(a)
. For
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
, take
r\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
such that
r<r_{0}
and
Mr^{n+1}<\varepsilon
, then

p(a_{-n-1})=p\Bigl(\frac{1}{2\pi\sqrt{-1}}\int_{\partial D_{r}(a)}(\zeta-a)^{n}f(\zeta)d\zeta\Bigr)\leq\frac{Mr^{n}}{2\pi}l(\partial D_{r}(a))=Mr^{n+1}<\varepsilon,

which shows
p(a_{-n-1})=0
.
∎
